# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum XVII

## Funding proposal package for FP275

### Summary

This addendum contains the following six parts:
a) A funding proposal summary titled "Scaling up the Deployment of Integrated Utility Services (IUS) to Support Energy Sector Transformation in the Caribbean (Phase II: Scaling up IUS in Dominica, Grenada and St. Vincent & the Grenadines)";
b) No-objection letter(s) issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);
c) Environmental and Social report(s) disclosure;
d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;
e) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;
f) Gender documentation of the funding proposal.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

## Climate: The Borrowing Member Countries (collectively Barbados, Belize and Jamaica) or the "BMCs") are facing intensified storms, increased temperatures, floods, droughts, sea-level rise, and rising temperatures. These changes are affecting not only the availability and supply of energy resources but also the transmission infrastructure that is required to deliver electricity to consumers.

The BMCs have experienced a number of extreme weather events in recent years which indicate a trend of rising temperatures, escalating the risk of heat stress and severe weather events, which are expected to increase in frequency and intensity. The increased demand for cooling will be exacerbated by the need for cooling, with sustained average daily temperatures of 30°C typically resulting in a 10% spike in weekly sales of electricity.

The BMCs have also experienced significant power outages due to extreme weather events, which has led to an increase in the share of renewables in their energy mix and improving energy efficiency across all sectors.

## Energy: The BMCs heavily rely on imported fossil fuels for electricity, which constitutes a significant portion of their total import bill. This reliance is expected to continue as the BMCs transition away from coal-fired power plants with other sectors such as road transport, industry, agriculture, and land-use change also being notable contributors. The introduction of an IJS model in the BMCs will provide a mechanism for reducing this dependence by implementing measures to reduce this dependency, which is increasingly important as the demand for electricity is expected to rise in the coming years.

The IJS Model will be implemented through the Caribbean Renewable Energy Resources (CERR) is also a focus on enhancing its resilience and diversification of the electricity grid.

## Programme Approach

The introduction of an IJS model in BMCs is seen as a strategic step toward addressing various challenges in their energy sector. The programme aims to reduce the reliance on imported fossil fuels for electricity, mitigate climate change impacts, and decreasing dependence on imported fossil fuels. Additionally, the model supports access to clean energy, reform utility business models, and enhance capabilities for financing DERs.

The IJS Model will be a mechanism to directly implement demand-side RE and EE projects through utilities in an open market system.

The IJS Programme will include a "national project" in each country to be executed by the national electricity and power sector. The national project will be implemented as a series of sub-projects at different levels, which may include a series of sub-projects at the level of the utility's customers, notably residential customer sites (i.e., households) or commercial sites (e.g., small businesses). In addition to the national project, RE and EE technologies such as PV and/or other DER technologies will be installed at the premises of the interested utility customers. Utility customers will be provided with a loan from the IJS Programme to finance the installation of these technologies. The technologies through regulated qualified service providers that work closely with the utility. The utility will provide the support necessary for the implementation of the project, including the provision of technical assistance and repay the amount owed to the utility for the installation through their monthly bill. A major barrier for the uptake of these technologies has historically been the high upfront cost to install them so this Programme is expected to decrease that pressure.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 4

## Structure of the IUS Programme

The IUS Programme is structured in a way that it not only addresses all the key risks and barriers assessed during the programme preparation phase but also supports the much-needed ecosystem development for a sustainable, scalable, and replicable model behind the meter energy solutions in the BMCs. The below figure outlines the key components of the IUS Programme.

### Figure 1: Structure of the IUS Programme

| Component | Output |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Establish and promote IUS as a core element of EE and other DERs | 1. Strengthening regulatory frameworks, building the capacity of EE and other DERs to deploy EE and other DER technologies |
| 2. Scaling up EEAs by empowering the EE sector | 2. Installation of EEAs (including EE and other DER) in households and public sector entities |

#### Source: GCF/CPD

The Programme, through Component 1, establishes and promotes IUS as a core element of each Executing Entity's business model. It underpins timely and effective delivery of country-level support and enables the BMCs to guide and support their EEAs in scaling up EEAs by empowering the EE sector.

Programme Anticipated Benefits:

- **Reduction in GHG Emissions:** The Programme is projected to make a significant reduction in the carbon footprint of the participating countries by enabling the avoidance of an estimated 601,550 tons of CO2 equivalent (CO2e) emissions. This translates into approximately $34 million in avoided costs (US$34 per metric tonne of CO2e avoided); and for the GCF's contribution, the cost is approximately US$43 per ICODs avoided.
- **Increased Resilience Against Heat Stress:** The Programme is projected to provide energy efficient air conditioning units to 100,000 households in BMCs. This translates into approximately $25 million in avoided costs (US$25 per metric tonne of CO2e avoided); and for the GCF's contribution, the cost is approximately US$34 per ICODs avoided.
- **Decreased Burden on Utilities and Increased Renewable Energy Generation:** Energy efficiency interventions are expected to reduce energy consumption across various sectors, including residential, commercial, industrial, and transport. This will lead to a reduction in peak demand for electricity, resulting in lower utility bills for consumers and decreased overall demand on energy infrastructure. A shift towards renewables is anticipated with a targeted increase in the contribution of clean energy to the national grids. This includes:

#### GCF (2023) Keeping cost in a hot world by using more energy, making everyone more important than ever!

* Estimated CO2e avoided: 601,550 tons (US$34 per metric tonne)
* Estimated ICODs avoided: 78,900 (excluding parallel investments by the utilities, divided by the total GHG emissions avoided)
* Estimated GCF cost for CO2e avoided refers to the total GCF cost (US$34) divided by the total GHG emissions avoided

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

The implementation of technologies like solar photovoltaic systems, solar water heating, and possibly wind and geothermal energy, depending on the country-specific strategies and natural resource availability.

- **Economic Development:** The transition to a greener energy sector is intended to stimulate local economies through job creation in the renewables sector, foster innovation, and provide opportunities for local businesses. Energy efficiency measures will also be promoted as part of this strategy.
  
- **Capacity Building and Knowledge Sharing:** A core outcome of the Programme is the enhancement of institutional capacities and regulatory frameworks in each participating country. This involves training programs, development of national strategies, and capacity building at all levels.

- **Scaling Up Investments:** By making investments and establishing favourable conditions for private and international financing, the Programme aims to catalyse additional resources for the energy sector transformation. This will enable a scaling up of clean energy projects and attract further investment into the region.
  
- **Gender and Social Inclusion:** The Programme places an emphasis on ensuring that the benefits of the energy transition are equitably distributed, with specific measures to include women and vulnerable communities in the opportunities created by this transition. By addressing gender-specific needs through a Gender Action Plan (GAP), the Programme will address the following gender-specific needs: governance and participation (through targeted actions focused on women’s representation at all levels of decision-making); access and affordability (through targeted actions focused on the marginalised beneficiaries).

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

#### B.1.1 Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica heavily rely on imported fossil fuels for electricity generation, which is a major source of their GHG emissions.

The energy sector in these three countries is characterized by a continued reliance on fossil fuels (mainly coal) in Phase I and a commitment to reduce the share of fossil fuels in Phase II (by 2030). Despite recent efforts to diversify the energy mix, fossil fuels remain an important element of each country's electricity generation portfolio.

As a result, electricity generation is responsible for approximately one-third of total annual GHG emissions in Barbados. Belize and Jamaica are also significant contributors to total annual GHG emissions in these BMCs including road transport, industry, agriculture, and land-use change & forestry.

#### B.1.2 The Governments of Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica have each outlined clear strategies for addressing climate change and reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. These strategies are aligned with the Paris Agreement and aim to achieve net-zero emissions by 2050 or earlier. Belize acts as a net carbon sink and the emissions from Barbados and Jamaica are comparatively lower than those of other countries in the region.

The following section provides a summary of the mitigation measures these BMCs have derived from the Baseline Assessment. For detailed information, please refer to Annex 2b.


| Figure: Total emissions vis-à-vis energy sector emission (excl. industrial processes, etc.) in the three BMCs |
| --- | --- |
| **Emissions (Mt CO₂e)** | **Barbados (2021)** | **Belize (2021)** | **Jamaica (2021)** |
| Energy & Domestic transport | 3.5 | 4.6 | 7.8 |
| Industrial processes | 0.9 | 1.2 | 2.1 |
| Agriculture | 0.5 | 0.7 | 1.1 |
| Waste | 0.2 | 0.3 | 0.5 |

B.1.4. Baseline reduction targets: Barbados has established climate targets as well as international commitments and policies to reduce its GHG emissions across all sectors, including the energy sector.

The table below summarizes these targets.


| Document | Institution (date) | Key points |
| --- | --- | --- |
| NDC | Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (2021) | By 2035 - 20% GHG emissions + 35% emission: Total reduction of 705 GtCO2e emissions; 75% emission. Total reduction of 1,498 MtCO2e |
| | | Wind (100 MW); Solar PV (200 MW); Solar thermal energy by 2030: Solar centralised (205 MW); Solar distributed (105 MW); Wind onshore (150 MW); Wind offshore (150 MW); Energy efficiency (15%); and Centralised and distributed energy storage (200 MW) |
| | | The Sustainable Energy Framework for Barbados (SEFB) | Government of Barbados (2018) |
| | | The framework seeks to achieve a reduction of Barbados’s greenhouse gas emissions by 75% below the country’s projected baseline emission levels, through a combination of its country’s energy matrix and its reduction to oil price volatility through diversification. This is achieved through a multi-pronged measure to mitigate GHGs and support institutional capacity building |
| B.1.5. Assessed national communication (ANC) against the 2008 base year | Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (2021) | Barbados submitted its second National Communication (NC) under the UNFCCC in August 2021. The Second National Communication from Barbados submitted to the UNFCCC in 2018 reported total emissions of 1.4 Mt CO2e for 2015, which is a 37% increase over its 2008 base year emission levels (1.0 Mt CO2e). The baseline (BAU) emission projection, it did not disaggregate by sector. The economy-wide BAU emission projections for 2030 are estimated at 4.5 Mt CO2e. This is a projected increase of 39% over the base year emissions and an average annual increase of 16% from 2018 to 2030. Barbados has reported that its economy-wide emission reductions by 2030 is 165 kt CO2e*, which is an average annual decrease in emissions of 4%. The projected economy-wide emission reductions for 2030 are based on the assumption that the emissions reduction is uniform across all sectors. This means that the total emissions reduction of 165 kt CO2e was evenly distributed and then applied to the 2010 emissions for the energy sector (1.4 Mt CO2e). Based on this, the projected emissions from Barbados’s energy sector in 2030 is estimated at 1.3 Mt CO2e. |

### B.1.7 Emission reduction targets
Belize has established climate targets as well as international commitments and policies to reduce its GHG emissions across all sectors, including the energy sector.

#### Table 2: Belize's relevant international and climate commitments, policies, and climate targets

| Document | Institution (date) | Key points |
|---|---|---|

B.1.8. Associated current and projected emissions from the sector without the program

The emissions attributable to Belize’s electricity sector in 2020 were estimated at 170 kt CO₂e excluding emissions associated with imported electricity whose value for the same year was estimated at 106 kt CO₂e. Hence, the total emissions of the electricity sector in 2020 is projected to be 276 kt CO₂e. The Low Emission Development Strategy of Belize assumes an annual BAU energy consumption growth rate of 3% and a 50% increase in the SKU emissions for the electricity sector (domestic power imports) in 2030 is projected to be 371 kt CO₂e.

B.1.11. Associated current and projected emissions from the sector without the program

The emissions from the energy sector of Jamaica in 2012 was reported to be 7.4 M CO2e out of which 2.8 M CO2e is attributed to fossil fuel combustion through the use of non-CCS, BACs and other sources. Emissions in the energy sector are projected to reach 8.2 M CO2e by 2030.

B.1.12. The US Programme will support the BMCs to achieve this vision through the provision of concessional financing for renewable energy projects. The attributed emissions from the energy sectors* for each of the three countries are shown in Figure 2.

B.1.13. To deliver on their emission reduction targets, it is of paramount importance that BMCs scale up their renewable energy capacity. This scaling-up ambition is reinforced by the fact that electricity demand in these countries is expected to increase over the coming decades as their economies grow. Combined with the increasing use of fossil fuels for other purposes (e.g., transport), this will lead to more intensive sectors (e.g., tourism, agriculture) on fossil fuels. Opportunities for doing so are numerous and include the development of renewable energy projects, energy efficiency measures, and sustainable development goals that may simultaneously increase overall demand for electricity.

Adaptation

B.1.14. The Caribbean region, including Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica, is already facing, and will continue to face, more intense storms, floods, droughts, rising sea levels, and higher temperatures. These changes are expected to be most pronounced in the late 2030s and early 2040s, seasons consistent with global warming, through to the end of the century [1]. All BMCs broke temperature records during the summer of 2020. Barbados recorded its highest temperature ever on June 25th, while Jamaica recorded the highest temperature observed in any month for the second consecutive year. These extreme temperatures are expected to increase over time and will require new approaches to energy generation solutions, such as energy-efficient air conditioning, can help alleviate these issues. Additionally, paying PV generation projects a premium price for electricity during peak demand periods (e.g., 10:00 AM to 4:00 PM) will support beneficiaries in accessing these adaptation measures, increasing resilience against heat stress.

Table 4: Historical and projected climate change impacts for the BMCs

Relevant changes in climatic conditions

Barbados

Historical:
Since the 1980s, Barbados has experienced a notable increase in air temperature by approximately 2.5°C [2]. This warming is largely attributed to human-induced greenhouse gas emissions that impact the island's climate by altering weather patterns and intensity. Additionally, located within the hurricane belt, Barbados is prone to natural hazards like hurricanes, floods, droughts, and landslides, especially during the hurricane season from June through November, as evidenced by severe damage from hurricanes such as Eta in 2021 and Tomas in 2010.

Projected: Modelling conducted under the CBMIP framework, featured in the World Bank Climate Knowledge Platform (CKP), projects that Barbados will experience a further increase in air temperature with high emissions scenario (SSP5-7.0). By the end of the century, temperatures are expected to reach 28.3°C by mid-century and 29.4°C by the end of the century [3]. The projected range for maximum temperatures is projected to increase by 3% to 13% by the 2100s across scenarios, relative to the base baseline. This data also projects a greater number of days with maximum temperature exceeding 35°C from April to October. Minimum temperatures are expected to see a greater increase than maximum temperatures.

### Historical
According to the World Bank CKCP, Belize has experienced a significant warming trend, with mean annual temperatures rising by 0.1°C since 1960, at an average rate of 0.1°C per decade.

Projected: Modeling conducted under the CMIP framework, featured in the World Bank CKCP, projects a significant rise in the mean annual temperatures of Belize under a high emission scenario (SSP2-7.0). Temperature increases are projected to be between 38.5°C and 40.5°C by the end of the century, from a baseline of 25.4°C (24.96°C–25.73°C) in 1995 (base period: 1955-1994). The number of days with temperatures above 35°C is projected to increase by the 2100s from 1955 baseline of 122 days (368 days–148 days). Days when temperatures above 35°C are projected to increase in April to September. Nights with temperatures above 26°C are projected to increase by 307-435% across scenarios from 1995 baseline of 7 days (3 days–13 days) by the end of the century.

Significant increases in heat stress risk are expected, with a significant escalation in heat stress risks, even under the more optimistic SSP2-4.5 scenario (see Figure 10).

Heat stress is likely to constitute a major challenge for Belize and other Caribbean countries as the climate changes over the coming decades. These impacts are expected to be particularly pronounced in the summer months, including the wet season in Belize that is currently highly humid throughout the year (often between 85-95%). They are typically the driest month of the year.

These are the same months during which temperatures and temperature extremes (hot days, hot nights) are projected to increase. The country has a high potential for uptake/off-take to cooling solutions. In 2022, penetration of cooling technologies in Belize was 67%.

### Jamaica

As outlined in the World Bank CCMP maximum, average, and minimum temperatures are all expected to increase. Since 1950, average temperatures have increased at an average of 0.11°C per decade, maximum surface temperatures have increased by 0.86°C and minimum temperatures have increased by 1.04°C.

Projected warming under the context of CMIP6, as featured in the World Bank CCMP, projects that the mean annual temperatures in Jamaica will increase moderately from 25.58°C (25.35°C - 25.81°C) to 27.9°C (27.4°C - 28.4°C) by mid-century and further to 29.06°C (28.5°C - 29.6°C) at the end of the century. However, this scenario is highly unlikely given the current global GHG emissions trajectories.

Consequently, Jamaica is projected to experience a mean annual temperature increase from 24.73°C (24.01°C-25.45°C) in 2020 and is projected to reach 28.77°C (28.01°C - 29.46°C) by the end of the century.

The country has a high potential for uptake/off-take to cooling solutions, with a significant chance of experiencing at least one period of prolonged exposure to extreme heat, leading to heat stress, within the next five years.

The effects of heat stress have become increasingly evident in Jamaica. Schools, for instance, have reported that extreme heat has negatively impacted students' learning and well-being due to inadequate ventilation and lack of air conditioning units. Some schools have had to install or plan to install air-conditioning units by 2030. Additionally, the Jamaica Public Service Company has noted a significant increase in calls related to heat stress, with some schools reporting up to five times more than usual cases such as heat exhaustion, heat rash, heat cramps, and severe heat stroke, which can be fatal.

The programme is expected to increase the adaptation potential of all three countries by building resilience for customers and the grid. The region is expected to experience more frequent and severe storms, including flooding, storm surges, and other severe weather events that have the high potential to damage infrastructure and services. Solar rooftops with energy storage will be far more resilient to power disruptions than traditional rooftop solar systems. This programme will also reduce pressure on the grid during increased demand for active cooling solutions like air conditioning due to high temperatures through location-based incentives.

## B.1.5. The GCF Programme aims to facilitate the adoption of renewable energy and energy-efficient technologies, supporting countries to address the above-described needs/challenges by: (i) reducing greenhouse gas emissions and accelerating the transition away from reliance on fossil fuels; and (ii) increasing access to electricity.

Additionally, the integration of Distributed Energy Resources (DERs) into the grid will enhance its resilience by diversifying sources of electricity generation and shifting the demand. This diversification means that the grid will be powered by electricity generated from various energy resources and generation facilities.

### B.1.16. Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica, as Small Island Developing States (SIDS) in the Caribbean, face common challenges inherent to their geographical and socio-economic contexts. These include vulnerability to climate change impacts such as increasing temperatures, sea-level rise and extreme weather events. Despite varying geographic characteristics, all three countries share a commitment to sustainable development, environmental conservation, and climate resilience. They also share developmental priorities that are underpinned by a collective commitment to overcoming these obstacles through regional cooperation and policy integration.

### B.1.17. A summary of relevant baseline conditions and characteristics in the three participating BMCs is included below. More information is available in the Baseline Assessment Report – see Annex 2b.

| Table: Content/Baseline conditions in the three participating BMCs |
| --- |

#### Content of targeted BMCs
- **Barbados**: Located in the eastern Caribbean, it is a 34 km long and 23 km wide island nation situated approximately 100 km east of the northern coast of Venezuela. Barbados has a tropical climate with warm temperatures year-round and clear waters. With a diverse population of approximately 290,000, most of African descent, it is known for its rich cultural heritage and vibrant tourism industry, which accounts for over 75% of GDP and employs more than half the working population.

**Page 21**

Electricity sector: The Barbados Light & Power Company (BLPC) holds a monopoly on electricity generation and distribution in Barbados. In March 2019, BLPC was granted permission to allow distributed generation systems utilizing renewable energy sources for commercial and industrial customers. As of January 2023, distributed generation systems accounted for approximately 5% of the total electricity generated by BLPC. By end of 2020, BLPC served 131,522 customers, with a peak demand of 141 MW and an installed capacity of about 168 MW. The government has set out several strategies to increase energy security and expand renewable generation including the addition of solar PV farms and energy storage devices to improve grid stability. The Renewable Energy Rider (REN) and Feed-in Tariff (FIT) programs have been instrumental in increasing solar PV use and other renewable energy sources, as well as measures and the diversification of the energy mix are ongoing to reduce emissions and enhance sustainability.

**Barbados**

Belize, located on the Caribbean coast of Central America, is distinguished by its diverse ecosystems, including tropical rainforests, coral reefs, and barrier islands. The country has a population of approximately 421,754. It boasts a multicultural society with a mix of Indigenous peoples, descendants of African slaves, and immigrants from Europe and Asia. Belize ranks as the second poorest country in the World Bank’s list despite facing challenges such as poverty and unemployment. Its economy is heavily reliant on tourism, which accounts for approximately 20% of GDP. The climate in Belize is tropical with high humidity and significant variability in rainfall and temperature, which along with its location, makes it susceptible to climate change and natural disasters like hurricanes and droughts. Belize has a rich biodiversity that includes several protected areas, encompassing marine reserves, national parks, and nature reserves. Despite its beauty and biodiversity, the country faces challenges such as deforestation, illegal logging, and poaching.

Electricity sector: Belize Electricity Limited (BEL), regulated by the Public Utilities Commission, is the sole provider of electricity in Belize. As of 2019, BEL served approximately 357,846 customers with a peak demand of 9 MW. While Belize has made progress regarding use of alternative energy sources such as biomass and solar power, the country still relies heavily on imported diesel fuel for its electricity generation needs. Recent slight increases in the use of petroleum fuels combined with the continuous use of biomass and solar power have led to an increase in electricity costs. BEL has made several amendments to include DG, allowing customers to sell excess energy back to the grid. Electricity consumption patterns show considerable seasonal variation due to the country’s reliance on tourism as a major economic driver. Belize is also an exporter of electricity despite the lack of a formal interconnection policy. Projections indicate that Belize will continue to rely heavily on imported diesel fuel for its electricity generation needs, with projections indicating that renewable energy use and energy efficiency measures are likely to increase in the near future.

Electricity sector: Electricity prices in Belize are relatively low, with tiered pricing systems currently in place.


Jamaica

Jamaica, located in the West Indies, is the third largest island in the Caribbean, positioned south of Cuba. The country has a population of approximately 2.9 million and its diverse culture includes African, European, East Indian, and Chinese ancestors. The island’s population is predominantly Christian with a significant Muslim minority. Jamaica is one of the most economically developed economies of the Caribbean, as of the third quarter of 2022, showcased a 5.6 percent year-on-year growth in GDP.

Electricity sector: The Jamaican electricity sector includes the Jamaica Public Services Company (JPS), which provides electricity to approximately 95% of the population, and JPS, owned by Marleyland and West East Power, alongside the independent power producers (IPPs) that provide electricity to the remaining 5%. The national target set at 30% by 2030 is expected to be achieved through increased use of renewable energy sources. III. MCS readiness for programme deployment

B.1.18 All three countries have expressed a strong interest in implementing IUS to enhance their electricity sector performance, energy efficiency, and climate change mitigation efforts through proactive efforts towards institutionalizing IUS and launching IUS frameworks. By adopting IUS, these nations aim to reduce the environmental impact of their electricity sectors by reducing emissions and resource footprints. In addition, the MCS are making positive institutional and regulatory improvements, motivated to support a strong and effective IUS.

B.1.20 Barbados has assessed the readiness level of IUS readiness in Barbados in terms of the legal, regulatory and institutional frameworks outlined below.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

## Barbados Public Sector Smart Energy Programme (PSEEP)
The project provides a package of economic incentives and technical assistance towards SMEs to improve energy efficiency and renewable energy adoption. This includes capacity building, a pilot project for the installation of solar panels on roofs to EVIs, and capacity building.

## Disaster Risk and Energy Access Management (DREAM) Project
The program aimed at reducing greenhouse gas emissions through disaster risk reduction by improving the national energy policy framework, awareness, and capacity building. The project also seeks to enhance system resilience against natural disasters such as solar PV installation on roofs, tendering process and installation.

## Belize Energy for Sustainable Development in the Caribbean (ESD)

## B.2 (a) Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)
### Climate problem
B.2.1 The Caribbean region is currently grappling with two significant challenges: climate change, characterized rising temperatures, and an energy sector heavily dependent on imported fossil fuels, which are subject to price volatility and supply disruptions. Many countries in the region are also experiencing severe climate change impacts, including intensified storms, floods, droughts, sea-level rise, and increased frequency of extreme weather events.

### B.2.2 Historical and projected climate change impacts show a trend of rising temperatures, increasing the risk of heat stress (heat waves) and leading to higher demand for cooling and electricity. For example, the International Energy Agency has noted that heat increase could double the demand for cooling services with sustained average daily temperatures of 30°C typically leading to a 15% spike in weekly sales. Additionally, the shift towards renewable energy sources is expected to be accelerated by increasing the share of renewables in their energy mix and improving energy efficiency across all sectors.

### Goal
B.2.b The IUS Programme aims to address the climate problem through a paradigm shift impact in the BMAC energy sector. This paradigm shift can be achieved if the project establishes and operationalizes an innovative integrated approach that includes: (i) policy reforms, (ii) regulatory reforms, (iii) market-based reforms, and (iv) regulatory reforms. THEN this paradigm shift will occur, leading to an accelerated transition to decentralized clean energy, increased energy security, and climate resilience across Barbados, Belize and Jamaica. BECAUSE a combination of these reforms has been established that will lower GHG emissions, improve energy security and enhance climate resilience.

### B.2.a The proposed IUS Programme aims to reduce GHG emissions from the energy sector and enhance resilience to heat stress (cooling systems coupled with PV generation) in the three participating countries. This will be achieved by implementing a combination of renewable energy DER technologies/measures. The Programme will accomplish this by addressing key barriers currently inhibiting businesses, homeowners, and other stakeholders from making such investments, including limited


---
**Page 28**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 22

B

awareness, knowledge, and capacity, limited access to financing, limited risk appetite; limited availability of qualified ESCOs, and policy and regulatory frameworks that do not sufficiently incentivize investment in DERs.

**B.2a.5:** To overcome these barriers to investment, the Programme will support the electric utilities in Barbados, Belize, Dominica, Grenada, St. Kitts & Nevis, St. Lucia, St. Vincent & the Grenadines (the “Participating Utilities”) to accomplish this, the Programme will also address key barriers currently preventing utilities from implementing this model. The Participating Utilities are required to submit a Business Case for each project that they propose for the launch phase of the IUS scheme; the need for complementary technical assistance (TA) to successfully begin implementation of the IUS Model is recognized and addressed in the Business Cases. The Programme will directly assist the three national electric utilities to support behind-the-meter investment in distributed RE, EE and other DERs among a critical mass of customers (mostly households and businesses, as well as some public sector entities), including through capacity building for the utilities and other stakeholders to continue scaling up investment using the IUS Model after Programme closure.

**Paragraph 2a.6:** The Programme aims to be a long-term sustainable, scalable, and replicable solution to address the BMCs, and, by extension the region, climate challenges. The Programme is envisaged to achieve the shift impact, as outlined in the Programme’s Goal Statement, by:

(a) ensuring a robust market system to accelerate deployment of renewable energy technologies as well as energy efficiency solutions;

(b) strengthening stakeholders’ institutional capacity to advance clean energy in the participating countries and have additional impacts on other sectors;

(c) strengthening the technical capacity of Technology Service Providers (TSPs) to cater to the needs of the market;

(d) providing equal opportunity for women and other social groups (such as low-income households, women led households, etc.) to adopt clean energy solutions; and

(e) providing simplified access to more affordable finance; etc.

**B.2a.7:** The Theory of Change figure below elaborates the broader goals of the IUS programme.

## Figure 12: IUS Programme Theory of Change

B

If the project establishes and operationalizes an innovative Integrated Utility Service (IUS) model by facilitating access to concessional financing, capacity building, and regulatory reforms, then a paradigm shift in the energy sector will be achieved. This will lead to significant improvements in energy security, reliability, affordability, and environmental sustainability.

### IMPACT
- **PRIVATE SECTOR SHIFT**
  - Reduced transaction costs for private sector investors
  - Enhanced institutional arrangements and governance of public utilities

### OUTCOMES & CO-BENEFITS
- Output 1.3: Strengthened capacity and management of public utilities through IUS model
- Output 2.3: Enhanced access to financing for public utilities through IUS model
- Output 3.3: Increased level of service delivery by public utilities through IUS model

### PROGRAM ACTIVITIES
- Activity 1.3.1: Capacity building and technical assistance for public utilities
- Activity 2.3.1: Facilitation of concessional financing access for public utilities
- Activity 3.3.1: Strengthening institutional arrangements and governance of public utilities

### ASSUMPTIONS
- (a) Continued engagement with the private sector, coordination with other stakeholders to ensure financial sustainability.
- (b) Strong political will and commitment from governments to implement reforms and improve service delivery.


---
**Page 30**
---

B.2.a To achieve the over-arching goal the Programme aims to deliver the following two main Outcomes and a corresponding intermediate Outcome:

Outcome 1: Reduced GHG emissions through widespread adoption of RE, EE and other DER technologies.

The Programme will support countries in their transition from local energy resources to meet its energy needs. Since the local energy resources are primarily clean sources of energy, in the long-term the Programme is expected to contribute to a reduction in GHG emissions.

Outcome 2: Enhanced climate resilience and adaptation of beneficiaries to heat stress.

The Programme will support energy efficiency solutions such as efficient ACs. The programme therefore provides direct long term heat stress mitigation benefits to the end-users.

Outcome 3 (intermediate): Increased access to financing for clean energy investments, allowing utility customers to adopt RE, EE and other DER technologies at scale.


B.2.b To achieve the two main Outcomes and one intermediate Outcome described above, the Programme will aim to deliver four Outputs:

Output 1.1: Strengthened regulatory and institutional frameworks facilitating the deployment of distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies.

The Programme will support the partner countries and the key stakeholders in their efforts to develop and implement a comprehensive regulatory framework that will strengthen the regulatory policy environment in the country for smooth implementation and roll-out of the IUS model and associated activities. The programme will also provide technical assistance to countries to:

1. Immediately address regulatory barriers impacting the deployment of the IUS Model under Component 2, and Activity 1.1.2: Address broader policy framework to strengthen prospects and incentives for investment in RE, EE and other DER technologies.

Output 1.2: Enhanced capacity of customers to invest in distributed RE, EE and other DER through the IUS Model.

The Programme will support countries in their efforts to build a long-term societal shift towards activities and investments that contribute to counter climate change goals and resilience. The Programme will implement three Activities to support Output 1.2 – Activity 1.2.1: Raise awareness of the benefits of RE, EE and other DER technologies through targeted campaigns; Activity 1.2.2: Just IUS Model and associated services; and Activity 1.2.3: Provide customers with hands-on experience in deploying distributed RE, EE and DER projects – which are described in more detail in Section B.3 (below).

Output 1.3: Increased technical capacity of utilities, TSPs and other stakeholders to deploy and maintain distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies through the IUS Model.

The Programme aims to achieve this through collaborative learning and knowledge sharing among the various stakeholders involved in the deployment and maintenance of the IUS model. The programme will support countries by:

1. Tailored implementation manuals, developing additional capacity for business development and cross-cutting areas such as Environmental and Social (EAS) management, Monitoring and Reporting (MAM), Gender Equality and Inclusion (GEI), etc.
2. Activity 1.3.1: Facilitate knowledge exchange among the targeted BMGCs; Activity 1.3.2: Enhance technical capacity of utilities, TSPs and other stakeholders to deploy and maintain distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies in the context of the IUS Model; Activity 1.3.3: Training for qualified (technology) service providers, notably TSPs, to support customers; Activity 1.3.4: Support information exchange with other (non-partner) countries including government agencies, utilities, TSPs and other stakeholders involved in the deployment of distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies – which are described in more detail in Section B.3 (below).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 26

## Legal and regulatory framework: BMs need clearer legal or regulatory guidance on issues like DG tariff/fit-in and whether utilities can offer on-bill financing. There is also a lack of a standardized approach to this.

### Limited customer awareness and capacity: Customers often lack awareness of the benefits of the proposed technologies, affecting implementation smoothness.

### Limited availability of TSPs providing end-to-end solutions: While there are TSPs with the necessary technical expertise, they do not provide end-to-end solutions.

### Limited access to low-cost finance: Difficulty in accessing financing due to being considered high-risk, cumbersome processes for loan applications, and a lack of equitable financial products are significant barriers.

### Risk adverse stakeholders: Both small electricity customers and financial institutions are hesitant to invest in behind-the-meter solutions, partly due to unclear risk assessments and high interest rates.

### Limited technical standards: There is a need for globally accepted technical standards to ensure the standardization of technologies across different regions.

#### B.2a-13. In addition to the typical behind the meter barriers as detailed above, there are other areas specific to the BMSCs that may impact IUS Programme roll-out:

- **Capacity constraints and model understanding: Regulatory authorities face capacity issues and require additional training on new regulatory models, requiring additional dedicated training.
- **Limited locally-available financing options: High local financing costs and limited access to low-cost finance make it challenging for utilities to support the financial aspects of distributed RE, EE, and other IUS activities.
- **Limited distributed RE and EE ecosystems: A supportive ecosystem development is critical, requiring technical assistance for regulatory frameworks, TSP certification, customer engagement, and overall ecosystem building.

#### B.2a-14. The activities of the IUS Programme, described in detail in Section 3.3, have been designed to address the above barriers by targeting the root causes that have hitherto hindered the adoption of RE and EE technologies in the targeted BMSCs. The following table illustrates how the IGS Programme activities will help overcome the identified barriers and support the roll-out of the IUS Programme.

| Legal and regulatory framework | * Under-clear legal or regulatory framework<br>* Risk-averse stakeholders |
|  | * Lack of clarity on DG tariff/fit-in<br>* Uncertainty about whether utilities can offer on-bill financing |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 27

## Access to Finance

- **Limited access to finance (low-cost loans)**: The U.S. model eliminates the need for households and businesses to secure a loan from (often risk averse) financial institutions to invest in energy efficiency measures, thereby reducing transaction costs for the customer.
- **Risk averse stakeholders**: The U.S. model shifts investment risks from customers as they are not required to take out loans or make upfront payments. Instead, it reduces the financial burden and exposure to potential losses for utilities.

## Limited customer demand and TSPs capacity

- **Limited customer demand and TSPs capacity**: Under output 1.2, the Programme will engage with a large pool of customers to increase their awareness of available SEES, as well as how they can be used to reduce energy costs and improve comfort in their homes.
- **Risk averse stakeholders**: The programme will also strengthen the role of help desks to assist customers prepare pre-project application forms. This will be achieved through in-person customer awareness sessions. The customers may also benefit from assistance with the completion of the pre-project application preparation and submission.

## Technical and institutional capacity

- **Limited technical standards as a barrier for some stakeholders**: Develop inclusive outreach strategies – direct to customer, technology provider, and other key stakeholders.
- **Risk averse stakeholders**: Organize customer awareness sessions. Implement an online help desk to address U.S. related queries.
- **Limited technical standards as a barrier for some stakeholders**: Provide basic energy-saving measures for customers and promote capacity building support to the TSPs.
- **Risk averse stakeholders**: Furthermore, the programme will remove the barrier posed by the limited availability of qualified ESCOs by capitalizing on the electricity utilities to take on additional roles in providing technical assistance. This will be achieved through development of DORs – a role that they are already well suited to fulfil due to their existing customer base and ability to provide energy efficiency services.
- **Risk averse stakeholders**: The programme will also engage with local governments, municipalities, and other institutions with novelties and business models in the participating BMCs.

## B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

### Programme objectives
B.1 The IUS Model is a mechanism to directly implement demand-side renewable energy and energy efficiency technologies utilising an open market system. It is a model to support renewable energy and energy efficiency technology deployment through a strong enabling environment created by supporting policy development, institutional strengthening and the IUS at a county-wide scale for supporting energy access and energy efficiency interventions.

### Project/programme description
B.3.1 The Programme funding proposal constitutes the first two phases of the overall IUS Programme. Phase 0 is currently being implemented in Jamaica to pilot the IUS Model (without the remainder of this funding proposal). "IUS Programme" is used to refer specifically to Phase 0.

B.3.2 The Programme aims to address the need for democratizing energy policy, increasing customers' access to affordable and reliable electricity services, improving energy efficiency, reducing GHG emissions, and enhancing utility capabilities for commercial financing of DERs. The IUS Programme is not only expected to provide a replicable model for other countries in the Caribbean region but also facilitate knowledge sharing, crucial for a unified approach to energy challenges in the Caribbean.

#### Product Diversification and Proprietary Ownership in Utilities
- The U.S. model will enable smaller units within the BCGs to diversify their product offerings, which can help them become more competitive in a rapidly changing market.
- This diversification will also allow utilities to explore new revenue streams and technologies that can enhance their overall energy supply chain.

#### Enhanced Utility Financing Framework for DER Investments
- The U.S. model's financing mechanism can provide a solution to the challenges faced by smaller units within BCGs, enabling them to invest in renewable energy sources.
- This framework will also help utilities to access new revenue streams and technologies that can enhance their overall energy supply chain.

#### Ecosystem and Market Development Research in National
- The U.S. model's energy balance has been less favorable than the European model due to the lack of a clear regulatory framework for renewable energy.
- The U.S. model could be used as a case study to demonstrate how a national-level approach can help utilities to access new revenue streams and technologies that can enhance their overall energy supply chain.

#### Regional Learning and Knowledge Sharing Potential
- The U.S. model's energy balance has been less favorable than the European model due to the lack of a clear regulatory framework for renewable energy.
- The U.S. model could be used as a case study to demonstrate how a national-level approach can help utilities to access new revenue streams and technologies that can enhance their overall energy supply chain.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 10

#### Programme approach

**B.3.3 The IUS Programme will include a "national project" in each of the three countries to be executed by the national electric utility and overseen by the Ministry and regulator responsible for oversight of the electricity sector. The national project will provide customers with access to financing for energy efficiency, energy storage, and other DER technologies. Utility customers will first receive education and awareness on the benefits of these technologies and how they can be implemented in their homes. They will then sign up with a registered qualified service provider that works closely with their electric utility. The utility will provide the upfront financing for the installation of the technology, which is repaid by the customer over time through a monthly charge to customers/consumers; who will then repay the amount owed to the utility for the installation through their monthly electricity bill. This approach allows customers to access these technologies without automatically bear the high upfront cost to install them, so this programme is expected to alleviate that pressure.**

**B.3.4 The IUS Programme is an innovative solution that fosters collaboration. A behind-the-meter transactional model will be used for financing and implementing energy efficiency, storage, and other DERs by utilities, system operators, independent power producers, technology service providers, customer and business-to-business aggregators, and others. This model allows the utility to provide upfront financing while the following graphic provides an overview of the operational elements of the IUS Programme, including the key actors and components:**

**Figure 15: IUS Programme Schematic**

| **Executing Entity** | **Beneficiaries** |
| --- | --- |
| **Green Climate Fund (GCF)** | **Local Government** |
| **Caribbean Development Bank (CDB)** | **Country Regulator** |

Source: CPCS

As indicated in the above graphic, the Caribbean IUS transaction, like other behind-the-meter transactions, involves several actors. These actors are public entities like the government ministries, electricity regulators, and private entities like service providers, customers, etc. The CDB-led IUS programme however is unique given that it provides upfront financing for the installation of DERs, which in turn requires the involvement of multiple critical actors, as listed below, to the transaction unlike a typical behind-the-meter transaction.

### Programme description

B1.5 The IUS Programme is designed in a way that it not only addresses all the key barriers that are otherwise inhibiting investment in the transition to a more sustainable and distributed energy system (as outlined in Section B2 and further elaborated in Annex 2: Feasibility Study), but also supports the mechanisms for scaling up investments in distributed RE, EE and other DERs, including micro-grid and meter energy solutions in the BMCs. Figure 20 below outlines the key components of the IUS programme.

## Figure 17: Components of the IUS Programme

### Component 1: Establish and promote IUS as a core element of electric utilities' business models

The Programme, through Component 1 aims to establish and promote IUS as a core element of electric utilities’ business models in the BMCs by underpinning timely and effective delivery of county-level support provided via this Component will build directly on the considerable progress that has already been identified and demonstrated (as outlined in Section B2 and further elaborated in Annex 2: Feasibility Study). As a result, Component 1 will address the pending regulatory and institutional barriers that need to be overcome to begin financing IUS sub-projects at scale. Component 2 will then build on this foundation to scale up investments in distributed RE, EE and other DERs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 32

## B

### Introduction

This document proposes the use of (and financing of sub-projects) through the IUS model by electric utilities in the three participating BMCs.

#### Output 1.1: Strengthened regulatory and institutional frameworks facilitating the deployment of distributed renewable energy

The Programme will support regulatory authorities in the participating countries to enhance and strengthen their regulatory frameworks through legal and regulatory amendments, update or develop new regulations, and/or establish new institutions as needed for the implementation of the IUS model.

#### Output 1.2: Development of a sub-project approval process

The CDB Technical Assistance (TA) Execution Team will work with the relevant authorities in each BMC to develop a sub-project approval process that is consistent with national legal frameworks, and which can be used by customers irrespective of their customer group, demographic, etc.

#### Output 1.3: Development of an IUS implementation framework

The CDB TA Execution Team will also support the development of an IUS implementation framework for each BMC to ensure that the IUS model is consistent with national legal frameworks and regulatory requirements.

---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

#### Activity 1.2.3 Address broader policy framework needs to strengthen prospects and incentives for investment in distributed RE, EE and other DERs.
The activity will assist national stakeholders (including utilities, energy ministries, regulatory authorities, etc.) in developing a comprehensive policy and incentive support framework to support investment in priority technologies, including those to be supported under this proposal. The activity will also provide technical assistance to the TSP consultants/specialists to directly assist the national counterparts with these reform processes. This support is critical for the successful implementation of the TSPs. The TSPs will engage external consultants/specialists to develop TSP qualification guidelines and criteria, as well as to conduct outreach activities in collaboration with the utilities, energy ministries and regulatory authorities. This is needed to ensure that a minimum technical standard or benchmark could be established in the BMCs to help reduce/eliminate sub-par sub-project installations.

**Executing Entity:** CDB (via the TCA Execution Team)

**Source of Funds:** GCF grant financing

#### Output 1.2: Enhanced capacity of customers to invest in distributed RE, EE and other DERs through the IUS Model.
The key barrier inhibiting investment in sustainable energy and energy efficiency solutions is the lack of customer awareness and knowledge about these technologies and their benefits. The TCA will address these with these technologies. Under this sub-component, each participating Utility, National Designated Authority (NDA), and other relevant stakeholders will be provided with training on how to effectively market and promote these technologies through various means, including workshops, seminars, and knowledge sharing activities. Through the CDB TA Execution Team, these stakeholders will receive direct technical assistance in developing a comprehensive marketing and outreach strategy for their outreach efforts. Customer outreach will be delivered through various mechanisms, for instance: awareness raising campaigns, targeted marketing materials (including infographics, posters, pamphlets, and other multimedia materials) that will showcase the benefits of the priority technologies (i.e., lowering electricity costs, reducing greenhouse gas emissions, etc.), and training workshops to build customer building awareness will be BMC- and stakeholder-specific. These awareness and outreach initiatives will help customers understand the benefits of these technologies and how they can implement them in their solutions; (b) identifying the modalities of the IUS program and how it is delivered: (i) the amount of power that can be generated from each technology, (ii) the cost of installation, (iii) the amount of power that will be sold to the grid, etc. The emphasis will be placed on underserved customer groups like women, etc. The support provided under this sub-component will include: (a) developing a comprehensive marketing and outreach strategy for marketing material, developing inclusive marketing, communications and outreach strategies, conducting workshops and seminars, and providing training materials to build awareness of the available TSPs; helping customers understand the benefits of the priority technologies (i.e., lowering electricity costs, reducing greenhouse gas emissions, etc.), and training workshops to build customer building awareness will be BMC- and stakeholder-specific. These awareness and outreach initiatives will help customers understand the benefits of these technologies and how they can implement them in their solutions; (b) identifying the modalities of the IUS program and how it is delivered: (i) the amount of power that can be generated from each technology, (ii) the cost of installation, (iii) the amount of power that will be sold to the grid, etc. The emphasis will be placed on underserved customer groups like women, etc.

#### Activity 1.2.1 Raise awareness of RE/EE opportunities and potential savings:
Under this activity, the CDB TA Execution Team will contract external consultants/specialists to work with national counterparts to organize six outreach sessions (1 person per country in PIF and 1 person per country in PTI). The objective is to raise awareness among customers about the opportunities and advantages/benefits of clean energy solutions. These sessions are also expected to cover information about the TSPs, including how they can be accessed and what they entail. The results from these outreach sessions will therefore contribute to Activities 1.2.1 and 1.2.2, but the costs have been doubled in this activity because of the need for two consultants/specialists to work with the national counterparts (with support from local NGOs and industry/business associations), and aim

### 9. Activity 1.2.2 Promote an inclusive and just SREP model and associated services:

Through this activity, the TA Execution Team will work with national executing entities to develop a Programme-IUS-based communications action plan. Similar to Activity 1.2.1, the service provider will be responsible for developing and implementing the communications plan, delivering their respective communications action plans. Both the plans and the associated outreach materials will be shared with the CDB and other stakeholders.

By making the Programme’s information accessible to everyone, including those who are blind, deaf or have other disabilities, it is expected that this will raise awareness, encourage participation, and drive the Programme's long-term success.

**Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team)**

**Source of funds:** GCF grant financing

### 10. Activity 1.2.3 Provide customers with hands-on support to identify and develop distributed RE, EE and other DER sub-projects:

Under this activity, each electricity utility (as a national executing entity) will engage a National Energy Consultant to provide technical assistance to the utilities in business development and customer outreach/support, as well as other programme-related activities. The National Energy Consultants are expected to contribute to both fostering a pipeline of suitable sub-project proposals in each country as well as supporting the identification and development of these projects.

The TA Execution Team will also engage a consultant to assist each utility to establish or strengthen their helpdesk services for customers, which can be used to support the development of their projects applications. Among other responsibilities, the National Energy Consultants are expected to assist the utilities in developing customer outreach strategies. The technical assistance contracted consultants will also assist the utilities to develop simple, user-friendly energy savings calculators and tools that can be used by customers to assess their energy use and improve accessibility to energy-saving information and empower program participants to make informed decisions on how best to reduce their energy consumption.

The National Energy Consultants will also provide guidance on the eligibility criteria to be used for the selection of specific beneficiaries (low-income residential customers, etc.). As a result of the technical assistance provided by the National Energy Consultant developed, the Programme will facilitate access to such energy audits. CDB estimates that a total of 300 households per country will benefit from this activity in FY15 and an additional 200 households with 20 selected annually per country from FY1+ based on the eligibility criteria.

**Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team)**

**Source of funds:** GCF grant financing

### Output 1.3 Support the development of a Programme to enable TSPs and other stakeholders to deploy and maintain distributed RE, EE and other DER technologies:

#### 11. Enhance technical and institutional capacity: Inefficient technical and institutional capacity has been identified as one of the key constraints in the transition from the current electricity supply model to a more sustainable energy system. This sub-component will support the material shift from the conventional electric utility business model to more innovative approaches which are not yet widely used, but have the potential for significant environmental benefits and cost savings resulting in a radical shift from the status quo. This sub-component aims to improve capacities and institutional arrangements of national executing entities (NEEs) as well as regional stakeholders, both in and beyond the targeted countries to make this transition, institutionalize the IUS model, and ensure that it is embedded into the existing electricity supply and distribution systems. In addition to the CDB, regional stakeholders like the CARICOM Secretariat, CARLIEC, and

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 15

#### Activity 1.3.1 Facilitate knowledge exchange among the participating BDCs:
Under this activity, the CDB will facilitate the development and dissemination of project-specific national case studies, document and disseminate good practices and lessons learned related to programme implementation, including through the use of a dedicated webpage. The CDB will also develop and disseminate knowledge exchange materials, such as presentations and briefing notes, and hold online/individual knowledge exchange sessions with BDCs, where they can share their experiences and lessons learned (using inputs from) the utilities, regulators and energy ministers, allowing them to learn from one another and identify best practices that are difficult to implement in US.

Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team)

Source of funds: GCF grant financing

#### Activity 1.3.2 Enhance Executing Entities' and other stakeholders' capacities to implement the IUS model:
In a second phase, the CDB will support the EEUs in establishing new (or adjusting existing) internal systems and procedures that will be significant and a risk to the utilities. This activity therefore focuses on implementing the IUS model in a way that is consistent with the US business environment and regulatory model. As part of this support, the CDB TA Execution Team will contract a service provider to work with each EEU to develop an implementation plan for the IUS model. The service providers will also build on the overall Programme Operations Manual (see Annex 21), and will include clear guidance on how to manage the IUS model in terms of project management, risk management, related risks, product/service offerings, reporting requirements, environmental & social risk management, etc.

The CDB will support the preparation of a US business development strategy for each national Executing Entity. The service provider will also develop a web-based digital application system to facilitate the receipt and processing of sub-project applications. The EEUs will have access to an online training platform that includes training on processing sub-project applications. They will also have access to external consultant support to assist during the implementation phase, including in areas such as project management, risk management technologies (described in more detail under Component 2 below). Finally, within this activity, CDB-contracted service providers will work with EEUs to develop and implement new internal systems to better advance EGS goals and manage EAS risks – efforts that will be complemented by targeted training on EGS and EAS management. The training will include a focus on the use of KYC, AML/CFT for relevant utility staff (one for each utility in FY1) and a refresher training for each utility in FY2. The training will also include sessions with the necessary tools to build knowledge for the successful execution of the IUS model.

Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team)

Source of funds: GCF grant financing

### Activity 1.3.3 Train qualified (technological) service providers, notably TSPs, to support conservation and energy efficiency projects:
The training will be conducted through specialized training modules, designed to support long-term market growth within the IUS programme. The training will cover a wide range of topics including project management, financial planning, risk management, demand-side management/mitigation, GESI, and M&R. Additional subjects such as contracts, customer outreach, quality assurance, etc., may be included in the training modules depending on specific needs of each EAS. The trainings will be delivered by CDB-contracted service providers, who are experts in the field of energy efficiency and conservation. The training will be conducted for each country (with a particular focus on EAS management) again in both FY1 and FY2. These trainings will call for qualified TSPs to support the implementation of projects. Better-capacited TSPs are key to the long-term success of the energy transition, as the efficiency and effectiveness of their services directly impacts the quality of the projects and whether energy solutions are cost competitive.

15. **Activity 1.3.4 Support information exchange with other (non-partner) countries, incl. governments, regulators, TSPs and utilities:** Under this activity, the CDB TA Execution Team (with support from the IUS Secretariat) will organize a series of workshops and sessions in PVS and BIMCs across the region. These materials will share insights, challenges, and opportunities encountered by other countries in implementing the IUS model and its potential customization for different jurisdictions. The workshops will be organized to provide participants with an overview of the IUS programme. To further support these efforts, under Activity 1.3.4 CDB will host four (4) virtual information sessions in PVS. These sessions will cover a range of topics including: i) an introduction to the IUS model and its potential customization for different jurisdictions; ii) an overview of the IUS programme; iii) an overview of the progress, challenges, and solutions encountered by other countries in implementing the IUS model; iv) an overview of the benefits of the IUS model. Each session will feature a Q&A segment to foster interaction and engagement. Finally, this activity will also support the CDB TA Execution Team in organizing a one-day workshop on an in-person knowledge sharing summit in PVS to share IUS with the Caribbean’s experience in implementing the IUS model and its potential customization for different costs.

16. **Activity 1.3.5 Create a Community of Practice (COP) in the BMCs, inviting governments, regulators, TSPs and utilities to participate as members of the COP:** The objective is to create a Community of Practice (COP) to enhance peer-to-peer and south-south learning among stakeholders of the IUS programme across the region. This will be achieved by facilitating regular meetings between different jurisdictions to share knowledge and experiences, thereby facilitating the advancement of the IUS Programme in the region. The COP will also serve as a platform for sharing best practices on energy and utility-led distributed energy modes akin to IUS. Furthermore, an in-person field mission will be organized to facilitate peer-to-peer learning among stakeholders from different jurisdictions and BMCs. This mission will feature site visits to key IUS projects and enable in-person, multi-stakeholder discussions regarding collaborative learning and the sharing of best practices within the IUS Programme framework.

#### Executing Entity: CDB (via the TA Execution Team)

**Source of Funds:** GCF grant financing

### Component 2: Scaling up investment in distributed RE, EE and other DERs by implementing the IUS model at scale

17. This component builds on the work done under Component 1 to finance behind-the-meter technologies and services that are critical for scaling up investments in distributed RE, EE and other DERs within the IUS Programme. Participating utilities, in their capacity as national Executing Entities, are responsible for implementing this component by providing financial support to the IUS Programme including screening and approving sub-proposals, disbursing to TSPs, collecting and processing receipts, and reporting on progress. The CDB TA Execution Team will provide technical assistance to participating utilities under Component 2. This assistance will work in parallel to invest in the deployment of smart metered/advanced-metering infrastructure (AMI) that is critical for scaling up investments in distributed RE, EE and other DERs, and thereby support the broader transition towards a more sustainable, resilient and deployable energy system.

## Preapproved technologies

### B.3.8 Based on the assessment, four technologies have been preapproved for the S2S Programme, namely:

- LED lighting
- Residential air conditioning/cooling
- Solar rooftop PV (with or without storage)
- Solar water heaters.

Based on the current market conditions and the applicable legal and regulatory framework as described in earlier chapters, the overall ecosystem is relatively conducive for these technologies in the participating SBMs.

### B.3.10 There are however associated services that may be bundled in a package service offered by the utilities to their customers. These include energy efficiency retrofits, metering, demand response, load correction and other ancillary power quality services, and building/management systems. As such, additional preapproved products could be unlocked for utilities by advancing the four preapproved technologies.

Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 | Page 45

Indicative administrative processes

B.3.19 Sub-project applications will be received by the National Executing Entity from eligible beneficiaries seeking to access programme funding. The sub-project application is then appraised by the relevant department/unit of the Executing Entity that is leading the IUS programme. The Executing Entity may seek support from the TA Execution Team in this appraisal process.

The National Executing Entity will review and assess all sub-project applications received. Each executing entity will rather focus on supporting business development and sub-project origination, as well as monitoring and reporting on the progress of approved sub-projects. In the case that a National Executing Entity receives multiple sub-project applications from one applicant, it should ensure that these applications are reviewed in parallel.

B.3.20 The National Executing Entity will only approve an application if: (a) the applicant meets the applicable Know Your Customer (KYC) criteria for each country; (b) the applicant satisfies the definition of final beneficiaries; and (c) the technology is prescriptive under the IUS Programme. In addition, the National Executing Entity will review the following aspects of a project application, including but not limited to: (a) if the application made for one or more than one eligible technologies; (b) if the project has been in existence for at least 12 months and is expected to be operational within five years from the date of application; (c) in case of grid connected renewable energy sub-project the Executing Entity needs to check if there is enough available hosting capacity in the distribution feeder connecting the applicant’s site to the national grid. The National Executing Entity will also review the financial and technical creditworthiness of the applicant, (d) socio-economic background of the applicant (for inclusivity under the programme). For more information on the eligibility criteria for sub-projects and a detailed description of the eligibility criteria, refer to Annex 21.

B.3.21 After this review, the National Executing Entity will decide to either approve, reject or undertake additional assessment of an application as appropriate. The National Executing Entity will assist the respective BMCs in sub-project application appraisal, as needed. Based on the results of the assessment at this stage, the National Executing Entity will provide the sub-projects financing commitment.

The generic guide for the guidance on the IUS Programme is presented by Figure 21 below. Detailed time-bound, bottom-up-to-top-down project planning along with the respective responsibilities at each stage, will be developed and included in the Executing Entity-specific Operations Manuals that will be developed during the inception phase of the programme implementation period.

Knowledge management

B.3.22 CDB will be responsible for knowledge management under the IUS Programme. In addition, the National Executing Entities will also share their respective project documents with the respective BMCs and the TA Execution Team on a quarterly basis. The CDB TA Execution Team will then share these documents and information with the CDB AE Team. B.4 Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

Implementation arrangements

B.4.1 The CDB is the Accredited Entity for this programme. CDB has been operating in the Caribbean Region for over 50 years and has a strong track record of providing financial and technical assistance to support the region’s development priorities. As the lead implementing agency, CDB will provide TA services throughout the region, including BLPC, BEL, and JPS. As the Accredited Entity, CDB will lead programme implementation in each country, with the exception of Belize where a different entity within CDB will assume responsibility for fulfilling these functions, as outlined in Figure 22 (below).

• **TA component:** CDB, through its dedicated TA Execution Team, will directly execute most activities and deliverables under this component. The TA team will be led by a National Energy Consultant, who will be the National Energy Consultant, which will be procured and contracted by the national Executing Entities using a competitive process. The TA team will also work closely with other CDB-supported Executing Entities as CDB and governed by subsidiary grant agreements between the Bank and these Executing Entities.

Under this Component, the Programme will facilitate capacity building and execution capabilities of the regulatory body, the Executing Entities, and other key stakeholders through the TA grant financing. It will do so by addressing capacity, knowledge and awareness gaps that are inhibiting the flow of finance to the clean energy sector in the three BMOs. The programme will also support the establishment of a distributed clean energy market and business ecosystem in the three BMOs which is self-sustaining through the development of a replicable model for financing and deploying distributed clean energy projects. This model will be used as a viable option for accelerating utility-led distributed clean energy deployment in other Caribbean countries, including Belize. The programme will also support the identification of potential investments under Component 2 and help catalyse the broader process of transforming Caribbean finance flows to support the development and implementation of climate-related sub-projects.

B.4.2 CDB will support the partner BMOs, Executing Entities, and other stakeholders to better understand the links between their business operations and climate change, as well as apply this understanding when developing strategies for financing and implementing climate-related sub-projects.
