Key programme actors

B.4.9 Below we provide an overview of the role of each of the types of institutions which will be involved in the Caribbean iUS programme within each participating BMC.

The GCF:  
GCF is accountable to the Parties of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and is guided by the principles and provisions of the


---
**Page 57**
---

The CDB

As the Accredited Entity, the CDB will provide financing, financial oversight, overall programme supervision, and ensure the executing entities abide by the policies and standards of the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The CDB will also ensure timely credit recovery, and ensure robust programme monitoring, reporting, and environmental safeguards.

Environmental Sustainability Unit (ESU): The ESU is the Focal Point for the Green Climate Fund in the CDB. It will bear overall responsibility for supervising all programme activities, including project design, implementation, and evaluation. The ESU will provide TA support to be executed by the TA Executive Team; and the execution of the Lines of Credit (LoC) will be supervised by the LoC Supervision Unit (LSU). The ESU will also ensure CDB staff (mostly from the SEU), the LSU, and all relevant programme information are kept up-to-date.

Board: The Board of Directors of the Caribbean Development Bank is the ultimate supervisory authority within the Bank and has the responsibility to oversee Management in administering the GCF. The Board will receive quarterly progress reports on the programme and request/receive updates on the programmes. Board decisions or resolutions will be executed by Management, and Management will provide regular updates to the Board as it follows through on Board decision or resolution.

Management: The Vice Presidents, Directors, Division/Unit Heads, and Departmental Directors make up the Management team. They are responsible for acting upon Board decisions or resolutions, as well as approving all financial transactions related to the programme. Any action taken on programme-related activities by the Board or the ESU requires this level of decision.

Other involved areas within CDB that will support the programme through oversight are the following:

Office of Independent Evaluation: The CDB Office of Independent Evaluation will be involved in supporting/quality-assuring the independent mid-term and final evaluations of this programme.

Procurement Policy Unit (PPU): The Procurement Policy Unit (PPU) will be involved in supporting CDB-managed procurement processes, including those managed by the TA Executive Team. The PPU will also support the processing of invoices and payments to consultants contracted by both the CDB and the TA Executive Team.

Risk Management: The Risk Management Department monitors the Bank's exposure to operative, financial and portfolio risk. For the programme, the Risk Department will receive periodic reports from the ESU and the TA Executive Team and may request corrective actions to ensure financial good standing by the utilities.

Government

Government ministries, departments and agencies are pivotal in shaping energy policy and overseeing energy market participants. Decision-making authority is often delegated to entities such as regulatory bodies, which have a mandate to ensure that underlying sectoral goals and objectives are met. For instance, in the case of GDF, RE, and RFF, the Energy Regulatory Commission (ERC) has been given responsibility for broader policy goals and objectives like the NDCs etc., regulations for private sector involvement, setting energy tariffs, defining codes and standards for energy asset operations, and determines tariff rules.

The success of a behind-the-meter RUE/EE market is grounded in the government's policies that act as a facilitator for both market development and growth. Without

### NDA Units
National Designated Authorities are government institutions that serve as the interface between the GCF and the country. They are responsible for the implementation of financial activities in the country and communicate the country's priorities for financing low-emission and sustainable development.

The respective NDAs undertake various programme requirements like (a) the programme’s alignment with the local context, (b) stakeholder engagement, (c) liaison between all stakeholders, (d) support with GCF MABs, etc.

### Regulatory Model
Never copy models such as the SGS create new lines of revenue for utilities. To integrate this revenue stream into the utility’s established regulated business, regulatory frameworks must be in place.

BMGs will ensure that (a) the regulated business model does not violate or contravene any existing laws and regulations; (b) the utility is able to comply with all relevant GCF and BMG regulations like net metering/filling, etc. are well defined; (c) there is clarity regarding energy access and affordability; (d) a clear regulatory framework for procurement of goods and business-oriented interconnection/operation process is in place; and (e) robust technical and grid standards are followed for equipment, etc.

### Technology Service Providers (TSPs)

TSPs are entities in the sector capable of providing various services like EE, solar rooftop, solar water heaters, battery storage for optimising time-of-use, and operation and maintenance initiatives geared towards reducing customer energy expenses, among others.

TSPs will play a wider role in the IIS programme. TSPs will not only provide both distribution and generation services but also EE services. TSPs will be expected to enhance customer outreach and assist customers to navigate the IIS application approval process.

TSPs will enter into an agreement with customers and utilities to utilise their services as per the technical, regulatory, and commercial specifications of the respective BMCs.

TSPs will aim to provide a range of EE services, including energy efficiency, demand response, and conservation aspects of the programme through the TA support. By engaging with (and sometimes directly providing) these services, TSPs can help customers achieve additional – and better - services, thereby transitioning toward being able to serve households and businesses that are not yet to the more comprehensive sets of energy services provided by ESCOs in other jurisdictions.

B.5.1 Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

While the Caribbean region has embarked on significant efforts to enhance financing options for the deployment of energy efficiency and renewable energy projects in the Caribbean Community across Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica. While some public and private financing exists in Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica, there is a lack of dedicated funding sources for EE and RE projects that are included in the IUS programme. Additionally, there is a shortage of commercial banks offering loans for distributed generation (DG) projects, which are often too small to be considered by large financial institutions but are critical to local households and businesses (particularly the types of lower-income groups and smaller businesses than the typical customer base of utility companies). The lack of dedicated funding sources for EE/RE projects in particular, are rarely considered for financing. One significant gap is the upfront cost barrier when it comes to RE projects, which can be prohibitive for many households and small businesses.

Although RE and EE technologies provide substantial savings over time, the initial investment required can be a major deterrent for many households and small businesses, particularly those that are private investors who may not have the capital or willingness to invest heavily without immediate returns. On the public side, credit is often constrained and must be allocated to competing priorities, leaving limited resources available for EE/RE projects. The lack of dedicated funding sources also makes it challenging to prioritize and fund energy transitions without substantial external support. Additionally, the lack of a robust regulatory framework and successful demonstrations causes further deter private investment.

Funding structure

B.5.2 To make the proposed programme (and its underlying projects and sub-project) feasible, CDB is requesting concessional financing from the GCF. These concessional resources are needed in part because they will be used to support a new initiative called the IUS scheme. The IUS scheme aims at supporting EE/RE projects by providing concessional loans to the utilities for the purchase of RE and EE technologies as part of their core business models. The process of launching an IUS scheme is complex, and it requires strong political will from both the private sector and partner governments who are not able to bear on their own. The following types of GCF financing are therefore critical to the success of this programme.

B.5.3 Like other concessional loans under their initial 30-schemes, partner utilities will need concessional loans to make RE/EE sub-projects appealing to customers, and to enable a more inclusive set of customers to access these technologies. The IUS scheme is designed to address the challenges faced by strong savings and short payback periods associated with distributed RE, EE, and other DER Caribbean households and businesses. The current economic situation exacerbated by COVID-19's economic fallout. Recognizing this, CDB aims to encourage investment in EE/RE projects through a new IUS scheme that provides concessional loans at rates similar avian risk to, introducing the IUS model with affordable rates and favourable terms is crucial. CDB believes that the IUS scheme will provide utilities with an attractive financing option for making customers more accessible costs for RE, EE, and DER technologies than market rates. Note that several mechanisms are in place to ensure that concessional loans are used only by eligible project beneficiaries, including by establishing maximum spreads that can be charged by the electric utilities.

That being the case, CDB is proposing that the GCF Secretariat provide a concessional line of credit amounting to USD 20.8 million. CDB in turn expects to blend the GCF resources with USD 16.2 million in co-financing from the Caribbean Development Bank (CDB) and other development partners to extend a concessional line of credit to each utility/ational Executing Entity. Even after the utilities have received their concessional loans, they will still need to pay interest on these loans at rates determined jointly with national counterparts, notably the regulators during PTY -1. The concessional GCF resources are expected to be blended with co-financing from CDB and other development partners at rates (tentatively 4.5-6.0%) that are competitive relative to the financing provided by traditional/commercial banks. In addition, the concessional line of credit will be structured in a manner that is flexible enough to support greater social inclusion and incentive investment, yet not so low as to distort the local market for RE/EE technologies. The concessional line of credit will also be structured in such a way as readily available as those for renewable energy.


---
**Page 64**
---

B.5.4. Grant financing: The programme will facilitate capacity building and strengthen the execution capacity of TSPs and other financial service providers by providing the Executive Board with a grant funding mechanism. CDB will support the partner BMGC, Executing Entities, and other stakeholders to better understand the GCF’s unique approach to climate change finance and its innovative financing mechanisms. CDB will also help partners develop their understanding when developing, appraising, financing, and implementing climate-related sub-projects, which are not yet eligible for funding under the GCF financially. CDB is therefore requesting grant financing to support the implementation of these activities.

B.5.5. The GCF is uniquely suited to provide the above-mentioned types of financing for the IUS programme. CDB will also work with other partners to mobilize funds from climate-related financial institutions and concessional financing at its disposal through its Special Development Fund (SDF) window, and current concessional funding mechanisms such as the World Bank’s International Climate Initiative (ICI). This is necessary in order to launch and implement IUS at scale. CDB also works with other partners to mobilize funds for climate-related sub-projects that are not yet eligible for funding under the GCF financially.

B.5.6. Co-financing: The CBF expects to provide co-financing amounting to about USD 16.2 million as part of its overall operational costs, which will be used by the Executing Entities for sub-project installations.


B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 3 pages)

B.6.1. The programme includes several elements that will help ensure services, benefit streams and outcomes are sustained after programme closure.

(1) Project level: Through the TPA component, key stakeholders in the participating countries will have enhanced capacity to operate at the IUS model scale over a multi-year time horizon, thereby providing them with the ability to continue implementing IUS projects. The TPA component will also ensure that the programme closures. Through the TA component, the programme will also strengthen the capacity of national governments and other stakeholders in participating countries to implement the IUS model without the need for external support. Furthermore, the programme will connect the participating country’s energy sector with the private sector, thereby enabling the latter to provide financing to the former, notably the worldwide increase in interest rates that have significant increased debt servicing costs in many developing countries. The CBF expects to provide co-financing amounting to about USD 16.2 million as part of its overall operational costs, which will be used by the Executing Entities for sub-project installations.

(2) Sub-sector level: The programme will support the adoption and scaling up of energy technologies and energy efficiency services that are economically beneficial for the end beneficiaries. Greening the IUS model will also ensure that the participating countries’ energy sector, households, and businesses are expected to continue utilizing these technologies and practices for their entire lifetime. This will be achieved through the TPA component, which will provide technical assistance to Executing Entities and broader programme implementation team, in line with the Theory of Change, logical framework and monitoring and evaluation plan. The CBF expects to provide co-financing amounting to about USD 16.2 million as part of its overall operational costs, which will be used by the Executing Entities for sub-project installations.

(3) TSP level: The programme includes a capacity-building element designed to enhance the capacities of TSPs and other financial service providers in all three participating countries. As a result, these entities will be able to better assess climate-


---
**Page 65**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 59

B

related risks and opportunities and engage in supporting/financing the sustainable energy transition, including by working with the electric utilities to continue administering IUS services after programme closure. This will help ensure that the benefits of the IUS model are sustained and scaled up.

(4) End-user beneficiaries: The TA and capacity-building component of the programme places a significant focus on increasing awareness and capacity among potential sub-project beneficiaries (i.e., local households and businesses) to enable them to better understand, adopt, and use energy-efficient technologies and renewable energy technologies and IUS-type services, thereby increasing the potential uptake of IUS-type services over the medium term. The programme will also aim to increase the number of end-users who are aware of IUS in residential and commercial, through its outreach and awareness-raising activities. While the programme will only directly target households and businesses that have been identified as sub-project beneficiaries, it is anticipated to stimulate demand for financing across a larger customer base during and after the programme. This will be achieved by increasing the number of end-users who are aware of IUS and more inclined to anticipate to be more aware of the benefits of the prioritized technologies, more inclined to submit high-quality sub-project proposals. This will enhance the overall quality of financed sub-projects, leading to better project outcomes and a higher likelihood of achieving the desired impact in partner countries, especially considering their relatively small economies. Furthermore, the costs of every IUS-type service are expected to be lower than those of other financing options for driving demand. Coupled with the TA activities that aim to address supply-side barriers (i.e., by strengthening the capacity of utilities and other service providers), this customer-focused support should help ensure that the benefit streams of the IUS model are sustained and scaled up.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section assesses the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### D.1.1. Mitigation impact potential

The analysis on mitigation impact potential accounts for key differences in the national context in the partner countries and the sub-projects included within the GCF programme. The analysis is based on the Global Warming Potential (GWP) of CO2 equivalent emissions, using the 100-year time horizon and the Revised 1996 IPCC Emission Factor data set published by United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change, as well as in the approach to estimating the lifetime of sub-projects. The analysis is based on the potential for GHG emission reductions and their monitoring and reporting.

### D.1.2. The climate change mitigation impact for the sub-projects may arise from the grid electricity consumption avoided by the sub-project (e.g., solar rooftop PV, efficient AC) or from the energy-efficient technologies. The IUS programme has the potential to avoid a total of 851,500 Gg CO2 eq in GHG emissions over the useful life of the sub-projects. Table 10 shows the GHG emissions avoided by country and type of sub-project.

#### Table 10: GHG emissions avoided, dis-aggregated by country and sub-projects

| Type of subproject | Barbados | Belize | Jamaica |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Mitigation impact | Solar rooftop PV | 23.796 | 11,758 | 80,140 |
| Solar rooftop PV with batteries | 3,399 | - | 10,000 | 10,000 |
| Efficient AC | 144,488 | 16,615 | 219,654 | 7,024 |
| Efficient lighting | 13,759 | - | 20,524 | 10,252 |
| Solar water heater | 31,849 | 11,034 | 31,562 | 10,000 |
| **Total** | **275,893** | **43,792** | **334,157** | **100,000** |

Overall, the mitigation impact potential for commercial sub-projects is higher than that of residential sub-projects because of the larger size of sub-projects (e.g., larger system size of solar rooftop PV). The only exception is for solar water heaters in Barbados. The table shows the total GHG emissions avoided by country and type of sub-project, usage assumptions determined according to local contexts. The differences between countries are due to differences in the capital intensity of grid electricity.

### Adaptation impact potential:

#### D.2.1. The adaptation impacts are expected to include the occupants of buildings in households, businesses and other utility customers who develop (and secure financing from their utility) for sub-projects that enable investments in efficient technologies. These investments will reduce energy consumption and thus reduce the demand on the grid electricity system. This is expected to have a positive impact on reducing the increasing risk of heat stress that is outlined in more detail in Annex 2b: Baseline Assessment Report. The estimated number of direct beneficiaries for each sub-project type is based on the total number of residential customers who are expected to implement sub-projects through which they will reduce their electricity consumption and thus reduce demand on the grid electricity system.

## Indirect beneficiaries

Indirect beneficiaries are those individuals or entities who, although not directly participating in the programme, demonstrate a significant interest in the priority adaptation technologies and are likely to invest in or adopt these technologies as part of their business model. These indirect beneficiaries will be able to better understand the impact and awareness generated by the programme, contributing to market development and demand stimulation.

The estimated number of indirect beneficiaries is 30,247.

### D.2. Program shift potential (page: 500 words; approximately 1 page)

#### D.2.1 The proposed programme aims to contribute to the process of transforming the electricity systems and markets in participating countries towards a more sustainable model that supports energy access and climate development pathways. The SUD model serves as both an “implementation mechanism” for demand-side RE, EE, and electrification, and a “market enabler” for the transition to a low-carbon economy. By implementing this innovative model, the programme will support sector transformation by changing the way utilities think about, engage in and shape the energy sector. In particular:

* **Utilities:** The programme aims to encourage participating countries’ utilities to offer a new and innovative DER-focused service as part of their business model. The utilities will fundamentally change (and diversify) their business models from being solely providers of electricity to also becoming providers of energy services. In so doing, the utilities will become catalysts for the transition towards a low-carbon economy by providing customers with access to a wide range of DER technologies/measures that are not simply as consumer goods but rather as critical elements of a country’s overall energy strategy.

* **Customers:** The programme also aims to reshape attitudes about energy (and associated behaviours and decisions) among households and businesses in participating countries. By working with customers to better understand their energy use needs and the benefits of DER technologies, the programme will help them make more informed choices that are conducive to mitigating climate change, increase resilience and improve local air quality, and provide them with a simple and affordable way to access these technologies. In so doing, households and businesses in participating countries will become more active participants in their respective electricity systems. This shift toward more active and engaged households and businesses is fundamentally important for the success of the programme as it will help ensure that customers make energy consumption such that they make the greatest possible contribution to decarbonising and enhancing the resilience of the electricity system. In addition, by providing customers with access to a wide range of DER technologies/measures regulated not simply as consumer goods but rather as critical elements of a country’s overall energy strategy.

#### D.2.3 While always to change the ways that utilities and customers view (including their own use) and support the energy transition, the programme also includes other measures to help ensure a profound and lasting market transformation in participating countries. These include: 1) enhancing customer engagement by providing technical and institutional capacities of key energy sector stakeholders (including utilities, regulators and TSPs) to ensure SUD services can continue to be provided—with increasing efficiency—beyond programme closure.

## Technical Assessment

### Strategic Objectives

- **Strengthen legal and regulatory frameworks in beneficiary countries**
- **Enhance customer outreach and knowledge sharing**
- **Improve technical and institutional capacities**

### D.2.3
The programme will aim to address legal, regulatory, and operational challenges by supporting the preparation and amendment of legal and regulatory frameworks for the development of necessary enabling conditions, including the establishment of administrative procedures, and the operation of energy accounting procedures.

It will also provide capacity building in relation to U.S. national standards and best practices for customer outreach and knowledge sharing, and will develop technology savings calculators, and produce marketing materials. Critical efforts also include hosting knowledge sharing sessions, developing a regional database on sustainable energy solutions, promoting environmental and social standards, and encouraging regional cooperation to exchange insights and best practices.

### D.3 Sustainable development (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### D.3.1
The programme is expected to deliver significant sustainable development co-benefits. It will not only make a substantial contribution towards the achievement of the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): 7 (affordable and clean energy) and 12 (climate action), but also SDGs 3 (health and well-being), 5 (gender equality), 8 (decent work and economic growth), 9 (industry, innovation, and infrastructure), and 16 (partnerships for the goals). These co-benefits are described in more detail below.

#### D.3.1.1 Economic
The IUS model will enable utilities in the BMICs to diversify their revenue streams, engage in new business opportunities, and improve customer satisfaction by providing customers with a range of energy services that can be used to repay the investment costs to the utilities and cover other common operational expenses.

It is estimated that the programme will generate an average net financial savings (NFS) for the first beneficiaries/custodians expected to amount to over USD 100 million per year. This NFS will be generated from a combination of two types of projects: (a) programme-financed sub-projects, which will also generate revenue for (and potentially lead to an expansion in) the utilities’ customer base; and (b) co-investments by the utilities into existing electricity systems and energy sectors (to which the programme will make important contributions) also hold high potential for generating NFS. The economic analysis of these two types of projects is provided below.

The evidence suggests that considerably (generally local) labour will be required to support widespread installation and operation of the IUS technology, as well as the associated business models. This demand will be met by the utilities expending full-time staff after programme closure. Economic co-benefits will also be accrued by the central government through increased tax revenues from the new energy services provided (e.g., electricity exchange (Ex) reserves – a challenge that the programme will help address by reducing the demand/need for imported fossil fuels.


---
**Page 72**
---

### D3.1.2 Environmental

The transition to an electricity system and energy sector based on renewables will significantly reduce the potential for environmental damage associated with the extraction, handling, and combustion of fossil fuels. Moreover, this shift can greatly enhance energy quality in participating countries, given their high dependence on imported oil and gas. It is important to note that the nature of the investments to be made under the programme are not expected to result in large-scale deforestation or other significant environmental impacts. However, due to the small-scale nature of the envisaged investments, sub-projects are expected to have little/marginal detrimental effect on land use and biodiversity.

### D3.1.3 Social

Decreased overall particulate pollution resulting from reduced reliance on diesel fuel for electricity generation can improve health outcomes for residents of participating countries. These social co-benefits include improved air quality, which in turn leads to better respiratory health and increased life expectancy. In addition, the fUJS programme presents a significant opportunity for groups that have had limited access to energy services, such as women, rural populations, low-income households, and small-scale productive individuals. As current utility customers, the fUJS programme will allow them to bypass the need to pay high connection fees and other charges associated with grid connections. The fUJS model will enable these otherwise marginalized groups to invest in and obtain clean and EE technologies which are affordable for them. This is expected to lead to increased access to energy services, including widespread adoption of sustainable energy technologies but also fosters social equity and facilitates a move towards inclusive development.

### D3.1.4 Gender

The programme will take a proactive approach to supporting gender-equitable development. This is guided by the Programme’s Gender Equality Strategy (GES) and the Gender Equality Impact Assessment. Specific actions to be taken by the programme include: (i) working with policymakers to mainstream gender equality in all aspects of the programme, including through the preparation of developing gender mainstreaming guidelines, conducting gender sensitization training for policymakers and regulators, and ensuring that gender is considered as a key criterion when selecting project beneficiaries; (ii) encouraging utilities to integrate women more prominently into energy sector planning and decision-making processes by providing them with opportunities to participate in and engage without benefit from the Programme, including by mainstreaming the energy audits for low-income customers; (iii) supporting the development of gender-sensitive utility tariffs that are specifically tailored to the needs of marginalized groups; (iv) supporting the electric utilities/Non-Executing Entities to utilize the programme as a platform for engaging with women and other marginalized groups through conducting gender-sensitizing training for the utilities participating and TSPs, supporting the utilities in developing gender-sensitive utility tariffs, and providing them with opportunities to engage with women and other marginalized groups; and (v) supporting the utilities to adopt/greenprint systems for capturing and analyzing beneficiary data disaggregated by gender. The programme will also support the development of a monitoring framework aligning with each country’s context to reduce corruption within their NDCs. Moreover, by advancing the energy sector in participating countries, the programme is expected to contribute to enhanced energy security, resistance to heat stress, and grid resilience in the face of increasing climate change.

### D4 Needs of recipient (max 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The BMGCs are acutely vulnerable to climate risks due to their geographic exposure to increasing temperatures and extreme weather events. Their economies are heavily reliant on climate-sensitive sectors like tourism and agriculture. These risks are exacerbated by high reliance on fossil fuels in their electricity sectors, contributing to significant greenhouse gas emissions. The adoption of renewable energy and EE technologies will help aligning with each country’s context to reduce corruption within their NDCs. Moreover, by advancing the energy sector in participating countries, the programme is expected to contribute to enhanced energy security, resistance to heat stress, and grid resilience in the face of increasing climate change.

At an economic and social level, the three participating countries are grappling with significant fiscal constraints and high levels of poverty. The current energy access situation in these countries masks significant social and income inequality within the countries. Such income-focused classifications also fail to account for the countries’ pronounced vulnerability to climate change and other hazards, all of which can

D.4.2 It is also important to note that within the participating countries, lower-income households, smaller (and more informal) businesses and marginalized groups have thus far mostly been unable to participate in (and benefit from) energy access programmes. These groups are often excluded due to a lack of awareness about these customer groups. These energy audits will ensure that the programme is able to provide a just transition towards universal access for all customers, including those who have previously been left behind by other programmes and businesses but have thus far been able to invest in DERs; this has mostly been limited to those that are on the upper end of the income spectrum. The IUS model will be designed with the specific needs and challenges faced by lower-income and marginalized groups, this dynamic is unlikely to change. The IUS programme therefore aims to provide a more inclusive approach to universal access for all customers, including enabling customers to access the financing needed to invest. The IUS model (and the way it will be deployed under the programme) will ensure that the needs of lower-income and marginalized groups are taken into account by these groups, such as women, PWLDs, the LGBTQIA+ community, and indigenous peoples. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) will provide a framework for ensuring that the IUS model is designed to address the specific needs of these groups in terms of access to resources and information. This will enable these groups not only to benefit from the prioritized technologies but also to participate more fully in the energy transition, including by increasing their agency and social empowerment. The programme will simultaneously provide training and capacity-building initiatives for lower-income households, smaller businesses, and marginalized groups (i) to increase their ability to manage environmental & social risks; and (ii) support the participating electric utilities to be inclusive and responsive to the needs of marginalized groups and to reinforce their policies for successful rollout of the model.

D.5 Country ownership (max. 300 words, approximately 1 page)

Alignment with national priorities

As outlined in Section B.6., the proposed programme is closely aligned with the priorities identified in the NDCs submitted by all three countries: Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica. All three countries assign considerable importance to reducing GHG emissions from the energy sector by scaling up investment in renewable energy and energy efficiency through the deployment of DERs. The IUS model will be designed to support these national priorities as well as those stipulated in the policies, strategies and plans that underpin their NDCs. This includes:

a. Barbados: National Energy Policy (2019-30); National Climate Change Policy Framework.

b. Belize: Belize 2016-2030; National Energy Policy Framework; National Energy Strategy and Action Plan (2018-2035); National Renewable Energy Action Plan; and Action Plan.

c. Jamaica: National Energy Policy (2009-2030); Climate Change Policy Framework; Vision 2030 Jamaica – National Development Plan.

D.5.2 National stakeholders have also been actively involved in efforts to get their countries “ready” for IUS, including by providing technical and financial support activities over the preceding years. These same stakeholders—including the utilities, energy ministries, regulatory bodies, and other relevant government agencies—will continue to provide technical and financial support activities over the preceding years. These same stakeholders—including the utilities, energy ministries, regulatory bodies, and other relevant government agencies—will continue to provide technical and financial support activities over the preceding years.

Capacity of executing entities to deliver

D.5.3 All three participating electric utilities (as National Executing Entities) are already experienced with DEBT initiatives. The National Executing Entities will build on their experience when executing their projects under this programme. They will also be supported by a Technical Assistance team to ensure that the financing is equitable manner. With support from the TA component, the National Executing Entities will also: (a) facilitate all aspects of project preparation and implementation; (b) prepare and submit the project documents for GCF financing; (c) adopt and implement a standardized and timebound application process; and (d) utilize and distribute the resources in an efficient manner. The Technical Assistance team, which is led by the project preparation team have assessed the utilised policies and procedures to confirm that they have sufficient capacity to deliver the programme. The TA team has also identified areas of strength as well as specific areas where additional support and guidance would be needed – all of which has informed the design of GCF-supported projects.

The role of NDEs and other (regional) stakeholders

D.5.4 Similar to other “behind-the-meter” initiatives, the IUG programme involves a complex process with multiple actors. These include public entities such as government ministries and electricity regulators, as well as private entities such as project developers and financial institutions. The complexity of this process is further due to regional scope and the involvement of the GCF. This uniqueness necessitates the participation of various stakeholders in the design and implementation phases of the programme. The Technical Assistance team plays a pivotal role by ensuring the programme’s alignment with the local context, engaging stakeholders, and acting as an intermediary between them. The TA team is also responsible for providing guidance to NDEs during the programme preparation process, often directly supporting data gathering and stakeholder consultations. As NDES focus on support that their final programme proposal, as evidenced by the No-Objection letters that are included in Annex 1.

D.5.5 Moreover, the programme places a strong emphasis on engaging with regional stakeholders such as CREEC and other relevant organizations. The Technical Assistance team will also engage with local communities, associations, and customers. Particular emphasis is placed on engaging underserved communities, as described in Section D.3.1.

D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max: 500 words; approximately 1 page)

D.6.1 The IUG programme is poised to significantly reduce greenhouse gas emissions in the three participating BMCs, through the deployment of energy-efficient technologies such as LED lighting and solar water heaters. The 79 per cent reduction in GHG emissions is achieved by avoiding 20,583 metric tonnes (Mt) of CO2e avoided for the overall programme (including GCF financing and CDB co-financing). The cost-effective nature of these projects is further demonstrated through their inclusion as “cost-effective modals” as outlined in Table D below. These are considered to be competitive cost-per-basis rates in a SIDS context, where emissions reductions are often more costly than in larger countries.

D.6.2 The programme also delivers important adaptation benefits. ## E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### E.7.1 The implementation of the IUS programme will involve:
* Monitoring and reporting progress in relation to the programme's logical framework, as defined section E.6;
* Monitoring and reporting on financial progress (i.e. approvals/commitments, disbursements, reflows).


---
**Page 111**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 185

- Monitoring and reporting on sub-project compliance with environmental and social risk management requirements, guided by the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) that is included in Annex 6.
- Monitoring and reporting on the implementation of the GAP that is included in Annex 8.
- Mid-term and final evaluations to assess implementation progress and challenges and propose corrective actions.

## E.7.2 Additional information about the monitoring and reporting processes is included in Annex 21: Programme Operations Manual. Further Information about the envisaged methodologies for the pre-estimate of emissions that are likely to be reduced/avoided, as well as the methods to be used to calculate actual emissions reductions during the implementation of the UGS programs, is included in Annex 22: Assessment of GHG Emission Reductions and their Monitoring and Reporting.

### Monitoring and reporting

Under Component 1, monitoring will be led by the CCB TA Execution Team, which will be overall responsibility for procuring and contracting almost all consultants and other service providers. The CCB TA Execution Team will ensure that all sub-projects are implemented in a timely manner and complete their assignments in a timely and efficient manner and will gather the data and documentation needed to demonstrate progress (in line with the logical frameworks for the programs) during the implementation of the consultancy and contracting contracts. This will include monitoring to ensure that the ESMF and GAP (and its components) are being followed.

Under Component 2, the monitoring of sub-projects will be led by the electricity utilities (as National Executing Entities). Initial data on sub-project implementation (e.g., installation of eligible technologies) will be sourced from the contracted TSPs, as well information about adherence to necessary environmental and social risk management requirements. The CCB TA Execution Team will also provide support to the utilities in this regard.

To supplement the meter-based monitoring, the CCB TA Execution Team will also act a team to assist with providing additional data/infomation from sub-project beneficiaries (e.g., through interviews, surveys and site visits). This will enable the utilities and CCB to cross-check the data from the meters at regular intervals throughout the program implementation period, and thus validate the meter data and/or explain anomalies. The full will gather other from a vendor of metering equipment while also following up with those whose meter data deviates from the change/benefits that the utility would have projected at the time of sub-project approval.

### E.7.4 Reporting

Reporting from electric utilities (National Executing Entities) to the CCB Accredited Entity Team: CCB will enter into a supplier agreement with each electricity utility that specifies the utility's reporting obligations. This will include both financial reporting (information on disbursements, revenues, etc.) and progress reporting (information on the sub-project pipeline, approvals, implementation status and progress vis-à-vis performance metrics), covering each utility’s full portfolio of sub-projects financed using the blended tier of credit provided under the toll program. The utilities will be required to prepare and submit these reports to the


---
**Page 112**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 106

#### E

**GCF Accredited Entity Team at regular intervals to be specified in the subsidiary agreements. The utilities' reporting to CDB will also include information on compliance with environmental and social risk management requirements, as well as GESI requirements, for the portfolio of sub-projects. The utilities will be expected to gather/collect the data needed for these reports, including using the methods/processes outlined above.**

**Reporting from CDB to GCF: Using the data contained in the reporting provided by the National Executing Entities and the CDB TA Execution Team, as well as other relevant information, CDB will prepare the following reports:**

- **Annual Performance Reports (APRs) that provide detailed information on the status of the programme throughout its implementation period.**
- **A mid-term evaluation (interim evaluation) report at the midpoint of the implementation period of the programme, which will be prepared by an independent external evaluator.**
- **A final evaluation report at the end of the implementation period of the programme, which is expected to be prepared by the same independent external evaluator as the mid-term evaluation.**

#### 6.7.5 Evaluation

In line with GCF requirements, CDB will contract an external evaluator to conduct independent evaluations halfway into the programme implementation period (mid-term review) and at the end of the programme (final evaluation). The GCF Accredited Entity Team will procure the evaluator using an open tender process and will support the selection of the evaluator. The mid-term and final evaluations are expected to be conducted by the same external evaluator, unless it is determined that these evaluations will be conducted in line with inter alia, the following principles: independence; competencies and capacities; impartiality; credibility; transparency; integrity and good governance.

The evaluation methods for the mid-term and final evaluations are to be determined, but are tentatively expected to include: (i) key informant interviews with beneficiary customers and institutional beneficiaries (i.e., utilities, regulators, TSPs); and (ii) detailed review of relevant programme documentation, including monitoring data pertaining to the implementation of the project. The mid-term evaluation will also consider the progress made in implementing the GESI strategy, as well as lessons learned from contracted by the CDB TA Execution Team, APRs and other project reports, based on an agreed list of evidence to be provided by CDB to the contracted evaluator.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F1. Risk factors and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1 | Challenges in establishing efficient systems and procedures for IUS service delivery

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Medium | High |

**Description:**

F.1.1 There is a moderate likelihood that utilities may face challenges in putting in place systems and procedures to deliver the IUS programme. For instance, there is a risk that utilities may not complete the start-up phase during PY 7 as envisaged.

(Mitigation Measures)

F.1.2 To mitigate the challenge of establishing efficient systems and procedures for IUS service delivery, the TA component focuses on comprehensive support and capacity building for utilities. This includes advising regulatory bodies to develop a framework for the implementation of the IUS programme, providing training to build technical capacity. External consultants will provide direct assistance and expert advice on regulatory enhancements. A digital application portal will streamline processes, while training will cover best practices and effective procedural frameworks.

### Selected Risk Factor 2 | Capacity constraints among TSPs

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

**Description:**

F.1.3 There is a risk associated with the quantity and technical capabilities of technology service providers (TSPs) in the participating countries to fully implement the IUS programme, especially for EIS and EE technologies.

(Mitigation Measures)

F.1.4 While this risk is noteworthy, the feasibility assessment did not identify it as a major concern. Additionally, the TA component includes training for TSPs to help them improve their services/products and enhance the efficiency with which they can deliver IUS services. Furthermore, the TSPs will be encouraged to connect directly with the SBACs through which the TSPs can share knowledge and best practices with one another, as well as with government officials, regulators, and utilities.

### Selected Risk Factor 3 | Regulatory and Policy Barriers

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | High |

**Description:**

F.1.5 Inconsistent regulatory frameworks and policies across the Caribbean can impede the implementation of the IUS programme. Challenges include slow permit processes, lack of supportive policies for renewable energy, etc.


---
**Page 114**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 108

## Mitigation Measure(s)

### Selected Risk Factor 4: Risk overestimation and/or engagement among potential beneficiaries

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | High |

**F.1.7:** Some potential beneficiaries may be reluctant to adopt/reinvest in new RE and EE technologies. This often stems from concerns over high initial costs, operational complexity, and cultural resistance to change, as well as regulatory uncertainty. Potential beneficiaries may also be aware of sustainable energy technologies but are not yet convinced that they will provide the desired benefits or results (yet) well-established.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

#### Selected Risk Factor 5: Risk related to compliance, including money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ML/TF | Medium | Medium |

**F.1.8:** The IUS programme includes a robust strategy to increase stakeholder engagement and buy-in. This will involve targeted educational campaigns to raise awareness of the benefits and practical applications of RE and EE technologies, as well as the importance of compliance with relevant regulations. Regular workshops, training sessions, and seminars will be facilitated to allow stakeholders to interact directly with these technologies, addressing any concerns they may have. Regular customer service offerings will also be provided to ensure that customers are continually informed into programme implementation, thereby enhancing acceptance and minimizing resistance.

**F.1.9:** The IUS programme preparation team have conducted a detailed assessment of the electric utilities' policies, procedures, capacity, and resources in relation to their ability to serve as Executing Entities without posing unacceptable/unmanageable risks related to compliance issues. These assessments included an evaluation of the electric utilities' systems for managing risk, including accounting and reporting systems, internal controls and policies/procedures related to prohibited practices and AMCFs. The two entities for which a detailed assessment was conducted are listed in Annex 2: Feasibility Study. JPS will be subject to the same type of review during PY1.


---
**Page 115**
---

In addition to entering into subsidiary agreements with the utilities (as National Executing Entities) that include provisions related to ABC/CFT, as well as other compliance-related risks. This is guided by CDB's own policies and procedures for managing these types of risks during the CCF acceleration process and found to be satisfactory and consistent with GCF standards and requirements.

In this context, it is also important to note that Jamaica is the only country from the BMIs that is currently in the Inception phase of the CCF acceleration process. The other countries are in the Implementation phase.


| Selected Risk Factor | Risks related to default/payment rates by participating utility customers |
| --- | --- |
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| Credit | Low | Medium |
| Description | F.I.1.13 Based on the experiences of stakeholders and jurisdictions that have implemented US-type models (i.e., utility-based financing for RE, EE and other DERs), CDB has estimated a very low default/payment rate among customers who are paying their regular utility bills to the electric utilities/national executing entities. This is because these customers are already making payments to the electric utilities/national Executing Entities to customer's regular utility bills, which are a priority for most customers and are not subject to any of the same financial constraints as other types of loans. Nevertheless, there is a risk that non-payment rates in the three participating countries may be higher than expected, particularly in the event of an economic downturn.


Mitigation Measure(s)

F.I.1.12 To mitigate the risk of higher-than-expected default/payment rates, the Programme has prioritized knowledge and awareness raising activities to support customers who are paying their regular utility bills to the electric utilities/national executing entities. This will help offset the repayment charges immediately. In addition, the use of on-bill financing for RE, EE and other DERs will reduce the amount of money that customers need to pay upfront and the additional charges associated with financing the investment on the same bill, which will help beneficiaries - including those with more limited financial literacy - to understand the real-time benefits of continuing to make payments. The Programme has also prioritized knowledge and awareness raising activities TA component includes activities to support enhanced customer awareness raising and outreach, through which customers can be informed about the benefits of financing RE, EE and other DERs on their regular utility bills. This will help ensure that customers are aware of the significant economic benefits over the medium/long term as long as they meet their repayment obligations over the course of the investment period. The Programme has also prioritized knowledge and awareness raising activities to support enhanced customer awareness raising and outreach, through which customers can be informed about the benefits of financing RE, EE and other DERs on their regular utility bills. This will similarly help reinforce these efforts to enhance understanding among customers and thereby mitigate the risks associated with higher-than-expected default/payment rates.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and Social Management (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### G.1.1 An ESM is developed to ensure that the programmes adequately identifies, mitigates, manages and monitors environmental and social risks and potential impacts during programme implementation, and complies with the requirements of the GCF and the SGP. The ESM will be prepared in close consultation with national stakeholders. By extension, the ESF will ensure compliance with the environmental and social standards of both the GCF and the SGP.

#### G.1.2 The programme is classified as Category B. This is because the nature of the investments to be made through the sub-projects have the potential for limited adverse environmental and social impacts and/or risks, all of which can be identified, assessed and managed during project implementation. An Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) will be developed in close consultation with national stakeholders in the three countries. The risks and associated risk mitigation/management measures outlined in the ESMF will be integrated into the Project Design Document (PDD). The National Executing Entities (NEEs), through their Support Consulting Firms (SCFs) each prepare a utility-specific Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and corresponding ESMP Implementation Plan. The ESMPs and ESMP Implementation Plans are developed to provide more detailed guidance to the national stakeholders – notably the electric utilities given their roles as national executing entities for the programme. These risks, mitigation and associated ESM measures through the sub-project lifecycle will be also provided/include:

- **detailed guidance on the specific ESM risks associated with each type of equipment/technology:**
  - a description of the risk and how it can be mitigated or managed during project application to ensure such risks are clearly identified and sub-projects can be properly categorised, which will be an adopted version of the draft ESMPs;
  - actionable guidance on which associated risk mitigation/management measures should be implemented in projects with a specific type of equipment/technology;
  - templates and associated guidance to support the development of the corresponding Environmental, Social and Health (ESH) Management Plan (ESHP) for each sub-project, which will be an adopted version of the ESMPs; thus is categorised as a Cat B project.
- **detailed guidance on how to develop ESMPs that NEEs have to adhere to the ESM requirements outlined in the ESR plans and, how to report to the utilities to demonstrate compliance with such requirements:**
  - publication of a template ESMP for each sub-project;
  - inclusion of an ESMP Implementation Plan into their contracts with TSPs; and
  - a submission plan that specifies the requirements associated with the collection, storage and final disposal of waste (e.g. old units and other technologies that are removed during installation, as well as problematic materials such as lead-acid batteries) in accordance with national regulations.
- **detailed guidance on how to develop ESMPs for each sub-project:**
  - a description of the risk and how it can be mitigated or managed during project application to ensure such risks are clearly identified and sub-projects can be properly categorised, which will be an adopted version of the draft ESMPs;
  - actionable guidance on which associated risk mitigation/management measures should be implemented in projects with a specific type of equipment/technology;
  - templates and associated guidance to support the development of the corresponding Environmental, Social and Health (ESH) Management Plan (ESHP) for each sub-project, which will be an adopted version of the ESMPs; thus is categorised as a Cat B project.

#### G.1.3 The ESM that is attached at Annex 6 also includes an initial overview of the sub-project workflow (with clearly defined roles and responsibilities for E&S management) to demonstrate how/when the identification and assessment of environmental and social risks, as well as the development and implementation of associated risk mitigation/management measures, will be implemented.

#### G.1.4 In addition to the ESM, an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) has been developed to guide decision-making processes in relation to indigenous peoples in Belize and Jamaica. See Annex 7 for details on IPPs that will be developed in tandem with the ESFIs for Belize and Jamaica, and in close consultation with indigenous groups. The IPPFs are designed to ensure that the identification of environmental and social risks associated with programme activities in Belize and Jamaica is done in an inclusive and culturally appropriate manner.

### G.2 All NEEs will have a dedicated Environmental and Social Management (ESM) Team. Given that the scope is limited and manageable, it will be important to strengthen and supplement the capacity of the National Executing Entities and their respective ESM Teams to ensure that they are able to effectively identify, assess, mitigate, manage and monitor environmental and social risks and requirements. The TA component of the programme therefore includes key support measures, including training for staff on environmental and social management, monitoring, and reporting during programme implementation. The TA component of the programme will also supplement the capacity of the electric utilities (in their role as National Executing Entities) to fully


---
**Page 117**
---

E&S-related responsibilities, notably by contracting: (i) a National Energy Consultant to work within each electric utility and (ii) an Implementation Support Consulting Firm that will also include an E&S Specialist.

### G.1.6 To enhance accountability and thereby help ensure that E&S risks are properly mitigated and managed, the following mechanisms have been established:

- **(a) Complaints/Compliments:** Any person affected by the programme (beneficiaries and non-beneficiaries who are affected by the programme and/or sub-projects) can submit complaints/grievances. This includes project staff, contractors, consultants, and other parties involved in the implementation of the programme.
  - Complaints/Compliments may be submitted to: (i) CDB’s Office of Integrity, Compliance and Accountability (IC&A); through its website (<https://www.caribank.org/compliance/>), by email (ic@caribank.org), or via mail at Head, Office of Integrity, Compliance and Accountability.
  - Building A Caribbean Development Bank P.O. Box 408 Writtle St. Michael Barbados B18 11000
- **(b) Complaints/Compliments:** Any person affected by the programme (beneficiaries and non-beneficiaries who are affected by the programme and/or sub-projects) can submit complaints/grievances directly to the GCF. More information about the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism is accessible at the following link: <https://www.greenclimatefund.org/>
  - Specific complaints can be submitted to the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism via the following link: <https://www.greenclimatefunds.org/reporting-complaints>
- **(c) Complaints/Compliments:** Any person affected by the programme (beneficiaries and non-beneficiaries who are affected by the programme and/or sub-projects) can submit complaints/grievances to: (i) CDB’s Projects Compliance Mechanism (PCM), which is further described below; or (ii) GCF Independent Redress Mechanism, which is also described above.

### G.1.7 The ESMF was developed in close consultation with relevant national stakeholders. The programme presented a number of challenges including the lack of relevant regulations and the electric utility was questionnaire-based survey.
- The team also organized virtual face-to-face interviews with TSPLs, whose insights were of critical importance given their central role in implementing interventions (and reporting on) risk mitigation and management. A total of 12 TSPLs were interviewed between April and June 2019. Data obtained through questionnaire-based surveys during the interviews, notes were taken, and each interviewee asked for about 30 minutes to share their experiences with the ESMF. The team ensured that all interviewees leading the interviewees during the interviews and reacted in a neutral manner by not signaling preferred answers.

## G.2.1 Stakeholder engagement will be similarly important during programme implementation. The ESMP provides clear guidance to the Executing Entities in this regard. The above-described trainings for electric utilities and TSPs will also further strengthen their capacity to support inclusive stakeholder engagement in line with ESMF requirements. The following are the key elements of the TA component:

### G.2.1.1 A Gender Analysis and Action Plan (max: 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### G.2.1.1.1 The design of the programme has been informed by a detailed Gender Assessment which focused on four key groups as relevant per country i.e., women, persons with disabilities (PWDs), the LGBTIQ+ community, and indigenous peoples. These groups are often excluded from access to energy services and technologies due to their inability to finance and financial services. Although the ways in which this challenge may manifest for each group often differ, the underlying causes are similar: lack of education, limited access to information, gender-based violence, economic power and lack of capital; the resulting effect is the same across the board - namely that financial and non-financial barriers exist at all levels of society. These barriers will not be overcome by a transition towards sustainable energy transition, and this will not automatically benefit from the services and opportunities presented by this programme.

#### G.2.1.1.2 An additional unique barrier observed primarily in Belize and Jamaica is the weak enabling environment for the adoption of renewable energy technologies. These countries have seen less work and campaigning done around promoting the use and uptake of said technologies. This can be a deterrent as persons may be unaware of the benefits that these technologies offer, and may not be aware of how to access them or what they cost. Additionally, current attitudes on these technologies may not be the most conducive to the successful implementation of this programme. Closing the information and empowerment gap is a critical one to be tackled through the TA component on several levels.

### G.2.3 An additional unique barrier observed primarily in Belize and Jamaica is the weak enabling environment for the adoption of renewable energy technologies. These countries have seen less work and campaigning done around promoting the use and uptake of said technologies. This can be a deterrent as persons may be unaware of the benefits that these technologies offer, and may not be aware of how to access them or what they cost. Additionally, current attitudes on these technologies may not be the most conducive to the successful implementation of this programme. Closing the information and empowerment gap is a critical one to be tackled through the TA component on several levels.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 / PAGE 113

## Figure 22: Summary of barriers and constraints experienced by marginalised groups attempting to utilise or engage with RE technologies

| Barrier | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Lack of technical knowledge | Marginalised groups face a lack of technical knowledge about renewable energy (RE) technologies. |
| Limited access to financing | Marginalised groups often have limited access to financing for RE technologies, which can be a significant barrier to their adoption and use. |
| Lack of awareness | Marginalised groups may not be aware of the benefits or opportunities associated with RE technologies. |
| Limited access to information | Marginalised groups may lack access to accurate and reliable information about RE technologies. |
| Limited access to training | Marginalised groups often have limited access to training on how to use and maintain RE technologies, which can lead to a lack of confidence in their ability to operate these systems effectively. |

### G2.4: Through the Gender Action Plan (GAP), the programme will address the following gender needs specific to this area:

#### (a) Improving the enabling environment:
The programme will support utilities and energy ministries to better understand gender and social inclusion issues in the context of the energy transition and integrate these understanding into their policies, strategies, and programmes. The programme will also provide training for utility staffs, TSPs and other key sector stakeholders on how to improve their understanding of the gender dimensions of energy access and use, including Gender-Based Violence (GBV), Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAR) issues in the sector.

#### (b) Enhancing awareness among marginalised groups:
The programme will support utilities and energy ministries to develop and implement strategies for reaching and communicating with marginalised groups on priority technologies (to be led by the energy utilities) and the IIS programme (to be led by the electric utilities). These strategies will place a specific emphasis on reaching, and raising awareness among, women and girls in marginalised communities. The programme will also include an A component to support implementation of these strategies which will similarly emphasise inclusivity as outlined in Section B.1 above.

#### (c) Promoting a more inclusive model:
The IIS model itself provides a more inclusive way to support sustainable energy investments among marginalized groups, by allowing them to access financing for RE technologies through the IIS programme and direct repayment on favourable terms through off-bond financing. The IIS model will directly address several of the financial barriers that often prevent marginalised groups from investing in sustainable energy technologies.

#### (d) Direct sub-project support:
While the above-described areas are expected to make meaningful contributions to improving the inclusivity of the IIS programme, lower-income households and other marginalized groups may still face additional challenges. The programme will provide direct sub-project support for these groups.

## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Legal arrangements

**G.3.1:** The CDB will sign a subsidiary agreement with each of the National Executing Entities that will govern the use of funds throughout the programme implementation period, as well as the terms and conditions for repayment of their respective loans from the CDB. These agreements will be subject to the same financial management standards and requirements that may have to be added to account for the provisions in the Funded Agreement between the National Executing Entities and the CDB and the GCF. The process of preparing such subsidiary agreements will be led by the CDB Legal Department in co-operation with the CDB Accredited Entity Team.

### Fiduciary standards

**G.3.2:** Each electric utility (as a National Executing Entity) will bear overall responsibility for executing its respective subproject and ensuring that it is carried out in accordance with relevant fiduciary standards. The utilities will administer resources using their internal systems and procedures (e.g., for financial management, procurement, etc.). These systems and procedures are subject to the same fiduciary standards as those which are captured in the overall Programme Operations Manual and will be elaborated in more detail in the utility-specific Financial Management Plan. In addition, the CDB has already assessed each of these utilities’ capacity to fulfill their respective Executive Entity roles and notes that each utility is capable of fulfilling its fiduciary responsibilities. The satisfactory completion of which will be a condition precedent to first disbursement of the line of credit to that entity.

**G.3.3:** The overall financial management of the programme will be done in accordance with relevant CDB policies, procedures, and standards. These include the Financial Management Policy (2019), as well as other policies evidenced by CDB’s accreditation to the Adaptation Fund and Green Climate Fund, as well as its track record of managing projects for these funds (e.g., a Sovereign Credit Facility with the World Bank and International Finance Corporation and other Multilateral Development Banks such as European Investment Bank, Inter-American Development Bank). The CDB OPM will therefore guide all aspects of financial management under the programme, including disbursements to each National Executing Entity under their respective lines of credit which are generally expected to use the CDB fund approach.

### Procurement

**G.3.4:** Procurement under this programme will be managed in accordance with the standards and requirements outlined in the “Procurement Policy for Projects Financed by CDB” (2019) as well as the “Procurement Procedures for Projects Financed by CDB” (2019). These policies and procedures are subject to the same fiduciary standards for public procurement, and cover the procurement of Goods, Works, Non-Consulting Services and related services.
