# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP275

## Proposal name:
Scaling up the Deployment of Integrated Utilities Services (IUS) to Support Energy Sector Transformation in the Caribbean (Phase I) Programme

## Accredited entity:
Caribbean Development Bank

## Country/[ies]:
Barbados, Belize and Jamaica

## Project/programme size: Medium


# Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

## 1. Overview

### 1.1 Overview
This funding proposal, submitted by the Caribbean Development Bank (CDB) as accredited entity (AE), seeks to establish and scale up Integrated Utilities Services (IUS). Proposed project activities will support the deployment of IUS in a range of accelerating decarbonization and enhancing energy resilience. The proposal represents Phase I of a larger programme that aims to build on lessons learned from other jurisdictions, replicate their business models to provide distributed renewable energy (RE), energy efficiency, and other demand-side management services.

The IUS model builds on the concept of utilities acting as energy service companies, bundling financing, installation and maintenance of clean energy solutions such as solar photovoltaics (PV) and energy storage. The utility would own the assets and they can be repaid through on-bill financing. The utilities would serve as key delivery agents, supported by regulatory reforms to ensure that they are able to operate in a competitive market. CDB serves as the central coordinating entity and lender, channeling GCF resources and co-financing from its own balance sheet.

The proposal is structured around two interdependent components:

- **Component 1 (Technical Assistance)** focuses on enabling conditions. It provides grant financing to support the creation of regulatory frameworks, institutional readiness, customer awareness campaigns, and capacity building for utilities and other stakeholders. CDB will establish a dedicated technical assistance team, supported by three national teams, to oversee project implementation in each country. The proposal includes a phased approach: non-revenue-generating activities are essential to market formation and require full commitment from the CDB.

- **Component 2 (Loan Component)** provides concessional financing facility for the actual deployment of distributed RE, energy efficiency, and distributed energy resources (DERs) at the customer level. The proposal seeks to mobilize USD 16.2 million from CDB to establish credit lines to the participating national utilities. Utilities acting as executing entities (EEs) will lend these resources to eligible sub-projects —


---
**Page 133**
---

# Impact potential

## Scale: Medium

The proposed BIF Programme seeks to deliver both mitigation and adaptation benefits through the utility-led deployment of distributed RE energy efficiency and other demand-side measures.

### 1.2 Impact potential

Scale: Medium

The proposed BIF Programme seeks to deliver both mitigation and adaptation benefits through the utility-led deployment of distributed RE, energy efficiency and other demand-side measures.


---
**Page 134**
---

energy poverty in Barbados, Belize and Jamaica. These three Caribbean SIDS face increasing climate risks such as intensified storms, prolonged droughts, sea level rise and rising average temperatures, which are already affecting the reliability and cost of power generation and distribution. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is supporting these countries to increase households and businesses to access and finance distributed energy solutions that are otherwise difficult to adopt due to cost, credit constraints and regulatory barriers.

- Mitigation: This figure shows the potential for avoided grid electricity consumption through the installation of rooftop PV solar devices, water heaters, efficient air conditioners and heat pumps. The avoided emissions from these technologies and their associated potential varies significantly by country due to differing grid carbon intensities and market barriers. The figures shown are based on a 5-year period (2017-2022) and are adjusted for each country's national context and nationally determined contribution trajectory.

13. This figure reflects differentiated assumptions about technology lifespans, ranging from 5 years to 20 years for rooftop PV solar installations, and incorporated projected technology costs and grid carbon intensities. The avoided emissions from these technologies potential varies significantly by country due to differing grid carbon intensities and market barriers. The figures shown are based on a 5-year period (2017-2022) and are adjusted for each country's national context and nationally determined contribution trajectory.

14. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) finds the mitigation estimation methodology is robust, clear and in line with Independent Fiscal Institution best practices, as detailed in annexes 3 and 22. However, as with many distributed energy programmes, realised emissions reductions will depend on a range of factors including the rate of technology take-up by end-users, the rate of replacement of existing technologies, and ongoing maintenance over time. Reliance on the IIS Programme or similar estimates should be limited to the indicative nature of the results through the delivery channel, but performance should be closely tracked in implementation.

15. Adaptation Potential: The IIS Programme also articulates a clear, though modest, adaptation potential for these three countries. This is based on an assessment of access to efficient cooling technologies particularly air conditioning systems which can help mitigate the health and productivity impacts associated with rising temperatures. The IIS Programme relies on update projections, as the GCF estimates that approximately 10,655 individuals (3,528 women and 5,277 men) will benefit directly from these adaptation measures, primarily through improved access to cooling technologies.

16. An additional 30,000 indirect beneficiaries are projected, based on broader awareness, demonstrated by the fact that 94% of respondents in the baseline survey were aware of air conditioning and its benefits. While this figure is not backed by rigorous market modelling, it is a reasonable proxy for the broader ecosystem impacts expected through regulatory reform, stakeholder engagement and replication.

17. The IIS Programme also provides an overview of the key findings from the more detailed vulnerability analysis and a clearer articulation of the causal pathways linking cooling access to health and productivity benefits. This is further elaborated in Annex 2b. Annex 2b provides some baseline data but monitoring and evaluation at the implementation stage will need to track adaptation benefits more rigorously, ideally with disaggregated reporting on gender impacts.

18. Enabling Environment and Structural Impact: Beyond direct mitigation and adaptation impacts, the IIS Programme also aims to support a range of enabling environment reform and institutional strengthening under Component 1. This includes establishing a regulatory framework for behind-the-meter solutions, enhancing utility and technology provider capacity, and supporting the development of a market for rooftop PV solar deployment. The emphasis on local stakeholder outreach and regional knowledge-sharing is


---
**Page 135**
---

appropriate and consistent with the goal of scaling the model across additional borrowing member countries in a potential Phase II.

The AIF has also proposed several mechanisms to ensure that concessionality from GCF is passed through to end beneficiaries, including limits on utility spreads and regulatory oversight of credit risk management. The proposal also seeks to address some limitations in offering long-term, low-interest financing from its balance sheet. The concessionality is expected to be passed through to the end beneficiary by limiting the spread between the AIF's prevailing commercial offerings in the region, thereby enabling access for customer segments that would otherwise not have access.

### 1.3 Paradigm shift potential

The proposal offers a well-considered approach to addressing entrenched structural barriers in the Caribbean energy sector and exhibits several characteristics consistent with the GCF definition of paradigm shift. The IIS Programme is designed to catalyse systemic


---
**Page 136**
---

transfer of energy services delivered, financed and valued.

26\. The proposed RIS Programme aims to advance structural transformation in the energy sectors of Barbados, Belize and Jamaica by embedding distributed RE, energy efficiency and other demand-side resources (DERs) into the existing grid infrastructure. Through this approach, the programme lies in positioning regulated utilities as facilitators of customer-level DER deployment, rather than as providers of a traditional supply-oriented or subsidy-based utility offer. This shift from the traditional supplier model towards a more service-oriented role is both operationally sound and aligned with the broader decentralization of energy systems.

27\. The RIS Programme will support the transition to a new energy system through clean energy technologies and behavioural tools, enabling greater participation in the energy transition by households, micro- and medium-sized enterprises (MSEEs) who have historically faced high barriers to participation. In parallel, the RIS Programme includes a robust enabling environment component aimed at addressing institutional and regulatory barriers to DER deployment, particularly for the distributed generation of RE through small-scale solar photovoltaic (PV) systems and other DERs. This includes support for permitting processes, energy accounting modernization, and the development of a national grid code. The RIS Programme also includes a financial anchor anchoring the model in long-term institutional practice and ensuring it can be sustained and replicated without ongoing external concessional support.

28\. While the RIS Programme is conceived at scale relatively modest, both in terms of greenhouse gas abatement and financing volume, its design is intentionally structured to serve as an enabling environment for a broader regional approach to other CDB borrowing member countries. Knowledge sharing efforts, digital platforms, and regional institutional engagement (e.g., through the Caribbean Centre for Renewable Energy and Energy Efficiency) will be key components in this regard.

30\. However, while the RIS Programme is well-conceived from a distributed energy market development perspective, its paradigmatic potential must be assessed considering broader sectoral policy objectives. In particular, several Caribbean Small Island Developing States (SIDS), countries have articulated highly ambitious national RE targets. Barbados, for instance, has set a goal of 100% RE by 2035 and is currently implementing a large-scale PV deployment with a mix of new RE capacity in various stages of development. This figure is more than double the country’s current installed RE capacity.

31\. In this context, there is a legitimate concern that a programme focused on customer-level DERs, particularly rooftop PV installations and efficiency, may draw attention and capital away from other forms of RE deployment, such as large-scale wind or solar farms. Moreover, there is a risk that the most financially viable customers – those with creditworthy profiles and access to financing – will be more likely to participate in RE via DERs potentially undermining the financial case for centralized RE investments and straining utility revenue streams.

32\. When asked about this, the AE acknowledged that many of the utility-scale RE plans articulated by national governments are aspirational and may not materialize due to permitting, financing or other implementation challenges. The RIS Programme is designed as a more grounded pathway to climate action with greater ease of implementation. While they may be pragmatic in terms of the immediate benefits for customers, it remains unclear whether such decentralized planning efforts, and whether the programme might inadvertently exacerbate systemic tensions within the power sector, particularly around grid stability, cost recovery and equitable access.

33\. The RIS Programme does include design features intended to mitigate some of these risks, such as utility ownership of the DER deployment process and integration with national


---
**Page 137**
---

## Sustainable development potential

Scale: Medium to high

The funding proposal outlines a range of expected sustainable development co-benefits, many of which are plausible and well-integrated into the IIS Programme core design. These span environmental (e.g., SDG13 climate action), social (e.g., SDG5 gender equality; SDG8 decent work and economic growth; SDG9 industry, innovation and infrastructure), and economic (e.g., SDG7 affordable and clean energy) dimensions.

### Environmental co-benefits

- **Renewable energy deployment:** The IIS Programme aims to support a shift away from fossil fuel-based generation through the deployment of distributed RE and energy efficiency technologies.
- **Under utility-scale RE installations, the proposed subprojects—rooftop PV, efficient cooling and lighting, and solar water heaters—are small-scale and remodified in existing structures.

### Social co-benefits

- **Gender equality:** SDG5 gender equality
- **Decent work and economic growth:** SDG8 decent work and economic growth
- **Industry, innovation, and infrastructure:** SDG9 industry, innovation and infrastructure

### Economic co-benefits

- **Affordable and clean energy:** SDG7 affordable and clean energy


---
**Page 138**
---

### Economic co-benefits

The HIS Programme presents a credible pathway to delivering financial benefits to both utilities and consumers. On the customer side, the estimated net present value of savings is US$10 billion over 25 years, which is equivalent to the IRE's integrated financial model. These savings could be reinvested by households and MSMEs or used to reduce debt. The utility sector will benefit from a reduction in the cost of revenue streams and the opportunity to develop service-based business lines that may prove more resilient than the face of structural change, such as electricity privatisation.

### Social co-benefits

The HIS Programme aims to improve household-level access to clean energy through the provision of affordable, reliable, and sustainable energy services. This feature is particularly relevant in the Caribbean, where access to commercial credit is limited and many households are unable to afford the upfront costs associated with small businesses, or are effectively excluded from the clean energy transition. By anchoring repayment in existing utility relationships, the programme removes the need for traditional collateral or credit history, potentially increasing access.

### Health-related co-benefits

Albeit indirectly, health co-benefits are also likely from reduced diesel usage which may lower particulate matter and other pollutants, with attendant benefits to respiratory and cardiovascular health. The HIS Programme will provide targeted support for low-income households in the form of free energy audits, which could enhance equity and social inclusion.

### Gender equality and social inclusion

The proposal is supported by a detailed Gender Action Plan and gender assessment and includes gender-disaggregated indicators in its results framework. The HIS Programme aims to improve access to clean energy services for women and girls, including through the provision of separate provisions. However, as with other co-benefits, their impact will depend on the depth and consistency of implementation. The plan to monitor progress towards these objectives is ambitious but feasible; however, although this data should be regularly reported and independently verified.

### Conclusion

The proposal integrates credible pathways to social inclusion, local economic benefits, and environmental sustainability, with a strong focus on gender equality and equity considerations. The inclusion of customer-facing support tools and technical assistance for marginalised groups will help ensure the long-term sustainability and quality of uptake; the programme design creates a strong enabling framework for their realisation.

### ITAP assessment

The ITAP assesses the sustainable development potential of the HIS Programme as medium to high.


---
**Page 139**
---

1.5 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

5a. Barbados, Belize and Jamaica, the participating countries, face well-documented climate and development challenges that align with the GCF prioritization of SIDS and climate-vulnerable developing countries. These regions experience frequent extreme weather events, sea level rise, heat stress, and disruptions to key economic sectors like tourism and agriculture. The reliance on fossil fuels for energy generation is high, as are imports of solid fuels, with limited local capacity to self-finance the transition to more resilient and sustainable systems.

5b. Despite being classified as middle-income countries, this status belies substantial underlying vulnerabilities. High debt burdens and restricted fiscal space limit public investment capacity in these countries. The lack of access to financing for the deployment of distributed energy technologies. The HIS Programme directly addresses these structural limitations by providing concessional funding at scale. Without such financial support from national governments and utilities would not otherwise be able to mobilize or scale this. This is particularly relevant given that many of these countries are still in the early stages of their climate contributions, which include ambitious emissions reduction and energy access goals but often lack credible, funded implementation pathways.

5c. In addition, there exist significant persistent equity gaps within these countries. Despite DER adoption having accelerated among higher-income customers, lower-income households have been left behind due to affordability constraints and limited access to critical shocks, have been largely excluded from participation in the energy transition. The programme's inclusive on-lot financing model, combined with free energy audits for lower-income households, aims to address these equity gaps by providing financial assistance while maintaining the existing utility-customer relationship. The model bypasses traditional credit-based lending channels and instead relies on a combination of government subsidies and concessional loans.

5d. The HIS Programme also integrates a social inclusion and gender framework. Through the Gender Action Plan and associated training and outreach, its aim is to improve governance, participation in energy planning processes, and access to clean energy services for women and towards groups that have been traditionally underserved, such as women, persons with disabilities, and low-income households. The programme's focus on social inclusion and implementation of these measures will require close monitoring the aim to position these groups as active participants rather than just passive recipients is well aligned with the GCF emphasis on a just and inclusive transition.

5e. The TAP notes that the geographic targeting is limited to three countries in this first phase, which may limit its ability to capture the full range of climate vulnerabilities. However, the selection of countries is appropriate given both their acute vulnerability and the potential for scaling up lessons learned from these initial pilots. The programme's design reflects an awareness of the constraints faced by the governments and utilities in launching new delivery models without external support particularly in a high-risk resource-limited context.

5f. The programme responds directly to structural vulnerabilities in climate exposure, fiscal capacity, and access to finance. By providing concessional financing for energy efficiency projects limited in geographic scope and initial coverage, the model is appropriately targeted and well-aligned with GCF priorities for SIDS and inclusive development. The success of the programme will depend on its ability to demonstrate results and impact and support institutional capacity-building that persists beyond the life of the programme.

5g. The TAP assesses the needs of the recipient as high.


1.6 Country ownership

Scale: High


---
**Page 140**
---

### Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: Medium to high**

The IIF Programme presents a reasonably strong case in terms of cost-efficiency, alignment of financial instruments, and structuring of concessionality. This is particularly notable given that many climate mitigation and adaptation projects often entail higher marginal costs than in continental or higher-density settings.

### Cost-effectiveness

The programme is expected to achieve mitigation benefits of approximately 601,550 t CO₂ eq avoided, resulting in an estimated cost of USD 70 per tonne for carbon dioxide equivalent.


---
**Page 141**
---

The overall programme and USD 41 per tonne for the GCF contribution. These figures are broadly consistent with GCF precedents for distributed energy interventions in SIDS contexts and are deemed competitive when compared to mitigation programmes with similar technical profiles.

On the adaptation side, the estimated cost per direct beneficiary is approximately USD 3,938, based on the 10,655 direct beneficiaries targeted through improved resilience to heat stress. While this figure is higher than the GCF’s usual range of USD 2-4 per tonne, it reflects the broader context of the dual objectives of the programme. The costs are associated not only with direct energy efficiency measures but also with capacity building for utilities, digital tools and customer-facing systems expected to deliver benefits beyond the initial implementation window.

Importantly, this programme also estimates over USD 260 million in financial savings for participating customers over the productive lifespan of the financial technologies. These net benefits are estimated at approximately USD 15 per tonne of CO2 avoided, reflecting energy savings from energy-efficient appliances and systems. This represents a meaningful value proposition for both customers and the utilities, although, as CDB acknowledges, actual results will depend on customer engagement with the new technologies.

The concessionality structure and co-financing: The financial structure involves a USD 208 million credit facility provided by the IFC to the AME utility companies. Participating utilities will lend these funds to end-users through structured credit lines, with interest rates anticipated to fall in the range of 4.5 to 6 per cent, depending on local regulatory determinations and market conditions. The terms are expected to be longer than standard terms compared favourably to prevailing market rates in the region, which typically begin at 6.5 per cent or more.

In-kind contributions of USD 25 million from the utilities in the form of AME are also included in the co-financing total. While CDB maintains that AME is a critical enabler of the IHS model and its associated risk mitigation, it notes that these contributions have been made independently of the IHS Programme and appears to reflect business-as-usual practices for AME. The inclusion of these contributions may overstate the degree of additional and direct financial commitment from national stakeholders.

The IHS Programme is nonetheless likely to catalyse further investment downstream. It is framed explicitly as a “bridge to bankability,” designed to establish proof of concept and performance outcomes, with the aim of demonstrating that AME can be leveraged to secure additional finance with less concessionality in future cycles. This ambition is credible, particularly given the strong track record of AME in the region and its experience on replication, but it remains contingent on execution quality and early success.

Risk allocation and incentive alignment: CDB will act as both lender and programme manager for the IHS Programme. The GCF will provide USD 10 million in grant-based technical assistance, which comprises over 3.5 per cent of the total GCF contribution. While this represents a relatively small share of the overall investment, it is significant relative to other projects while GCF bears a disproportionate share of de-risking; the AIF has provided a clear rationale. It argues that the technical assistance is essential to establish a novel delivery model in the Caribbean, where such models are not yet well established and there is limited experience with replication. Furthermore, grant-financed activities include compliance with GCF safeguards, social inclusion outreach and knowledge dissemination, all of which extend beyond direct support to the utilities.

That said, the IIFM would have preferred to see a more differentiated risk structure, such as a dedicated credit line for AME or a separate facility for the IHS Programme with performance outcomes. The current part-pool structure may be acceptable, but the rationale must be clearly articulated and communicated to all stakeholders involved in the project. While CDB emphasizes that its own lending is not commercially priced and is adjusted to reflect


---
**Page 142**
---

Its borrowing window, the absence of a more risk-sensitive capital stack reduces the strength of the increasing alignment framework.

To mitigate the risk of utilities over-capturing the benefits of concessional financing, CDB has indicated that spreads charged to end users will be capped in the subsidiary agreement. This is intended to ensure that the utility does not capture all of the benefit during the programme's first year, with CDB monitoring subsidy terms through regular reporting and review. The programme also includes provisions for ongoing compliance with sustained regulatory engagement and transparent monitoring of on-lending terms and customer-level impact.

Customer uptake and delivery risk: A key determinant of programme success will be the ability to generate sufficient customer uptake. In other IIS contexts, customers have rejected offers when they are not certain that they can afford them or when there is a lack of timely repayment, or when participation processes were too complex. The programme attempts to address these challenges by providing:

(a) Combined metering and billing solutions that combine costs and benefits in a single, transparent billing statement.

(b) User-facing tools, including energy savings calculators and online portals, to demystify technical information, maintenance and financial implications.

(c) Technical assistance for low-income customers, including free energy audits and help desk support; and

(d) Prepayment meter processes and performance standards for Technology Service Providers (TSPs) to ensure service quality.

While these are thoughtful features, the multi-stakeholder structure and regulatory dependence on a single utility may be critical to any effective implementation. Customer satisfaction and referral efforts will be critical to achieving scale, particularly given the relatively high cost of prepayment meters in this context. The programme includes provisions to delay or suspend further technical assistance disbursements if uptake stalls—an important accountability mechanism that improves revenue discipline.

Customer uptake risk: The fact that the programme does not appear to be meaningfully coordinated with other major climate finance initiatives in the region, including those from multilateral development banks and bilateral donors, is a concern. If these initiatives are complementary and focus on more traditional, bank-led financing models, it would have been helpful for CDB to coordinate with them. Working with the IIS programme alongside existing initiatives could help avoid duplication of market distortion, especially in relatively small markets such as Barbados.

Accredited energy fit: The All-of-6% cut is within the range of comparable programmes, but the high cost of prepayment meters and other upfront costs associated with a large technical assistance component. CDB justifies this fee through a bottom-up costing model and points to higher performance metrics. While these factors are valid, the IIS programme's relatively high cost of core services should be carefully considered and assessed alongside actual disbursement and performance metrics over time.

The programme is well-structured and incorporates several safeguards to promote cost-effectiveness, manage concurrency and enforce performance-based-disbursement. However, questions remain around the depth of national engagement with the programme and the ability of CDB to ensure that it provides more clarity on coordination with parallel financing mechanisms. The programme is well-structured to enable a phased approach to disbursements, but clear communication and oversight will be critical to realising its full potential.

The area we would have preferred to see more ambition includes the feasibility of introducing an additional tranche into the funding proposal, such as a subordinated tranche for CBF financing. This would enhance incentive alignment by ensuring that CDB retains greater exposure to performance outcomes. If the post-purchase is


---
**Page 143**
---

retained, we would encourage the Secretary to seek a more robust justification, including an assessment of whether alternative structuring options could better achieve alignment while preserving the IIS Programme financial viability and implementation timeline.

The ITAP assesses the efficiency and effectiveness potential of the IIS Programme as medium to high.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The funding proposal makes a solid attempt to address a significant climate finance need in the context of the IIS Programme. It provides a clear rationale, addresses structural financing gaps, and demonstrates potential for long-term paradigm shift.

The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# Introduction

In accordance with GCF gender policies and guidance, the gender assessment is done as a critical element of a project proposal to outline how the proposed activities meet its goal of supporting the participation of women in energy sector transformation and improving their access to and control over energy resources.

The Caribbean Development Bank (CDB) is in the process of implementing the "Scaling up the Deployment of Integrated Utility Services (IUS) to Support Energy Sector Transformation in the Caribbean" (Phase I Programme in the Caribbean, which is a Green Climate Fund (GCF)-funded project). The three countries selected for this phase are Barbados, Belize and Jamaica. The GCF has identified gender equality as one of its four cross-cutting goals (CoGs) to promote the adoption of renewable energy (RE) to facilitate energy transition and expanding the scale of energy efficiency (EE) measures.

The GCF is committed to maximizing the impact of its funding for adaptation and mitigation while ensuring environmental, social, economic and development co-benefits are advanced and a gender-sensitive approach is taken. With its updated 2020 gender policy, the GCF is committed to promoting gender equality in all its investments and operations. The policy has three main objectives which are as follows:

1. Utilise a comprehensive gender approach guided by the Institution and its network of participating agencies (PAs), national designated authorities (NDAs) and focal points to support climate change interventions and innovations.
2. Promote climate investments that advance gender equality and minimise social, gender and environmental vulnerabilities in climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts.
3. Contribute to shrinking the gender gap caused by climate change-exacerbated social, economic and environmental vulnerabilities through mainstreaming gender equality in climate action.

The gender policy's application to the IUS Project would entail:

- Gender inequality is addressed/reduced and through enhanced stakeholder engagement can generate sustainable livelihoods opportunities, promote health and wellbeing, and increase access to energy services.
- An enabling environment among CGI stakeholders built on existing policy commitments, social and environmental standards and redress mechanisms is supported and sustained in order to achieve gender equality.
- The comprehensive, systematic, and participatory collection of sex-disaggregated data and the execution of qualitative and quantitative gender analysis is supported to effectively monitor, report and evaluate project impact.


---
**Page 152**
---

The Assessment builds on the previous work in the baseline assessment and seeks to provide a deeper understanding of how the proposed technologies (solar water heaters, rooftop solar photovoltaic, efficient cooling and HVAC, LED lighting) will impact populations of the target countries as well as identify barriers to uptake.

The Assessment is intended to inform:
- the gender mainstreaming approach for the GCF proposal;
- capacity-building activities in the overall proposal; 
- gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation tools that may be needed;  
- institutional arrangements and operational actions/steps that may be needed in the short, medium and/or long-term to support the USD billion implementation in an equal, inclusive and just manner.

## 1 Limitations

This assessment uses a mixed method approach i.e. a desk-based research and stakeholder engagement. However, this process was still hindered by a few factors including:

- **Outdated data** – the region has limited human resources funding and infrastructure available to execute frequent data collection and analysis activities which results in the use of data at least 2 years old. The Caribbean Renewable Energy and Energy Efficiency (CREEE) is working with the Government for Renewable Energy and Energy Efficiency (GCREE) to narrow this gap, some of the essential data required by the assessment are still not available or are only partially available.
- **Data availability – access to data is a weak point in the Caribbean as many governments and agencies possess updated data but have issues with processing, analysing, and publishing it for public use. Access to human resources and infrastructure. Due to limited time for consultations and engagements there was little time to follow up in detail with the Statistics Departments of each country to inform this draft assessment.
- **Limited access to critical documents** – through various engagements with government stakeholders, we were able to obtain some key documents that are required to fully inform this project but despite our best efforts, we were unable to secure access to these documents for Jamaica. These include the Draft Energy Policy for Jamaica as well as the new access policy for Belize, amongst others.
- **Inaccessibility of key stakeholders – some of the executing Entities (the utilities) did not participate in any of the consultations and engagements with government stakeholders and/or the need or see a project document prior to their engagement on questions relating to gender mainstreaming. This is due to the fact that they are not aware of the importance of this assessment and the process flow for GCF proposals. Their engagement, as the proposed executing agencies, will be critical in ensuring that the Gender Assessment is integrated into the action plan that follows the Gender Assessment including technical assistance and capacity-building actions as possible elements of more than one Component of the proposed project.

As it stands therefore, the Gender Assessment reflects a few key limits and limitations which are able of being addressed by further desk-based research and stakeholder engagement on issues and behaviours on gender, gender mainstreaming, women’s empowerment and social inclusion in the sector as well as for this specific project.


## 1.2 Challenges

This assessment seeks to be informed by desk-based research and stakeholder engagement which would give insight into the enabling environment in each country by


---
**Page 153**
---

identifying the current circumstances the TUG would be operating in while also gaining knowledge on key stakeholders' capacities and practices on gender equality and social inclusion mainstreaming in energy sector activities and transformations. Executing the stakeholder engagement process was challenging due to various factors. Engaging with key stakeholders was inhibited by time conflicts, limited personnel availability, and logistical challenges such as remote or rural geographical locations. Due to the difficulty in scheduling meetings and the limited time available to conduct consultations and complete the assessment, stakeholder engagement was closed off on June 9th to facilitate the presentation of findings.

# Methodology

## Key chapter takeaway

A diverse range of stakeholders were met, yielding responses from 50/50 split of male and female voices. The insights and information gained from these engagements specifically informed current policy development in renewable energy.

Stakeholder engagement also captures the lived experiences of the stakeholder and raised awareness on issues that had not been previously considered.


## This assessment used a multi-prong approach

The first step enabled the desk-based research which involved gathering existing analysis on energy, renewable energy, energy and gender, and renewable energy transformations to obtain information that could shed light on how this project could be framed. The second step was to conduct stakeholder engagement by identifying and analysing the existing enabling environment for renewable energy. This research applied a renewable energy lens to the current socio-economic conditions within each country to understand how these factors affect renewable energy uptake, usage and appetite for renewable energy technologies.

A gender analysis rating tool was used to assess relevant policies based on how gender-responsive they were. The rating tool was developed by the Gender Analysis Working Group, using the elements that speak to gender, women, and other marginalised groups and the conventions of international human rights law. The rating tool is designed to be: (i) inclusive; (ii) inclusion; (iv) prevention/mitigation of risks/impacts. This is an important perspective in addressing the challenges associated with implementing CCF requirements for mitigation investments including those with adaptation co-benefits.


## Figure 2-1: Policy Gender Rating Tool

| Gender Rating | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Gender Inclusive (no mention) | The policy does not mention or does not reference gender or women. |
| Gender Sensitive | Some specific roles, issues and/or concerns are acknowledged within the policy. |
| Gender Responsive | Most of the relevant gender-related inequalities are not exacerbated as a by-product of the policy’s actions. |
| Gender Transformative | The policy acknowledges and identifies differences and inequities in the policy environment and sets out actions which seeks to rectify these issues. |
| Gender Policy | The policy seeks to address structural inequalities through social and cultural norms. |

The second step builds on the first step and used those findings to shape the stakeholder engagement to follow. Key stakeholders were identified, and a stakeholder engagement plan was developed. A gender analysis rating tool was also developed as part of this process in order to be used with stakeholders that help guide the conversation by providing context and key questions. The gender analysis rating tool is designed to be: (i) inclusive; (ii) inclusion; (iv) prevention/mitigation of risks/impacts. This is an important perspective in addressing the challenges associated with implementing CCF requirements for mitigation investments with diverse stakeholders sought to gain an understanding of their engagement on gender and


---
**Page 155**
---

energy and with energy activities, their capacities and experience with gender and gender mainstreaming particularly within a renewable energy project and their perspectives on issues that need consideration in the proposed IUS model approach. In addition to this, engagement with government stakeholders was also conducted to understand the existing frameworks and policies used when responding to the needs of the general public and vulnerable groups.

Executed in three phases, we consulted with twenty-eight organisations in total (13 NGOs and 09 governmental entities) and engaged a total of forty-four individuals (see Table 2-2 for list of stakeholders engaged). Phase 1 entailed engaging NGOs who worked with vulnerable groups and/or in renewable energy. In Phase 2, outreach was made to government stakeholders including the Ministry of Energy, the National Energy Board, and other relevant entities. Phase 3 was intended to target the utilities (the Executing Entities of the project) but was ultimately postponed due to time constraints. Twenty-eight (44) persons in total were consulted – 14 men and 14 women (see Figure 2-2).

# Country Level Analysis

## Key chapter takeaway

Although the three countries have their own individual cultures, economies and other aspects of society, the renewable energy sector in each country and their populations' interactions with the sector are similar. The assessment explores the gendered use and implications of the technologies that will reap numerous benefits for persons, though barriers that reduce their ability to improve lives remain.

This section explores the gendered usage and implications of the technologies of focus in this project across the three countries and identifies existing and potential barriers that would affect the uptake and adoption by these technologies by women and other marginalised communities. The assessment considers the following questions: Are there differences between men and women? If so, what are they? In what ways do they affect the daily activities of women, and can they bring increased quality of life? Will the increased distribution and adoption of these technologies support a just transition for all?

What institutional, social and economic barriers may influence the rate of uptake of these technologies by women and other marginalised communities? What will these factors affect the beneficiaries most in terms of marginalisation? How do these barriers relate to the project's TORs?

Differences based on sex and age are considered as well as other intersectional attributes such as income, geography, (dis)ability and ethnicity where relevant. IP issues are expected to be considered in the context of the specific needs for indigenous communities as they apply in Jamaica or Belize.

This section also reviews the enabling environment for gender mainstreaming in the energy sector and existing efforts to apply a gender lens to renewable energy and energy efficiency transformations. The review of existing legal, institutional, policy, strategy and planning frameworks is meant to identify gaps and opportunities for addressing barriers and constraints. Additionally, relevant international commitments and regional policies are mentioned where they relate to the project's TORs.

The findings from this section will be used in conjunction with the analysis of common and differentiated needs are summarised including any evidence or likelihood of a gender inequality of risk that target beneficiaries may be exposed to.

# 3.1 Barbados

## 3.1.1 Policy Environment

Barbados is a Caribbean leader in the energy sector. Its leadership has seen progress in the sustainability of its energy matrix in its ambitious goal of being 100% renewable by 2030. This goal has resulted in the acquisition of 49 electric buses and the installation of rooftop solar photovoltaic systems on homes.

One programme being implemented is the Barbados Reefs project as noted in the country's National Determined Contribution (NDC). The programme seeks to build resilience in the IUS Model technologies such as rooftop solar photovoltaic on homes and hopes to improve the social development of communities. This is achieved through the promotion of energy efficiency, cost deduction and improving the standard of living and quality of life.

Take, for example, the Barbados National Energy Policy which establishes the roadmap for the renewable energy sector in this country. The policy has no specific mentions of gender equality or how the different uses of energy by women would be considered and/or have influenced the development of the policy. This is a result of policies being gender neutral or simply gender-blind.

## Employment Rights Act

Of the 9 policies assessed, 8 are gender-blind; 1 is gender aware and 1: the Barbados NDC, gender responsive. Those most relevant to this country’s energy sector were all found to be gender blind. The NDC, subject to international requirements for gender mainstreaming, includes a commitment towards equal access to energy.

As noted in the 2021 NDC for Barbados, there is a commitment by the government towards “equality for all, especially as it relates to ‘gender’, as recognised in Barbados’ Constitution. The NDC also outlines how this will be achieved through the promotion of women’s economic entrepreneurship; business development to address unemployment and economic recovery; post-pandemic economic recovery; and climate change adaptation. The 2021 NDC references gender within the context of the Roots-to-Feet Program (R2FP), with a view to the positive impact on women in rural communities. In addition, the programme is also aiming to provide support to low- and medium-income households to become more resilient to climate change related events; the programme seeks to protect both private and communal roofs from the impacts of extreme weather conditions such as hurricanes and tropical storms, as well as rainwater harvesting, and nature-based solutions aimed at reducing runoff into coastal environments.*


---
**Page 163**
---

Beyond overarching national and international policies and agreements, a recently implemented policy at the Ministry level through the Ministry of Energy and Business is the Right to Renewable Energy Policy. This policy gives households, more generally those intending to use domestic renewable energy systems (e.g., solar photovoltaic or wind power systems) up to a total of 10kW without requiring a formal application and license under the Electric Light and Power Act. The policy also provides for a simplified registration process and assumed proceeds for individuals wishing to use this service. The act makes no notable mention of female-headed households or provisions for women using the service, however, the direct link between energy access and gender equality is clear. This can have implications for women in terms of household tasks, time allocation and potential cost savings depending on the return from investment in adopting renewable energy grid technologies.

### 3.1.2 Usage Patterns and Trends

Culturally, women carry out three tasks (a) as much unpaid care and domestic work as men, including child-rearing, cleaning, cooking, both unpaid and inviable. These culturally defined responsibilities are closely tied to energy and energy access. Energy access saves time and allows for some of these tasks to be carried out simultaneously with planning and preparation for other activities. The division of labor between women and men is often complicated by many women reduce the physical burden that may otherwise required for these tasks. When energy access is limited, this can lead to a greater burden on women's responsibility; these daily required activities are more likely to become a burden to women.

Men, on the other hand, carry out protective as well as economic and decision-making roles within households. These include (i) not carrying out domestic work or child-rearing tasks in a man should: (i) not do unpaid domestic work, (ii) have the final say in household decisions, (iii) control over financial resources, and (iv) be responsible for income generation.

In the Barbadian context, often times these cultural gendered roles are mixed with those of women and men. Women may carry out some of the tasks that are traditionally only a shared level of control over the acquisition of energy resources and energy access, an equal acquisition of assets is noted in the country scoring 100 in the WBL Index 2023.

### LED Lighting

Culturally defined responsibilities (CDRs) such as caretaking may lead to an increased use of LED lighting in matrilineal households. LED lighting enables women to perform these CDRs alongside other tasks, including cooking and cleaning. This is especially true for women who are working during times of inactive work (before or after work, during periods of no sunlight e.g., early morning or late evenings) or even during remote or hybrid work arrangements.

As seen in Figure 3-2, the increase in consumer price inflation exceeds the amount of household disposable income real growth in developing countries like Barbados.

This translates into an increase in the need for employment as a means of (additional) income to meet basic needs (food, clothing, shelter, essential services, access to drinking water, sanitation, etc.). This increased demand for employment can lead to higher unemployment rates. The need for employment can lead to the increase in non-traditional working hours (outside of 8 AM – 5 PM), which would, in turn, increase the demand for LED Lighting for safety and security as women (and other job seekers) walk home from school, work, or during public transportation or private vehicles.

As women generally feel less safe in a public environment than most cis-heterosexual men (especially when it is dark outside), women often adjust their schedules, considering ways of transport and daily routes with safety in mind. There is a need for better lighting on Barbadian roads, particularly at night. The Barbados Light and Power Company Limited has installed LED streetlights in partnership with the Government of Barbados and the Barbados Light and Power Company Limited. These LED streetlights are not only improving access to public transportation at non-traditional times of work but also provide a certificate of safety for women, especially those who are members of the disabled community to traverse during these non-traditional working hours.

Men share some similarities in LED Lighting usage when compared to women*. The 2010 Census data shows that men are more likely than women to walk home from school or work at night. Similar safety concerns are a factor in the provision of LED Lighting, which journeying after daylight hours. Road safety is also an area of need for the usage of LED Lighting by men, with this also reporting an upward trend in road fatalities (before and during COVID-19 pandemic)**


---
**Page 165**
---

which men are often the victims. However, the use of LED lighting is far more expanded than just the use of road fatalities.

## Solar Water Heating

The island is commonly applauded for its use of solar water heating in households and businesses, with over 50,000 solar water heaters (SWH). SWH technology combines the use of energy from the sun to heat water. The SWH is used within households for bathing, cleaning, cooking, and washing. Outside of the benefits hot water has for CFS, it also provides a number of other health benefits such as reducing the amount of soaps such as dirt and grease and aid in the provision of safe and nutritious meals for the household, the use of SWH is also beneficial to the environment.

The aging population (60+) in Barbados amounts to 18% of the population, according to the 2010 Census. This group within the population remains vulnerable to illnesses such as diabetes, hypertension, and osteoarthritis. The use of solar water heating for bathing has health benefits for the island's seniors giving them the added ability to combat low-grade non-chronic inflammation, reduce blood sugar, better circulation and soothing of the muscles and joints; better sleep patterns, and improved mood. According to a study by Hoekstra et al in 2013, Hoekstra et al noted within their study that, observations of a lowering in fasting glycated hemoglobin (HbA1c) levels following SWH use in individuals with type 2 diabetes and osteoarthritis, with type 2 diabetes support the notion of improved insulin sensitivity following HWH [33]. The mechanisms underlying this beneficial effect are not yet fully understood. However, it relates to blood sugar, Hoekstra et al noted that the lowering of fasting glycated hemoglobin (HbA1c) levels following SWH use and blood glucose concentrations following HWH in individuals with type 2 diabetes support the notion of improved insulin sensitivity following HWH [33], the mechanisms that underlie this beneficial effect are currently unclear.

These benefits can allow the elderly who have fixed incomes to continue to support positive health outcomes. The ability for seniors to continue to enjoy a healthy diet, which is always, in turn, allow for further investments in their diet (particularly, healthy food consumption) which can be costly to these individuals as the region experiences the highest cost of a healthy diet when compared with other regions.

## Rooftop Solar Photovoltaic

Rooftop solar photovoltaics remain an access problem across genders, because of the economic barriers for a roof-top installation. The island has no specific law (See Economic Barrier Section) yet as well as physical barriers due to rooftop types. With no specific data on how rooftop solar photovoltaic may be used differently across vulnerable groups within the country, it is generally presented as a cost-saving benefit to local households across the island.

---

The installation of a rooftop solar PV system provides collective savings to Barbadian users, over USD 2.5 million a year on their electricity bills (equivalent to 9,000 barrels of oil or over 2,000 tonnes of CO₂ emissions). As women reinvest 90% of their income in their families and communities, the benefits are far-reaching. The solar PV system also expands the scope of investment in other forms and more complex RE, EE and DER technologies.

### Efficient Cooling and Refrigeration

The island has two weather seasons due to its geographic location. These include the dry season from December to May and the wet season from June to November. Increasing rates of climate change have caused intensified storms and extreme heat during dry periods[25], but the use of air conditioning is increasing in response to these conditions, particularly in hotels. During these high dry periods, temperatures can be high temperatures with the Barbados Met Office predicting temperatures as low as 30 degrees Celsius in September 2023.[26] Its extreme heat causes increased need for heating and cooling.

Figure 3-3: Estimate Annual Energy Consumption of Room Air Conditioners in 2040. Source: United for Efficiency[27]

| | Business as Usual | Minimum Ambition | High Ambition |
|---|---|---|---|
| ANNUAL ENERGY CONSUMPTION FROM ROOM AIR CONDITIONERS IN 2040 (GWh) | 800 | 412 | 369 |

Under three scenarios, the UNEP United for Efficiency programme estimates that room air conditioners will consume 600 GWh with business as usual, 472 GWh with minimum ambition and 359 GWh in high ambition. The energy consumption is expected to increase by 18% if the technology improves at 11% per year, further increasing market share of variable speed models is anticipated, resulting in an additional 2% per year until new sales are entirely from that product type (see Figure 3-3).


---
**Page 167**
---

The usage of technologies such as efficient air conditioning and refrigeration can aid in women’s cooking responsibilities and within their natural agency journey including menopause. The use of an efficient refrigerator, for example, allows women to better balance their CDRs and job responsibilities. Women with menopause are more likely than other women to experience problems with these activities. At home, efficient air conditioning and cooling also stand to benefit 28% to 63% of middle-aged and older women in the United States from heat exhaustion, dehydration, or night sweats. This technology allows women to get a good night’s sleep at home, which is key to physical and mental health. Within the workforce, air conditioning can aid in the management of job burnout and stress. These technologies also provide comfort for women with menopause, allowing women with fluctuation, self-esteem, identity, and social needs.

With benefits from using efficient air conditioning and refrigeration during menopause (which traditionally occurs between 45-50 years), the use of these technologies creates an important demand cohort of over 20,000 thousand women within the age range of 45 to 59 years old based on the 2010 Census, that may not currently be considered in terms of energy use and demand.

### 3.1.3 Demand

The demand for energy in Barbados over the last 10 years is characterized by a declining trend, with an average annual growth rate for primary and secondary energy estimated at 1.297 BGE per day. This trend has been partially attributed to the improvement of household energy efficiency through the usage of IUS Model technologies like SWH and rooftop solar PV as seen in Figure 3-4.

**Figure 3-4: Household Energy Efficiency Trends**

| Year | Residential consumption (per unit of private consumption) |
| --- | --- |
| 2008 | 1.579 BGE per day |
| 2009 | 1.567 BGE per day |
| 2010 | 1.543 BGE per day |
| 2011 | 1.521 BGE per day |
| 2012 | 1.508 BGE per day |
| 2013 | 1.496 BGE per day |
| 2014 | 1.484 BGE per day |
| 2015 | 1.472 BGE per day |

At the macro level, demand for IUS Model technologies is influenced by the Government of Barbados’ efforts to reduce the import fuel bill to improve the country’s energy security and stabilize its economy. The government has set two main goals: (1) increase the share of electricity supplied by renewable sources by 2030 and (2) reduced consumption of electricity by 22% by 2028. Though there is no data measuring the degree to which barriers noted in the Barrier Section


---
**Page 168**
---

this report have influenced demand, it can be noted that barriers noted in the section below have impacted macro demand to some degree.

Notably, the island is also prone to natural hazards such as hurricanes. Using a mix of RE, EE and DER technologies can offer resilience, particularly through self-efficiency. For example, the Road to Resilience project in Barbados has demonstrated that the installation of rooftop solar PV systems can provide electricity independent of the grid, which provides households with energy security during power outages or other natural disasters*. With these capabilities, households can maintain a source of energy that may be required for the care of the members of the disabled community and enable key functions such as lighting and communications to reliable sources of power in a post-disaster scenario.

### 3.1.4 Barriers

This small island developing state (SIDS) has been taking steps in reducing its dependence on fossil fuels, with the government declaring that by 2030 all electricity generation will be from formal declaration to be 100% renewable, the Government of Barbados, and is also the local private sector e.g., Barbados Light and Power Limited), bilateral and multilateral partners (e.g., Inter-American Development Bank, World Bank, etc.), and civil society organizations. The goal is to increase the renewable energy and reducing the country's reliance on fossil fuel generated electricity.

These barriers range from gaps in legislation addressing inefficient air conditioning to social and cultural factors that prevent women from participating fully in the renewable energy sector and should be addressed to promote an equitable and just transition in this goal.


Institutional

The island has reduced institutional barriers in the uptake of the target technologies of the IUS project. However, most part. Legislation and policies have provided some reduced barriers for solar water heating LED Lighting, and solar rooftop photovoltaic; as the Government of Barbados (2019) and the Electric Power Act (Amendment) 2019 and the Household Right to Renewable Energy* with an online application process for households to apply for a grant. The grant is based on the size of the household, which takes into account roof dimensions, the type and size of the renewable energy system and PV Diagrams*. - improved access to financing and technical assistance for the installation of rooftop solar systems Grid. This process, however, brings about a CDRS-related barrier linked to gendered stereotypes (See Social Barrier Section). Specifically in reference to the skillset required for the installation of rooftop solar systems, there is a significant gender gap in Barbados. According to the Barbados Labour Survey 2012 where there are 10.9 thousand men within the construction, mining and quarrying sector compared with only 6.4 thousand women, enabling men to benefit more from this barrier than women.

---
**Page 169**
---

Another barrier at the institutional level is the absence of legislation requiring landlords to implement IUS Model technologies such as solar water heating and rooftop solar on their properties. Currently, as noted from stakeholder engaged as part of this assessment, renters who move out of a rental property may not be aware that they are responsible for renter air conditioning can be blocked by the landlord from installation and usage. As of 2010, the Barbados National Housing Corporation (BNHC) had received over three thousand applications for rental accommodations during a seven-year period. Such legislation would require conversations between stakeholders such as the BNHC, private landlords, tenants, and the government to determine best practices accounting for any unforeseen cost, such as water damage.

In addition, the absence of legislation governing the use of inefficient air conditioning and refrigeration sector is also a barrier, as noted by the Barbados Renewable Energy Association (BREA). The lack of enforcement of existing laws on energy efficiency in buildings can create a barrier. These barriers currently enable the use of air conditioning systems which are consumed at high levels of electricity. This situation could be exacerbated by climate change and lack of enforcement could hinder the adaptation of the IUS Model and reduce mitigation efforts for GHGs with the system using more energy than it should.

Notably, the Government of Barbados has been working to implement projects that improve access and investment incentives within the island. Two of these actions and projects include the Public Sector Smart Energy Programme (PSSPE) and the Energy SMART Fund. These projects implemented with or by the Government of Barbados which can aid in the transition to and within the IUS Model.

The introduction of Electric Mobility into Public Transportation Project is one such example. This project targets rides with air-conditioning, w.r.t., and wheelchair lifts, all of which the old diesel buses lack. There are now 49 electric buses, with about a dozen on the way, and the government has committed to having an additional 100 electric buses by 2035. This will aid in reducing reliance on the transport sector (which is responsible for 33% of energy consumption) while improving road accessibility in the island's transportation system for members of the disabled community.

The Home Ownership Providing Energy Project (HOPE) Project seeks to protect households with a gross net income of around USD 2,500 (BBD 5048) per month** however, the most vulnerable are those who are more vulnerable to poverty and those in poverty. Vulnerability is defined as the inability to cope with shocks or stressors, such as the shortfall required to be a part of the HOPE Project. Concern for young people, in this context, arises from their lack of economic capacity – economic and financial – due to unemployment or underemployment.

More generalised vulnerability is further exacerbated by post-disaster shocks such as rapid increases in prices, loss of income, and increased exposure to damage and loss of homes. An example is the passing of Hurricane Elsa in 2021, with reports indicating 1,335 roofs were damaged, 126 other houses damaged (otherwise), and 145 houses collapsed.

Economic

Due to its geo-political position i.e., Latin America and the Caribbean, Barbados like other Caribbean SIDS has been identified as being at risk to money laundering and the use of funding for anti-terrorist purposes. This is further compounded by the fact that many banks in the region are Canadian origin with some of these preconditions built into their lending criteria. The lack of a national credit bureau also means that there is no record keeping for the installation and usage of all technologies under the IUS model, as an account will most likely be required at a recognised financial institution.

This project seeks to support bank support further intensifies when there is no law mandating equal remuneration for work of equal value. With no law, employers can discriminate based on gender within the workforce, which may lead to women being paid less. This is noted in Figure 48 **HOPE Programme** retrieved from https://www.barbados.gov.bb/programmes/hope-programme-2019-2023.pdf

This provides difficulty in having the required income level required to be considered for a loan from these financial institutions as was confirmed within stakeholder engagements.

Although LED Lighting is generally affordable, with the bulb costing approximately USD 4.42 to USD 17.70 at one of the main hardware stores on the island and streetlighting remaining a public responsibility, the cost of solar water heating systems and air conditioning units are prohibitive for most Barbadians. The average monthly income in Barbados is more of a middle-class phenomenon. Persons within this economic and social class usually earn between USD 100 to USD 250 per week. This group includes the 3,800 women who earn within their homes. A 7.8 liters solar water heating system costs USD 2,000 (BDS $6,000),[4] a rooftop solar panel system costing between USD 10,000 to USD 15,000,[5] and a 12,000 BTU air conditioning unit costing at about USD 546 (BDS 1,600). [6]

This high cost of energy is more likely to serve as a barrier to uptake for low-income women and women who are in the informal sector. These groups include the 32,800 women who earn between USD 100 to USD 250 per week, as well as vulnerable households within the low-income category of USD 100 to USD 250 a week. These are the realities that make it difficult for women to escape from extreme poverty and the 24,800 women whose earnings fall below USD 100 (BDS $300) to USD 250 (BDS $500). [7]

Though financing through commercial banks is an option, the interest rates and the biases towards stable income sources when granting loans act as a determinant to financing outside of credit unions. The majority of women in Barbados are members of credit unions or other women-based organisational structure allows members to have a greater say in its operations.

It is known that commercial banks tend to favour stability, and secure employment for loans, excluding of having less favourable to the 3,800 female entrepreneurs on the island. [8] These

# Alternative or Programme

## Credit Union Loans

### Summary
These loans under various names such as "Barbados Green Energy Loan" provide lower interest rates than commercial banks and offer credit union financing for these loans.

### Associated Partner
City of Bridgetown, Credit Union Limited

### Targeted Technologies
- Roofing Solar Photovoltaic, Efficient Cooling and Refrigeration, LED Lighting, Solar Water Heating*

## Hire Purchase or Long-Term Financing

### Summary
This allows customers to hire equipment on short-term contracts with the payments for these.

### Associated Partner
Solar Dynamics Ltd., Barbados Unicom (Barbados Courts)

### Targeted Technologies
- Solar Water Heating and Efficient Cooling and Refrigeration

## Home Ownership Energy (HOPE) Inc

### Summary
This provides land to construct homes at no cost to the potential home owner. The house construction is paid for by a landowner with HOPE Inc. mortgage loan.

### Associated Partner
Government of Barbados Private Sector

### Targeted Technologies
- Roofing Solar Photovoltaic Systems

## Community Projects

### Summary
This consists of up to 15 persons to work together on a community energy project (and utilizes the government tariff at 10% premium).

### Associated Partner
Government of Barbados

### Targeted Technologies
- Solar Photovoltaic Systems

## Energy Cooperative

### Summary
Energy cooperatives through grouped investment and learning barriers remain relatively low, with the application fee of USD.

### Associated Partner
Barbados Sustainable Energy Cooperative Society Ltd

### Targeted Technologies
- Solar Photovoltaic Systems

These alternatives help to reduce the cost of investing and the purchase of these technologies, however, all these technologies within the IUS Model require the correct roof structure as was noted in the previous section. The social and economic side of the adaptation of technologies for the IUS Model remain biased towards a specific group.

Heavy economic barriers affect just a transition and have been reinforced by key stakeholders' consultations with the Bureau of Gender Affairs – Barbados, the Business and Women's Professional Association (BWPA) and the Ministry of Labour and Social Security (MOLSS) – Energy Division. Today, a large portion of Barbados’ energy transition remains in the hands of those who are not low-income earners.

Social

Education allows people to emerge from poverty, reduces inequalities and creates a climate of peace and tolerance in each society. Education remains subsidised by the Government of Barbados for students up to the age of 18 years old. However, tertiary education is not free, or fields of study that may require some cost inputs from the student. This subsidized cost enables vulnerable communities to have a greater access education up to tertiary level. However, with limited resources, approximately 40% of young females and males in Barbados experience some level of opportunity cost should they opt to pursue any form of tertiary level education. A woman facing poverty might be forced to choose between having children or deferring payment.

Another factor of consideration is the volatility of the industry, women are mostly employed in. Take for example, the accommodation and food services industry which employ the second highest number of women after wholesale and retail relating to 9,003 thousand women, exposed

to the external shocks of the global economy, for example, the COVID-19 pandemic.™ This volatility increases financial stress for women, particularly those in maternal households to provide for their dependents in down seasons of the industry.

Gender stereotypes are a barrier to the uptake of these technologies and women continue to be under-represented within the sector (see Figures 3.9) and enrolment into courses and programmes for these technologies are under-enrolled by young women and women (as noted within Stakeholder Engagements).

Gender stereotypes also act as barriers in terms of access to technical areas related to these technologies seeing 1,500 men within the electric; gas, steam, water, and air conditioning supply in comparison with 1,000 of the 2022 Labour Force Survey (DFS). Moreover, they limit women’s employment opportunities and their ability to participate in decision-making processes.™ These stereotypes act as a major barrier for women being able to benefit from the employment opportunities that are created by the adoption and just transition of these technologies. This reduces the ability for a just transition within this sector, as men continue to disproportionately be also employed in these areas.

Women in matrilineal households can find themselves dependent on men for the maintenance and upkeep of these technologies. This could lead to an increase in gender-based violence (GBV) and domestic abuse.™ The cost of maintaining these technologies at home is out of household takes on more financial stress by purchasing these relatively high upfront costs for RE technologies, which are often not affordable for women.™ In addition, the IUS Model highlighted amongst several men that thought women’s lack of economic power made them vulnerable to abuse.

The use and demand for technologies under the IUS model, i.e., solar water heating, rooftop solar photovoltaic, efficient cooling, and refrigeration, remain differentiated by gender due to gender-based roles as caretakers of households.™ However, in terms of models for the technologies and the policies of the Government of Barbados to barriers these technologies, there are some gaps in legislation, high economic barriers and gender stereotyping that need to be addressed before women will benefit from these technologies.

Some of these gaps illustrate the following key issues within the adoption and just transition for the IUS Model:

- Indirect gender inequality within loan accessibility for women, persons with disabilities and other vulnerable groups with a low income.
- The absence of gender-responsive and transformative policies and capacity strengthening in policy development and implementation.
- Strong gender stereotyping affects women’s participation at technical levels within the energy sector.
- Subsidies for the installation of these technologies, i.e., solar water heating and rooftop solar photovoltaic.

**Page 176**
---

The unclear relationship between a renter and the landowner, with the lack of legislation. The rights both landlord and tenant is needed to be addressed within legislation.

To ensure a just and fair transition in adapting these technologies, there is a need for the strengthening of gender mainstreaming capacity at all levels of government. Strengthening the organisational and institutional gender mainstreaming capacities. Educational and economic empowerment is also needed for women to take up these capacity-building and decision-making level roles. Women should be encouraged to participate fully in the planning, implementation, and responsibilities such as cantalking, better-improving participation, and engagement of women within the energy sector.

### 3.1.5 Institutional Gender Mainstreaming Capacity

Institutionally gender mainstreaming capacity remains limited. The main organisation/body with established gender mainstreaming capacity the Bureau of Gender Affairs in Barbados, has been fairly successful in implementing gender mainstreaming through its own internal structures (IGS) and through non-governmental organisation (NGO) partnerships. Presently, the two ways of boosting institutional capacity Bureau has determined a member of an NGO being hired as a consultant for the Bureau.

Similarly, most stakeholders also highlighted the use of gender mainstreaming to strengthen their gender mainstreaming capacity. However, limited extra capacity. This is another area of concern, particularly in achieving an equitable and just transition of the IUS Model as gender mainstreaming capacity remains limited. The lack of capacity has been a barrier for women and other vulnerable groups to go beyond their traditional roles and work as job-creators, employees, entrepreneurs, and decision-makers within the energy sector. Such support at this time enables equal participation and equal distribution of benefits of renewable and available groups would see the reduction in poverty especially the feminisation of poverty from unequal access.

# Belize

Belize, a small developing country, is highly vulnerable to the effects of climate change. Adapting to and mitigating the impacts of climate change will require large expansive financial investments at the national level. The country's gross domestic product (GDP) of $6.7 billion USD, noting recovery from the financial impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic but decreased by 2% in 2020 compared with a growth rate of 3.5% in 2019. This has implications for the capacity and ability of the population to adapt to climate change. The cost of living is rising, particularly for food and other essentials will rise. This means also that persons will have tough decisions to make as they balance their needs with their income. Persons who live in urban areas are better off than those in households, particularly those in rural communities, will be hit harder by the rising cost of living due to climate change.

In 2021, the Belize Statistical Office released the findings of a poverty study it undertook in 2018 and 2019. The study notes that there was an increase from 35% (in 2016) to 47% (in 2018) in the number of persons living below the poverty line.

The study notes that 9% of the population is critically poor and 38% are moderately poor. The study also notes that indigenous ethnic groups saw the highest rates of poverty at 78%, and 52% respectively and these were followed by Afro-Caribbean (46%) and Mestizo (40%). The six districts with the lowest electricity connection rates as seen in Figure 3-11.

Toledo possessed the lowest poverty rate at 52% while the remaining districts range from 45-57%. The study notes that persons living below the poverty line are more likely to be female and live in households where the head of household has no formal education, a low level of income, and poor access to electricity. Persons with no secondary education have a 12% rate.

In 2020, 97.1% of Belizeans had access to electricity which indicates significant work being done by the government to provide all Belizean with reliable access to electricity because as seen in the UNICEF 2015 Multi-Indicator Cluster Survey (MIC3), only 91.8% of the population had access to electricity.

As seen in Figure 3-11, of the six districts Toledo district had the lowest rate of 70.8% in 2016.

# Policy Environment

Energy policies in Belize have not possessed strong content that spoke to empowering women in the sector or implementing measures to address their needs; however, mention is made of health risks faced by women with the usage of wood for cooking at the same time, women's low participation in decision-making and leadership roles (Figure 3-12). The revised policy seeks to expand opportunities for all and could serve as a good starting point when initiating any revisions or policy development within the rural electrification sector.

| Nation Energy Policy 2011 | It outlines the policy needs to develop-and implement a national plan for sustainable energy supply. | The policy notes the lack of education and promotion on renewable energy among the public. It raises awareness about the benefits of trained women in the energy sector. It also notes that gender-balanced training is | Neutral |
