Seven policies were analyzed: those most directly relevant to the energy sector and including the National Gender Policy (all are summarized in Figure 12). Of the 7, 2 are transformative, 1 responsive and transformative, 1 responsive, 2 aware and one blind. The existence of the Gender Policy is a positive development for gender equality and inclusion across economic and social dimensions for a just transition in the context of the IUS model implementation in Belize.

The updated NDC* of 2021 for Belize was developed using a gender and vulnerable group analysis approach. This document outlines the country’s climate change mitigation strategies, long-term and long-term strategies that exist within the commitments and activities outlined within the public sector. The NDC targets and actions in the document were developed with the participation of the public sector, civil society organizations, indigenous peoples, and other stakeholders through a gender-responsive manner. Actions and targets in the updated NDC were submitted after undergoing a gender analysis to ensure that they are inclusive of women’s needs and interests as well as strategies for improved gender sensitivity in NDC implementation and the longer-term low-emissions strategy. While there is no direct reference to women in the document, it does include references to women in each of the targets outlined for the agroforestry sector. The updated NDC also includes a target that focuses on reducing GHG emissions through alternate production systems.

In terms of consideration for indigenous persons within the actions of the NDC beyond consultations, specific references to indigenous groups include promoting the monitoring and stewardship of natural resources in accordance with traditional practices and knowledge, and better managed toward the goal of producing net carbon sink areas in over 10,000 hectares of indigenous land. The document also notes the development of area-specific strategies for adaptation and mitigation that consider climate change impacts on water availability, including enhancing policies and frameworks that consider (climate and water) sensitive development and urban planning. The document also notes the need to promote the continuation of current practices in land stewardship that exist in indigenous communities. The document also notes the need to ensure that the NDC is balanced and inclusive in terms of climate finance. These efforts are supported by climate finance from the Global Environment Facility.

Belize’s Revised National Gender Policy*, updated in 2013, is focused on building upon and continuing the country's efforts to achieve and sustain gender equality and equity. It includes objectives for achieving gender equality and equity through the promotion of the full participation of men and women, with equal co-benefits being distributed. The policy also includes the objective of promoting the full participation of men and women in decision-making processes, services, and opportunities between men and women. The revised national gender policy is based on the vision, whereby there exists:

“A society in which all men and women, boys and girls are able to achieve their full potential through equal access to resources and rights; live together in mutual respect, dignity and harmony; and are equal partners as they participate in services and resources for realizing and sustaining their economic, social, political, and cultural development for equal enjoyment of life.”


---
**Page 182**
---

Two additional policies were reviewed that give mention to women and vulnerable persons, specifically children within the country. These were the National Gender-Based Violence Plan of Action 2010-2013[4] and the Belize National Child Labour Policy and Strategy 2012-2015.[5]

Both policies were shown to be gender transformative, while the GBV Plan of Action provides several recommendations for addressing GBV. The policy sets out specific goals and associated objectives for responding to GBV and supporting survivors of GBV. This plan addresses provisions for women’s empowerment and protection from violence. It also addresses the need for greater public awareness of the root causes of GBV and greater awareness and understanding by men of the root causes of GBV. The National Child Labour Policy and Strategy is a gender transformative policy in that it recognizes the importance of its development of action and denotes the need to build awareness of legal actions and support while also recognizing the importance of women’s empowerment and their equal participation. The policy and the plan provide a framework in which the protection and safety of these children are addressed, as well as the prevention and needed possible exploitation and harm that may arise intentionally or unintentionally.

A new energy policy is currently in development for the country to replace the previous one noted in the previous section. This new policy aims at improving access to electricity within this area for the new policy (access to the data has not been received yet)[6]. Additionally, New Service Areas are being developed with the aim of providing electricity to these areas which aims to reduce the cost of electrical connections by removing the additional connection setup fees and reducing the time it takes to connect a person to the grid. The policy also provides persons to gain access to electricity. In cases of area with no existing connections, the policy has provisions for persons to pay a deposit instead of the entire connection setup-cost upfront.

3.2.2 Usage

Routing Solar Photovoltaic

Within the Maya indigenous communities in the Toledo district (found in the southwestern part of the country) exist technical capacity to deploy solar energy technologies. Three Maya women were trained at Barefoot University in Belize and have since returned to their community with first-hand experience, the benefits of women being involved in the field could be seen. With this capacity generated by these women, they are now able to provide electricity for up to 100 homes using solar power over a two-year timespan. Additionally, through a project called Indigenous Female Solar Engineers Scaling-up Solar Energy to Macalhuita and Graham Creek, a collaboration between Plenty Belize and the Barefoot University, the communities of Macalhuita and Graham Creek provide these communities with solar powered electricity while also equipping these communities with the knowledge needed for maintenance.

This ongoing project has a strong emphasis on ensuring all residents of the community are empowered through skills-building and other capacity building workshops made available to all. Another aspect of this project is that it provides access to electricity in areas where there were no such services before. The project also provides solar-powered lighting kits with women who were trained at Barefoot University serving as models for other women and girls of the community. This project has provided a significant increase in household income, direct household benefits for all members. These benefits address children’s need for education through the provision of light and power for technologies that aid in education. For the adults in


---
**Page 183**
---

the household, access to electricity provide them with new opportunities for their livelihoods or enhancements to their livelihoods which in turn, enhances their ability to make decisions to increase their quality of life and implement climate change adaptation measures.25 Alongside the direct benefits of access to electricity are three other key indirect benefits such as 1) small shops with the ability to run a refrigerator/freezer on one or two systems and thus be able to store perishable goods, 2) the ability for schools to have refrigerators to store foods and extend food's shelf life, 2) a medium size power system installed at the primary school which would enhance the school’s ability to provide quality education to these children, and 3) a solar water heating system that can be used in households to reduce the time spent by women in these areas by providing clean water and reduce the time spent by primarily women in fetching water for household use.

Access to electricity provides numerous benefits to those living in rural communities. It provides cleaner energy use for lighting and other tasks and reduces exposure to smoke and other pollutants that are released from burning wood, charcoal, or kerosene lamps. It also allows for electric motors or fossil-fuel based energy sources typically used in rural communities. These allow them to save money on fuel costs and reduce the amount of time spent collecting firewood. Access to electricity enhances the quality of their lives in other ways. With reliable access to electricity, persons in very remote areas can use a refrigerator to store perishable goods, which would enhance food security for the community as it increases their security to an extent as calls for help can be sent out faster than persons have to journey out of the community and thus also applies to requesting medical aid. Additionally, having access to electricity allows them to engage in income generating activities that would ideally expand their capacity for participation and to exercise rights in the context of energy as well as other areas. The City of Toledo, Ohio, has been a leader in this area. In 2015, the City of Toledo Check District also utilize the combustion of fuels to meet their energy needs as well and face similar issues.

The abovementioned issues and concerns primarily affect those living in Toledo and Stann Creek districts. Those living in Coya, Orange Walk, Conzal and Beleze City districts which constitute the remaining areas of Belize are not affected by these issues because they have been reducing their need to rely on the burning of fuels to provide energy to carry out domestic duties or to power appliances. The only issue that is faced by these communities is the pollutants released from the combustion of these fuels. A notable exception to this would be for the island of Caye Caulker which has a diesel generator to supply the island with power. The island imports 730, 000 gallons of diesel annually to provide electricity for the entire island; however, in 2019, CDB approved a loan to fund the conversion of this diesel generator into a solar powered system that would generate electricity utilizing renewable energy for 50% of its energy and reduce electricity costs from US$1.26 per kWh to US$.08 per kWh. This will provide clean air, reduce greenhouse gas emissions, and resilient to storm surges and hurricane winds.

**LED Lighting**

In 2019, the Energy unit of the Ministry of Public Service, Energy and Public Utilities launched an initiative in collaboration with electrical students at the Institute for Technical and Vocational Education and Training (ITEVT) to install LED lights in public buildings. The initiative saw the installation of over 500 LED lights in various government buildings across the country. This is in public building and energy conservation measures to the public while also developing the


---
**Page 184**
---

technical knowledge and skills of the ITVE students. The initiative noted that over a 5-year period, the cost of using fluorescent lighting would have amounted to USD 27,311.09 (BZD 55,302.91) while the use of LED lighting for the same period would only cost USD 13,635.95 (BZD 28,040.00). This means that the savings on electricity costs alone would be USD 13,675.14 (BZD 27,262.91) over a period of five years. In addition to this reduction in electricity consumption which in turn, means households can save additional money on increased energy bills, LED lighting also has the added benefit that it is more durable and requires less maintenance. This means that single income especially female-headed homes with some relief as their financial burdens are lessened which could possibly translate to better mental health as persons would have more disposable income for other needs in addition to the savings from electricity costs. It can be subjected to improve other aspects of the household.

BEL carried out a renewable energy project nationwide, aimed at replacing 13,000 streetlights with LED lights in areas that were underserved and isolated areas across the country that did not have well lit streets, noting that some lights were installed in strategic areas frequented by people on their way to work. The Minister of Public Service and Aviation (Hon. Mr. Mark Anthony) and the Minister of Agriculture, Forestry, Fisheries and Food Security (Hon. Mr. George F. Benjamin) along with other government officials and members of the public attended the launch of the project in Belize City. The Minister of Public Service and Aviation (Hon. Mr. Mark Anthony) expressed satisfaction over the activity as recent crime in the area had reduced significantly since the installation of LED lights, noting that the lights were installed to provide a massive passive measure to reduce crime as persons would feel less inclined to commit criminal acts when their movements are being monitored. Additionally, women seemed to feel a bit more empowered to participate in society, particularly in sectors that may have them working late at night as the light provides a sense of safety.

Solar Water Heating

The Development Finance Corporation (DFC) has partnered with the Belize Biltmore Place to promote the adoption of renewable energy in businesses. In 2019, they provided the plaza with resources and training on how to use solar water heating systems. The initiative was aimed at raising awareness of financial instruments that can be utilized to invest in SWH as well as other RE technologies. This initiative was targeted at hotels as they use a large amount of hot water for laundry and guest usage. Caribbean Regional Energy Development Programme of the CARICOM Secretariat (CREDP) has also been working with businesses in Belize to promote the benefits of using SWH in their businesses as well as promote CARICOM region SWH manufacturing and exportation.

While a lot of the promotion and awareness efforts for SWH has been targeted at the tourism sector, SWH can also be beneficial to the domestic household. The use of a SWH can lead to the reduction of electricity consumption by up to 70% as persons would no longer need to use electricity for showering and other domestic tasks. Through this reduction, persons could possibly save up to USD 125 per year on their electricity bills. This means that persons will have more disposable financial through the savings made from using a SWH. Additionally, it can lessen the demand on water performance by speeding up the process of heating water when needed.

Efficient Cooking and Refrigeration

The country experiences a most tropical climate which slightly differs from other Caribbean Countries. The wet season occurs from May to October and its dry season occurs during November in April. The country sees a mean annual temperature range from 23-27°C, noting


---
**Page 185**
---

that mean annual temperatures have increased by 0.4°C at an average rate of 0.13°C per decade since 1960. As the effect of climate change begins to hit harder and affect more persons, there is an increased need for cooling and thus efficient cooling for the cost saving aspects of air conditioning systems. The Belize National Meteorological Service (BNMS)’s meteorological office shows temperatures from 31-34.5°C across the country with maximum temperatures reaching 38°C.

No data was found on differential usage if cooling between men, women, children and the elderly. It is important to understand that efficient cooling will be important in coming days to combat heat strokes as they may become common and rising temperatures may exacerbate discomfort and stress levels. The government has been working towards achieving its energy security and sustainability goals by diversifying its energy mix away from fossil fuels (see Figure 3-13), the government has been working to develop policies and strategies to guide the country in utilizing renewable energy sources and increase the adoption of EE to reduce energy consumption.

## 3.2.4 Barriers

### 3.2.3.1 Financial Considerations

Rising costs of living is not the only factor that inhibits the financial ability of Belizean people, particularly those in rural areas and low-income households. The lack of access to formal identification impacts the finances available to persons whether they are employed in seasonal sectors, working as informal workers or self-employed. This is especially true for women in the informal sector, particularly in rural areas as they may lack qualifications (education) or simply be unable to afford the costs associated with obtaining a national identity card. The cost of obtaining a national identity card identification, however, it must be noted this could be a barrier of entry to low-income persons as the application fee is BZD 120.00 (USD 35.86) and the processing fee is BZD 49.00 (USD 12.91 (BZD 26) to replace)** while the driver’s license costs USD 44.68 (BZD 90) to issue and


---
**Page 187**
---

USD 29.79 (BZD 60) to renew yearly. These costs may be untenable for some as they balance goals for themselves and unemployed dependents.

The UNICEF 2015 Multi-Indicator Cluster Survey for Belize found only 37% of women and 40% of men have a secondary level education. It also notes that two in five (2 in 5) have only primary school or less, and one in four (25%) has no formal schooling at all. These factors alone or working in conjunction together leave a notioned proportion of persons (35.3% or 642 out of the 182,084 employed persons[1]) with only informal work as a means of generating income.

Working in the informal sector may provide persons with opportunities and certain freedoms particularly rural women who may not have the ability to access formal education (due to inadequate childcare and/or lack of access to quality care). Despite these benefits, informal sector work can lack the security and integrity afforded to formal employment. This can have far-reaching consequences for the individual and their families. The informal sector in Belize could pose as a barrier to accessing loans and other financial tools from financial institutions.

Having access to financial services is critical to purchase renewable energy technologies (e.g., solar panels, water pumps) that are affordable (USD 1984.50[2] or USD 3969.42 which is 7-14.1% of the average yearly income (USD $28,000[3]) and can be a means to access other financial tools such as savings and credit will be a necessity for low-income female-headed households and other vulnerable groups to access renewable technologies, without which they otherwise else be left behind in the transition to green energy.

Similar barriers are experienced by indigenous communities, especially those in rural parts of the country. These communities face challenges related to their remoteness, limited access to roads, access, often with fully developed roads and telecommunication infrastructure and/or coverage. These factors make it difficult for these communities to access services provided by indigenous state. The barriers posed by limited transportation options and long travel times severely limit economic opportunities; be it seeking employment or selling goods as examples of how they may be able to generate income. Given the importance of agriculture as a source of livelihood for many and depending on the way agricultural activity is managed, many may be ineligible for access to credit due to their inability to provide collateral (e.g., land titles) and identification, financial records, business plans, marketing plans[4] and some rural indigenous farmers may not have the capacity or ability to have the capacity to develop these critical documents to meet the scope of the institutions.

Persons with disabilities (PWDs) face their own unique barriers and plight when it comes to accessing services. Many PWDs are unable to access services due to a lack of information on whatever aid they receive from relatives or organisations or government subvention. Many PWDs also have limited access to education, which is critical for them to be able to meet the needs of their disability and (ii) their limited access to education because of their disability thus the lack of necessary qualifications. Some PWDs live with relatives on whom they are entirely dependent financially, thereby creating an additional set of possible challenges to caregivers.

Given this financial dependence, PWIDs have limited economic power. In addition to the lack of finances due to unemployment, the cost of caring and training for disabilities can be quite expensive - this could range from the price of obtaining mobility aids to requiring frequent medical treatment or other services that are not covered by their conditions. PWIDs ability to implement renewable energy technologies thus is lower due to these financial demands as they form additional financial hurdles that PWIDs must deal with.

### 3.2.3.2 Housing

The state of housing has been an issue identified in Belize even within urban areas of the country in relation to the implementation (READER) technologies identified for the PWID population. The survey conducted by the MICS found that the project entails roofing solar (although there is a possibility of this technology being implemented in other ways). However, it was noted that access to electricity and water are requirements for homes to meet certain standards in order for these technologies to be effective.

Figure 3-16 shows the characteristics of housing across the country derived from the MICS survey. The results show that only 79% of urban households have access to electricity while 80% would not be able to support some of these technologies efficiently and would require adjustments to their homes. In rural areas, this figure is even lower at 65%. Similarly, 88% of rural households have finished floors, roofing and walls. 79% of urban households and 12% of rural households possess air conditioning.

The survey also found that 30% of urban households and 40% of rural households are unable to support the solar water heaters or solar photovoltaic and may even suffer from other problems. From the survey, 67% of households are headed by men with the remaining 33% headed by women. The Mestizo/Spanish/Latino ethnic group accounts for the largest ethnic group for household heads in both urban (51%) and rural areas (48%). The indigenous ethnic groups of Maya and Garifuna account for 0.1% and 5.7% respectively.

The state of housing quality is especially of concern for PWIDs in situations where they are living on their own or with family members who may not be able to provide the care that they need to upkeep their homes. Another group of major concern, because of housing quality and housing infrastructure, is indigenous communities living in rural areas, especially those who live in traditional houses. The survey found that 79% of urban households have access to electricity while EE and DERs require an adapted approach (either installation or the technology itself). Rooftop solar, for example, may compromise the structural integrity of the roofing over time without adjustments.

Figure 3-16: Housing Characteristics of Urban and Rural Homes in Belize


---
**Page 189**
---

An additional housing-related issue to consider is the ownership of the home. 68.9% of all households are owned by a household member with 50.3% of urban households and 71.2% of rural households being owned by a household member. For persons in urban areas, this suggests that they may be more likely to have access to energy technologies relative outside of the home. For people living in homes they do not own, there are likely limitations on what they can do with regards to installing energy technologies. In addition, property owner based on the rental agreement/lease they signed. The Landlord and Tenant Act does not provide provisions that would prevent renters acting on their own volition, this means the extent of modifications that a tenant can make is limited by the terms in the lease or the legal contract between the landlord and tenant. Implementing rooftop solar photovoltaic, solar water heating systems, heat pumps, and other technologies may be more difficult for tenants relative to renter versus property owner and the legal requirements in place as they may require infrastructure changes that are not possible.

With regard to leadership and decision-making within homes, it is notable that 67% of households are male-headed while only 33% are female-headed. Given that men and women have different energy needs and preferences, it would be important for researchers to consider whoever is making the decision on the sources of energy to be used by the household as there can be a significant difference in how these decisions are made. For example, male-headed households may spend more time at home performing domestic work and will benefit more from technologies that provide convenience or reduce the amount of time spent doing this type of work. Women headed households would likely have less time for cooking and cleaning and would, therefore, have more use for hot water. Likewise, women in male-headed households would spend more time at home and would benefit more from the cooling provided by air conditioning systems.

On the opposite spectrum, female-headed households may have the same needs of the solar water heater and other technologies as women in male-headed households, however they must contend with additional responsibilities such as childcare, eldercare or other dependency for multiple income sources in cases such as married couple, common law couple or other types of residence. Women in these situations are more likely to be responsible for the home while a woman caring for children or other dependents. In this situation with more limited resources, women have higher their needs and choose according to their priorities whereas acquiring solar water heater or other technologies may be designated as a low priority action in favour of other pressing needs.

Livelihoods

A critical element to consider when assessing the barriers and the capacities of persons to implement the target technologies is understanding the source of their income generation. Livelihoods can be defined as the means by which people make a living, earn an income and would have implications for the climate change adaptations, in this case the implementation of RE/EE/EDD technologies they can afford and are capable of implementing. While livelihoods could be considered a barrier to accessing these technologies, it is important to consider that there other barriers to accessing these technologies such as housing and finance. Income from livelihoods would then determine what technologies a person can afford, what losses they are


---
**Page 190**
---

eligible and other related measures they are able to adopt for the successful and effective implementation of the target technologies.

One of the biggest factors that affects a person’s livelihood and their options for livelihood is their ability to access education and assets. Having access to necessary resources and education, empowers people to make informed decisions about how best to adapt to climate change adaptation measures in this instance. From the highest education level attained by heads of households, 26.7% have a tertiary school level or higher; 30.5% have only primary school level of education as their highest form of education, 26.7% with a secondary school level, 19.5% with a tertiary school level and 7.2% with no formal education. It is critical to acknowledge that women are more likely than men to be employed in the informal sector compared to those held by persons as seen in the Belize Labour Force Survey. From the 2022 survey, service jobs accounted for 35.4% of all employment and were dominated by women (61.8%), particularly female-dominated (39.7% females to 15.4% males) while elementary occupations followed with 30.5% of all employment and were also predominantly held by women (62%). Service jobs are sales jobs are associated with the sector, but it is a concern that 28% of employed persons in this sector are female. The tourism sector is another sector where women contribute to the sector. The seasonal nature of the tourism sector is one concern people’s sources of income decrease during the off-season and increase when the season starts. For example, there is high season for tourism in Belize is from December to April which coincides with its dry season. As this will be a low level of ban before diffusion and other issues that may result from the lowest economic ability during this time.

Traditional gender norms are at play in the share of men and women in the tourism sector. Despite the fact that women dominate the tourism sector, they are not considered part of this sector as jobs related to catering and providing services to persons who are typically perceived as tourists. The division of labour is also a factor which has led some to believe that some being deemed for men continues to perpetuate the labour market segmentation and impact on women’s earnings in the sector. Women make up 50% of all employees, but the ratio of women employed in sales and services jobs accounting for double the number of men in this field. The tourism sector is also a sector where women are more likely than men to be the first persons choose careers. The domination of women in this field has one major consequence which is that it is a seasonal sector with low levels of employment during the off-season when the country receives less visitors and business slows down. The gender division of labour can also occur within the same women and men in clerical support worker roles (3:5:5 for women and 5:6% for men). Meanwhile, women are more likely than men to be employed in the following breakdown; plant and machine operators 8.1% to 6 men, women craft and related trades workers 20.7% to 4.9% of men, food preparation and serving workers 35.7% to 7.8% men to 0.8 women. Noted in 2020 Energy Report Card, women only accounted for 30.3% of all employees in the energy sector. The division of labour is also seen within public utilities commission (PUC) (see figure 3-17), further exhibiting the traditional division of labour seen in the country.

Men enjoy more stable sources of income which would allow them to amass finances and be able to buy into the services more easily being offered via the IUS project model. Issues of affordability are also a concern for women who may not have sufficient savings to make upfront payments or be able to acquire loans. Investments into RE, EE and DER would pose less of a risk and allow for greater financial security in the long term as well. For women, challenges may exist due to the nature of their work and other responsibilities they may have outside of paid employment. Women were also more likely than men to be outside of formal employment, unemployed or informally-employed, family cars and duties were identified as the main cause of those not actively seeking work in the 2022 LFS. The traditional division of labour within households was cited by women as a barrier to accessing education and employment opportunities as men are represented more in the associated engineering and energy fields. The three most common reasons given for this were: lack of interest, lack of confidence and fear for women to get involved in the sector.

### 3.2.5 Institutional Gender Mainstreaming Capacity

A limited gender mainstreaming capacity assessment was performed with stakeholders as part of the initial project planning phase to support a gender assessment of Belize. These series of questions were designed to obtain some insight into the capacity of stakeholders to mainstream gender within their organisations and to identify any institutional frameworks within their organisations exist for gender. Meeting with country's main body charged with implementing the IUS project was necessary to understand its capacity and ongoing work that may be able to inform or feed into this project.

From the government institutions, it can be seen in Figure 3-18, there is no gender mainstreaming capacity within the Ministry of Energy. The ministry has not had any experience in gender sensitivity and capacity-building, highlighting a need for gender sensitisation and capacity-building. It is important to note that NGOs have been involved with the government on several occasions to provide technical support gender mainstreaming activities and capacity-building, given their history with supporting renewable energy initiatives from another Belizean NGO, Plenty Belize.

Noting that the Ministry of Energy that has not had any experience in the past in the field of gender equality, the ministry has newly analysed these projects from a gender perspective. A competency-based approach is used in hiring and procurement.

Important to flag is the limited gender mainstreaming capacity of the Executing Body (BEL) as they will be charged with executing and handling various aspects of the project. Their limited


---

capacity will need to be a priority to address and resolve as early as possible in the project’s lifespan.

# Executing Entity Gender Capacity Analysis

One of the key stakeholders for this project will be the utility, BEL (the Executing Entity). They will be charged with executing this project which would entail managing the funds to be allocated to support the distribution of the renewable energy technologies to customers and interacting with communities. Given that the target beneficiaries are vulnerable groups such as rural, low-income women, indigenous people and PWDs, it was important to understand how BEL could best engage these groups in order to ensure their participation and the sustainability of the organisation and staff. Given BEL’s importance to the project, it was key to engage with them on the design of the project.

BEL shows flexibility in meeting and accommodating its customers through policies that allow customers to conduct all their business via WhatsApp communication. For those customers who do not utilise WhatsApp, BEL will go to their location in order to conduct business with said customers.

BEL frequently participates in trade shows which they use to educate customers on policies and procedures in order to boost customers’ ability to engage the utility. It is noted that BEL has no specific gender policy in place. However, it is noted that BEL does have a programme that works with indigenous groups to increase access to electricity. BEL is aware of the Free, Prior, and Informed Consent (FPIC) process and that indigenous people are guided by FPIC to a degree.

BEL has a grievance redress mechanism in place for employees; however, for customers, grievances are handled by the regulator (PUC).

BEL notes that it is not in possession of a gender policy.


## Jamaica

### 3.3 Policy environment

Jamaica’s renewable energy capacity stood at 17.1% in 2020 with solar and wind contributing to most of the country's current renewable energy usage, which is made up of a lot of work needs to be done to meet the country’s goal.

Jamaica’s Integrated Resource Plan 2018 laid out the country’s goal to reach 30% renewable energy usage by 2030. However, the country has a new goal it is aiming to achieve following Prime Minister Andrew Holness’ announcement in July 2020 that Jamaica will aim to generate energy by 2030 instead. The transition towards more energy efficient methods has the capacity to increase economic growth and reduce poverty while also enhancing community health, income generating opportunities, and capacity-building efforts. This is a win-win process of creating an enabling policy environment and barriers to entry, particularly for women.

Figure 3-19 provides a review of current policies. The policies listed in the Jamaica context include the National Policy on Gender Equality (NPGE), the National Policy on Energy Security and can be deemed as negligible in terms of support for any necessary gender mainstreaming actions. However, the National Policy on Gender Equality (NPGE), the NDC and the Vision 2030


---
**Page 195**
---

all provide a good framework for gender mainstreaming action and could serve as an entry point for revisions to relevant energy policies.

Seven policies were identified in Jamaica: 3 neutral, 1 sensitive and 2 transformative. The latest, the LTS, combines several elements and provides a critical long-term vision for where energy fits within a broader climate change response and sustainable development pathway. While the energy sector is not gender blind, other broader policy areas are more gender transformative.

The NPCIE is quite detailed and one of the few across the region and among the three countries that it includes all relevant sectors, including energy. The fact that two of the above-mentioned policy frameworks are transformative also lays a critical foundation for just transition elements to be included in the framework and can be used as a model for other countries. It provides a framework on the country’s commitments toward gender equality missions and goals. The policy supports the achievement of gender equality and justice for both women and men, in line with the goals and objectives as defined by Jamaica’s Vision 2030. The policy also seeks to address the underlying causes of gender inequality and gender ideologies and create transformation within the public sector. The policy's objectives support reducing inequalities between women and men through a focus on social protection, culture, social, political, and economic structures and systems. It also encourages the promotion of capacity-building and improved organisational behaviour throughout the development, implementation, and monitoring of policies and programmes. The policy promotes gender-cutting initiatives across sectors: poverty, education, labour, health, agriculture, and social services. The policy also seeks to ensure that all energy-related activities are gender equitable energy and women’s involvement or provision of services, noted within the policy.

Jamaica's update of its NDC was published in 2020. In preparation of the document, consideration was given to the impact on different population groups in a gender-responsive way. The document notes the country's progress towards its measures for reaching SDG 5 and highlights the importance of gender equality as an enabler of climate action aimed at improving environments for inclusivity and fairness as outlined in the National Policy for Gender Equality 2018-2021.

Jamaica’s National Development Plan™ (Vision 2030), developed in 2010 in intended to provide a roadmap for making Jamaica a developed country by 2030. The plan consists of several goals and associated outcomes. Within the four main goals are measures for social protection, health,


---
**Page 197**
---

and education, a secure and cohesive society, a prosperous economy, and maintaining a thriving natural resource environment. The plan recognizes inequalities experienced by women and PWIDs and seeks to rectify these issues by creating an environment that ensures future policies are gender-sensitive and address the needs of PWIDs in order to mitigate existing social problems within. Within the energy sector, the plan promotes improved energy conservation within households and communities through the provision of energy-efficient appliances and tests for promoting a technology-enabled society, while noting a desire to expand knowledge and development, creativity and development of products and services, does not specifically reference women or PWIDs.

Jamaica has recently, as of July 2022, developed and launched a gender and climate change strategy and action plan (GCCSAP), guided by the results of a national gender and climate change vulnerability assessment. The GCCSAP identifies key areas of concern related to climate change linkages, providing recommendations for action in the country going forward. Financed by the Global Environment Facility, it provides provisions for institutional capacity for mainstreaming gender in climate finance programming.

### 3.3.2 Usage

In general, women are faced a disproportionate burden of labour compared with men, through their responsibilities taking care of children and other household tasks, in addition to any labour performed outside the home for earned income; in terms of women's abilities to access financial services, women face additional barriers due to lack of collateral, as well as gender-based plunders placed on women though their dual roles as caretakers and paid employees in the labour force.

Ensuring proper services through sustainable energy provisions can provide both cost and time-savings to consumers, especially women who already may be operating in a deficit in each of these areas. In addition, such savings may allow women to accumulate capital which can have additional benefits in terms of resource allocation toward better health, added income and education for women in most contexts.

### LED Lighting

In recent years the Jamaican Public Service (JPS) has been working on energy conversion projects and initiatives in Jamaica, including those for light-emitting diode (LED) lighting and high-performance sodium lamps. The JPS has reported that only 10% of its buildings are fitted for energy efficiency retrofits consume only 7% of Jamaica's total generated electricity. In 2018, the government announced a plan to replace all existing HPS lamps with LED lights, which covered a total of 67,000 out of the 105,000 streetlights on the nation's roads. In 2022, the company was also reported to have converted all existing high-pressure sodium (HPS) lamps with LEDs within Jamaica. The JPS has been working in partnership with the government and private sector to implement technology capabilities. JPS is continuing work on its strength general maintenance and repair programs.

Difficulties with the HPS lighting, including vandalism, have been an issue. These problems are confirmed with statistics showing that an average of 620 repairs per month were conducted by


---
**Page 198**
---

JPS, due to high rates of maintenance repairs due to crime. Efforts by JPS on street lighting is a part of Jamaica’s Energy Policy, to facilitate the Introduction of energy-saving devices e.g LED, solar panels, and solar street lighting under Goal 1. Applications from persons for new streetlights must be submitted to the Parish Council for approval. The Parish Council (PC) must be authorized by the Parish Council to install new street lighting infrastructure and outside of this, under Jamaica’s Energy Policy, there are no noted implementing frameworks that support the energy transition.

### LED Lighting for Support for Women and Vulnerable Groups

Provision of improved lighting services can support various vulnerable groups in the urban and rural communities. Improved lighting services can help reduce crime by increasing visibility, enhancing their senses of vulnerability and insecurity, and placing restrictions of their ability to safely pursue independent activities at night. Increased street lighting can also improve safety for women in areas with sufficient lighting. Increased Street lighting can offer important economic advantages in addition to safety benefits. Better lighting services can enable longer business hours which can be a significant factor in the success of small businesses. Improved lighting is also use for women who operate in the informal economy roles such as street vendors.

By creating safe gathering spaces, lighting services have also been shown to support the reduction of violence against women and girls (VAWG). Lighting services can improve the safety of people, especially women who stay indoors during the night. For other vulnerable populations, improved lighting services can help them navigate their environment more easily, regardless of how well they perform and function by supporting their visual, cognitive, and behavioral abilities through increased visibility.

Problems and challenges, however, do exist in transitioning energy systems and improving lighting infrastructure. The transition to LED streetlights is a good example where the upgrading government efforts to illuminate crime-ridden areas. The power company disclosed in 2019 that the three municipalities of St. Ann, Clarendon, and Hanover had over 500 streetlights out of their year- 525 streetlights have been stolen. The US Department of State reports that the municipalities of Montego Bay and Portmore have some of Jamaica’s worst rates of violent crime. These locations are also areas where LED streetlighting has not yet been implemented. Clarendon, St. Ann, and St. Moneague and Clermont in St. Ann, Jamaica's largest parish.

In such environments, the lack of proper public lighting due to theft can further exacerbate feelings of vulnerability and insecurity. Women living in these areas may need to navigate these areas during nighttime hours. Moreover, women are often primary caregivers and responsible


---
**Page 199**
---

for the safety and well-being of their families. Insufficient lighting in their communities can hinder their ability to protect themselves and their children, further compromising their sense of security.

As most of the country has already been converted to LED lighting, additional challenges can also occur during the repair and replacement process of solid LEDs. The JPS may be reluctant to connect new solar water heaters (SWH) in areas where there is no existing electricity supply. It may not be deemed feasible to fix streetlights, leaving the areas that need lighting the most, without. The safety concerns are compounded by the fact that many residents live alone and in some areas is another potential constraint as employees become hesitant to enter areas for fear of persons stealing the lights from their possessions and/or their own personal safety.

Solar Water Heating
In 2016, it was estimated that more than 5,000 solar water heaters (SWH) were installed in Jamaica. As of 2020, Jamaica’s use of renewables accounted for approximately 11 per cent of its energy: 8.9 per cent is hydropower and 3.5 per cent is solar.

The State has been slow to respond to the potential it is expected to have due to the high levels of incivility and urbanism. The country receives an average daily peak of 4.6 hours of sunlight for solar panel installation, which is more than enough to meet domestic energy needs. However, there are barriers specific to SWH adoption which include limited access to capital, incomplete information and data on SWHs, lack of awareness among the public about the benefits of SWHs, and the need to integrate into the grid and economic regulatory distortion. All of these factors pose challenges to promoting SWHs in Jamaica.

The country has a high female poverty rate, which makes it difficult for women to gain access to capital available for women; a lack of available sex disaggregated data to inform decision-making on the relations on gender mainstreaming in policy across the energy sector more generally.

SWH systems in the Caribbean region have low payback periods and a high net present value (NPV) compared with other technologies, not only will they reduce electricity bills but also generate profits or in this case immense savings. They can be easily extended depending on incentives, local conditions and the type of SWH used. The cost of installation is relatively low for the types used and other factors, but they generally begin at USD1,000 not including installation. This cost can deter home and business owners from investing. These issues of costing can pose a significant barrier to adoption in Jamaica where there are already many socio-economic and income constraints. It is estimated that 47% of children live in single-parent homes with their biological mothers or stepmothers, which makes it difficult for these women to access capital. Financial institutions have begun offering segmented renewable energy financing. Although the direct purpose of this financing is not to promote SWHs, it can be used by households to invest and utilize energy efficiencies more generally; conservation and alternatives, they can be utilised for the installation of SWH. The Development Bank of Jamaica, Exim Bank, National Housing Trust and


---
**Page 200**
---

The National People’s Cooperative Bank of Jamaica Limited all offer financial services specifically mentioning SWH.

Heated water may be considered a luxury for some, but it is part of many persons’ everyday life. While the water is mainly used by everyone in the house for showering, women tend to use water for washing clothes and other household chores. The cost of heating water has increased as the current levels of water heating currently account for up to 30 per cent of a domestic light bill in Jamaica. More sustainable methods of water heating should be considered.

SWHs are a more efficient way of heating water, which reduces the carbon footprint and is expected to reduce electricity bills since the fuel is free sunlight. Due to low operational costs, SWHs can pay for themselves over the long term cover the huge initial fixed cost. When all costs for purchase, installation, maintenance, and operation are taken into account after the payback period, the SWH systems are expected to provide hot water for free with lower electricity bills. The cost of SWHs is expected to be reduced by 20 per cent in 2030, making it a cost-effective option for low-income homes including single-mother homes, persons living alone and disabled persons.

Rooftop Solar Photovoltaics

The transition to a greener and more sustainable future can be facilitated by creating a sector for clean energy that is gender inclusive and gives women in rural communities more influence as both producers and consumers of energy. This will help to increase access to electricity, improving loan access, skill development, education, and broader systemic gender issues like property rights and land ownership. The sector also has the potential to provide income support women’s success in the sector.

Women may play a crucial role in the transition to renewable energy by influencing patterns of energy use within their households from using clean energy as consumers. With access to clean energy technologies, women's exposure to indoor air pollution can be eliminated. Additionally, the ability for women to charge mobile phones and other small appliances (such as electric phone chargers) are made possible due to increased connection to electricity and this in turns leads to a reduction of time spent traveling long distances to charge devices. This also means that they use energy directly (such as water pumps for irrigation and grain mills), as well as marketing these products and services (for example solar panels, clean cookstoves, solar lamps, etc.).

## Efficient Cooling and Refrigeration

Energy-related behaviors and cooling energy consumption are influenced by gender, noting variations in consumption among different genders.

A general finding from a study of cooling energy use by researchers in China noted that, particularly for women, using the air conditioner frequently is a regular practice that reflects cooling energy use. However, thermal preferences have more noticeable influence on cooling energy consumption than do other factors such as temperature and humidity. Women tend to set higher thermostat limits such as daily average hours of air conditioning use, the use of electric fans in place of air conditioners, and the use of curtains or blinds for shading windows. These practices lead to higher energy consumption for both men and women. Women are more likely to provide unpaid care for young children and elderly family members who may be sensitive to heat. This can result in poorer health due to physical and mental conditions and would therefore require more cooling in poorly ventilated homes. In addition, women tend to spend more time outdoors than men, which makes them more likely to be exposed to high temperatures or hot locations.

The effects of climate change from an outdoor labour perspective which can also have some implication towards health and livelihood management.[15]

A refrigeration and cooling case study conducted in 2020 in Kenya found that the use of cooling technology was able to boost productivity and give women more options to earn money and more flexibility. In addition, cooling appliances such as fridges, freezers, and air conditioners can extend shelf life, refrigerators can provide women more power by eliminating tedious tasks and providing added time and energy for other activities like cooking, cleaning, and preparing meals and can save food costs by reducing the need to get rid of food that has spoiled.

As another method to provide cooling for labour and in the home, fans are a relatively low-cost appliance which can help increase productivity and help those at most risk adapt to climate change.[16] A study conducted last year looked into the insights of women farmers on what they thought electric fans were the most common electrical appliance used (at a rate of 94%) followed by televisions, refrigerators, and air conditioners. The study was carried out in four parishes: St. Andrew, St. James, Clarendon, and St. Catherine.[17] Electrical appliances that consume a large amount of electricity such as freezers, dishwashers, and electric ovens were used by 74% and 25.6% of the households respectively.

At present there are currently no approved policies in place to implement and scale use of efficient cooling appliances for outdoor labour, as of 2021, as part of the regional Caribbean Cooling Initiative (C-COOL), Jamaica's National Cooling Strategy (JNCS) is currently being developed. The strategy was initially developed by the Ministry of Energy. The strategy’s recommendations were refined and were undertaken by various inter-agency working groups within the Ministry of Energy and the Cabinet. Once approved, would be the second such strategy of its kind adopted in the region.[18]

### 3.3.4 Barriers

Some of the specific challenges women face in terms of access to affordable energy, and more specifically their roles as typically the primary care providers in the home, include: having access to energy for maintaining domestic work, access to water and energy options that are both safe and reliable, and the ability to use energy efficiently. Women also have different hours of labour through their domestic duties and those outside the home. Various barriers also affect women’s use of technologies.

#### Institutions

A weak enabling policy environment for SWH also poses a barrier to implementation. While Jamaica has multiple policies on renewable energy, there seems to be only one with stipulations specific to SWHs. The National Energy Policy 2018-2037 includes provisions that facilitate the installation of SWH and solar lights in the public and private sectors and communities are included as a priority area for the implementation of these technologies. There is also a fund for conservation and efficiency. The incentives, include concessional loans and import duties on EE and RE, and the exemption of lithium-ion batteries from the National Consumption Tax.

Last year, Jamaica launched its first SWH awareness campaign. These campaigns are specific outreach campaigns for SWH in Jamaica. There have been efforts to increase awareness of the merits of renewable energy more generally; however, in order for women to have access to this renewable energy technology, it is important that a system of affordable and interest-free credit rates loan programs, should be implemented.

Another barrier that arises in the administrative process involved when applying for the installation of SWHs. The application process involves many steps and can take a long time to implement, posing an obstacle to people applying. This process makes it more difficult for women who are often responsible for domestic work and may experience a range of domestic violence and other forms of gender-based violence to occur during times where


---
**Page 205**
---

sunlight is absent. With a high rate of violence within the country, the lack of this infrastructure increases the risk and probability of these events occurring and further burden the already burdened police and security services in the country.

Social

Without increasing the provision of support through the addition of reliable and sustainable services and infrastructure, it is more difficult for women in low-income households to improve their positions and livelihoods, their ability to earn decent livelihoods, and to improve their socio-economic status. Women in low-income households are also at a greater risk than men because they face in their economic status from abilities to engage in income-generating activities and benefit from the provision of social services. The following areas are some examples of these issues mentioned including health, housing, and education for both women and the families they provide for.

As private consumers and users of domestic energy, for CDBs, e.g., washing, household cleaning and personal care, cooking etc., women need to be involved in energy efficiency and renewable energy projects from their direct and indirect impacts on levels and usage of household energy. Women can also be involved in the promotion of energy conservation and consumption and conservation practices through their own examples and sustainable behaviors. This can include encouraging other family members to adopt energy-saving, incorporate energy-conscious practices into daily life, or utilize educational resources and involve them in the decision-making process regarding energy use. Involvement in this environment impacts the way future generations will learn to engage in efficient and sustainable energy use and promote energy conservation.

There are other barriers faced in implementing rooftop solar, in particular. Due to the lower discretionary income in low-to-medium-income communities, hefty upfront expenses for rooftop solar can be a significant barrier. Additionally, many low-to-moderate income persons do not always benefit fairly from such offers. Single-family homeowners also frequently encounter challenges with roof durability and quality, as well as issues related to aesthetics, and shoddy or outdated electrical wiring. The best roofs for solar have a solid foundation, no leaks, and do not require much maintenance when first installed.104 Families with lower incomes are more likely to rent than own their homes, which can be an obstacle in the transition from expensive home renovations to accommodate on-site solar arrays.

Renting also can present barriers to accessing solar. In 2008, about 63% of Jamaicans lived in their own homes and owned them outright; however, for other types, the report says 51% of Jamaicans occupied rented or leased property.105 A survey found that investors consider affordability as a major barrier to rooftop solar adoption.106 The cost of installing solar panels for buildings with solar PV is investment roadblocks. The focus of apartment or townhouse owners is one of the biggest barriers to rooftop solar adoption, as they are not the owner of the property who bears responsibility for applicable, and any damages that installation or usage may cause to the property.

From Figure 2-3.2, it can be seen the NGO organizations possess gender mainstreaming capacity that can be used to address the noticeable lack of gender mainstreaming capacity held

# Multi-Country Level Analysis

## Key chapter takeaway

Though progress has been made across the region on gender equality, women’s empowerment and social inclusion generally and in energy access and affordability, progress and sustainable programs are still lacking. The focus of this project was to identify what is being done on financial and social barriers as well as instruments designed to improve an even promotion of a range of technologies.

## 4.1 Comparative Policy Environment Analysis

Looking across the three countries, based on the analysis executed, it can be determined that Barbados has made the most progress in terms of gender equality, women’s empowerment policies. From all the Barbadian policies reviewed, only the Barbadian NDC was found to be gender-responsive. The other two were gender-aware but not gender-sensitive. This is a major difference from the NDC development and submission. Belize and Jamaica (following closely behind) are making greater strides in incorporating gender mainstreaming into their gender policies to ensure the vulnerable women or vulnerable groups at all putting the policy on with Belize’s and Jamaica’s much older energy policies.

It is important to note that Barbados’ energy policy released in 2019 failed to mention gender, women or vulnerable groups at all putting the policy on with Belize’s and Jamaica’s much older energy policies. This is a major difference from the NDC development and submission as the policy is meant to drive country's energy direction until 2030.

The gender policies reviewed for Belize and Jamaica were more transformative, indicating that there was a greater awareness of the need for gender equality to occur within these countries. Two out of the 4 NDCs were gender-responsive which would be due to the acknowledgement of the differences in the populations and the need to address the needs of all groups. The policies outline actions that would mitigate and address these issues to ensure the needs of the populations are met.

A major focus of the desk-based research was delving into the policy environment for renewable energy and gender for each country to understand the enabling environment that exists within each country. This was done by reviewing the gender equality, women’s empowerment policies at the countries’ NDCs (outlines the countries' actions to combatting climate change which has implications on gender equality and its impact on persons), energy policies, gender policies (including policies relating to the protection of sustainable development goals) and other development policies that would guide the countries directions.

Twelve policies were reviewed overall and while 12 were gender-responsive or transformative, only one was found to be gender-aware at best. (See Figure 4-1). Specifically looking at the energy policies of the countries which would have major influence on the execution of this project, only the Guyanese draft policy was found to be gender-responsive.

**Conclusions:**

Considerations were started in 2022 for a draft gender equality strategy for the CARICOM region titled "Stepping up A Strategy to achieve gender equality in the Caribbean Community." The document comprises a series of strategic actions to achieve gender equality and women's empowerment in partnership with member states and international organizations. It is based on the CARICOM Gender Equality Policy which aims to strengthen CARICOM secretariat capacity to promote gender equality and women's empowerment in partnership with member states and international organizations, and mainstream gender into all policies.

The CARICOM Gender Equality Strategy is driven by six guiding principles and commitments as shown in Figure 4.3:

| Guiding Principles | Commitments |
|---|---|
| **Equality** | **Non-discrimination** |
| **Participation** | **Empowerment of women & girls** |
| **Accountability** | **Harmonization** |

This work centred on a human rights approach, which recognises every person's right to freedom from discrimination and violence while operating in a just and equal society. It also upholds respect for diversity, including the rights of persons with disabilities, children, youth, and people living with illness. The strategy also prioritizes governance which further recognizes the rights of all persons within CARICOM. The harmonization principle refers to creating effective governance in order to create effective governance that recognises the unique needs, barriers, and opportunities for both women and men.

### 4.2 Cross-cutting Stakeholder Findings

Despite being from different countries and operating in different contexts, stakeholders shared some key insights and concerns with regard to the issues discussed during direct engagement via key informant interviews and focus group discussions.

#### 4.2.1 Finance

- **Major hurdle to accessing loans particularly for women is time:** Banking transactions typically require a person to allocate a fair amount of time and effort to engage these services, and women especially those from female-headed households are usually constrained by their responsibilities at home.
  
- **Location plays a role in hindering access to financial tools,** particularly for many who reside in rural areas or remote locations with no convenient banking facilities. The nearest financial institution is usually very far away from where they live, and the distance acts as a deterrent.

  This particular barrier can also involve time - the time it takes to get to and from the bank/branch, which may be several hours each way.
  
- **Banking processes can be tedious:** and the requirements to engage in their services such as proof of identity, account opening, etc., lack of collateral often eliminate or discourage persons from seeking these options.

- **Persons working in the informal sector and low-income persons utilise informal saving mechanisms:** such as savings clubs, money lenders, and other informal financial institutions that allow persons to conduct large transactions when they are unable to meet requirements imposed by formal financial institutions. These pooled resources may be used to meet downpayments requested by financial institutions.

**Page 213**

# Gender Mainstreaming Capacity

Efforts are being made by energy ministries to enhance their gender mainstreaming capacity. The Ministry of Energy and Business of Barbados noted that work on a gender equality strategy and action plan for the electricity sector is being advanced which could have bearing on gender mainstreaming in the sector.

Many NGOs, particularly the disability-focused ones noted that they are rarely consulted for any project development or implementation, and it can be seen in the results of the projects. The lack of capacity to engage with women and girls is a major challenge.

Gender mainstreaming capacity is low among government stakeholders, notably many do not possess the skills required to effectively engage with women and girls in their countries. However, it must be stated that with increased engagement with international funds which require gender equality as one of their conditions, they fund, capacity is slowly being developed and more awareness on gender is being raised.

## Employment in the Energy Sector

The energy sector is traditionally not one in which women participated significantly in the past. The situation has changed somewhat with women involved across several areas. Numbers remain relatively low as men still dominate this field. Feedback received via stakeholder engagement indicates that there are no explicit policies to guide gender mainstreaming.

## Engagement with Vulnerable Groups

### Indigenous Peoples (Belize)

Many of the indigenous people are found in farremote or rural areas of the countries and exhibit some of the highest rates of poverty in the country and share similar vulnerabilities and challenges with access.

Respect must be paid to governing systems employed within the different communities and knowledge of these systems is critical for effective engagement with local indigenous populations. Recognizing this, acknowledging that a "one size fits all" approach will not work when engaging indigenous communities as they hold varying traditions and practices.

Exploring the technologies with the stakeholders, many shared similar perspectives and information on the benefits and demand for LED lighting. The inputs gained from stakeholders were fairly aligned with what was discovered through desk-based research and field visits to cross-check and verify experiences.

From the stakeholder feedback (reflected also in the Table), there is uniformity in the uses and benefits of the technologies across the three countries. While there is demand for each technology, Barbados sees the most demand for SWH due to the extensive framework supporting it. The other two countries see the most demand for Solar PV due to the inclusion of incentives such as income tax rebates. Noted across the board by stakeholders is that the cost of these technologies is a significant and prohibitive factor to persons purchasing these items.

## 4.3 Barriers Across the Region

From the analysis of the three countries, a pattern of barriers has emerged. Despite differences in location, culture, economics, etc., the people particularly vulnerable groups all face similar barriers. The most common barrier identified is lack of access to information and education. This is the most support for the adoption of renewable energy technologies by the population, vulnerability groups, and other stakeholders. There are also a number of other barriers that are part of a supporting framework.

There is relatively poor access to and availability of fair and disaggregated data across the three countries in the Caribbean region in general, particularly on energy use, demand, needs and uptake. This results in challenges in differentiating energy usage among men, women, gendered groups, etc., which makes it difficult to identify the most vulnerable groups. Reliable energy data usually is household rather than individual specific and does not engender intersectional analysis. Also available to some extent are disaggregated information on income for the design of targeted interventions, such as for awareness campaigns or about financial measures. This means that existing and future actions informed current data are less effective as the assumptions made may be incorrect. The lack of reliable energy data is a primary reason including the likelihood that investments will not address the practical or strategic gendered needs of vulnerable groups in the region and that it would make difficult to also differentiate impacts across groups of beneficiaries.


---
**Page 215**
---

Economic barriers are heavily present in all three countries as strict requirements for loans imposed by banks is the norm across the region and women are challenged to enhance their access and control of such resources because of reduced economic ability due to traditional division of labor. Women in these countries also face challenges related to poverty, which exhibit the highest rates of poverty while being located in the most rural areas of their respective countries. This makes it difficult for them to save money for modern and accessible energy solutions. Not only do they have to navigate the loan requirements but they must also contend with the challenges that time and distance impose on them.

Socially, we can see in the three countries that work in the energy sector is skewed more towards men than women. Women are often relegated to lower paying jobs or informal employment based on roles traditionally designated to women such as clerical and administrative work with few women taking up positions of leadership within these organizations. This gender imbalance is also influenced by patriarchal norms** and patriarchal norms' influence is not only restricted to formal work. The gendered division of labor in the household, which places a disproportionate burden on women, is skewed disproportionately towards women and is a common occurrence in the region. This imbalance of dimensions of poverty and vulnerability that women face and limited finances they are able to attain due to the resulting time-poverty that comes from them performing the majority of domestic, primarily unpaid care work.

# Key Conclusions

## Key chapter takeaway

While the demand and need for an IUS project is clearly present in the target countries and there exist enabling environments of varying degrees to support the project. At present, given the complexity of the project, the high cost of implementation, and the need to ensure sustainability and promote a Just Transition is highly questionable and will need several high level as well as practical solutions. The potential for increased income generation through the use of renewable energy participation of low-income and single-headed households could be tapped into as well, leaving fewer people behind in terms of access to services in the short and medium-term. Gender and social considerations are relevant in all countries.

Based on the engagement with stakeholders and the research there is both appetite and increasing need for renewable energy technologies, particularly affordable, easily maintained and those that can be used by low-income households. However, while demand exists at a population level, demand is primarily rooted in potential cost and income savings and the additional potential for income generation. The other benefits of using these technologies such as improved health outcomes derived from these technologies are often viewed as additional benefits and does not serve the main motivation for driving the interest/desire of households and businesses for technologies. For governments, there is a need to ensure that the uptake of renewable energy technologies provides mitigation-adaptation co-benefit.

Despite this demand, many regions across the three focus countries are restricted from investing in these technologies primarily due to financial barriers. Uptake of these technologies for low-income persons is often restrained by limited financial resources or inability to access additional financing. This can lead to competition between households and businesses leading to some conflicts, and competing priorities. This project’s ability to market and distribute these technologies will be critical as well as the ability to provide training and support in ways that respond to social, cultural and livelihoods needs is crucial to meet multiple SDGs.

Consideration must be given to involving vulnerable groups in the further development and implementation of this project. The project should also ensure that its sustainability goals are met without involving those typically left behind and who are generally underserved by existing policies and programs. This will require a significant investment from significant public sector and public finance investment. Moreover, the project should be active gender-responsive paying close attention to intersectional gender needs as well as intersecting social vulnerabilities.

For Belize with large rural populations and significant indigenous sub-populations, customer outreach is crucial. Coupled with the financial issues mentioned, these communities also deal with the lack of access to services in terms of health, education, and other basic services across the country and from technology hubs. Likewise, the needs of PWIDs require greater attention and a specific approach.

Noting the lack of gender mainstreaming capacity amongst the government stakeholders who will be involved in the project and critically the lack of engagement with most of the executing entities, it is critical that this project ensures that all actors are engaged in an informed and sensitized way in which shape implementation, sustainability and scaling-up will be critical in the pre-implementation period of the project as well as the first year of implementation.
