# Recommendations

## Key chapter takeaway

Ensuring the project can fulfill its goal of supporting a just transition will require the execution of some key gender mainstreaming actions. These are aimed at addressing existing capabilities and gaps that were found in this gender assessment. Critical gender action areas include: ensuring that women’s voices are heard, particularly those who are most vulnerable; ensuring that women have access to and control over resources; and ensuring that women’s livelihoods are more long-term in also setting a key good practice for other multi-country energy programmes. Utilizing the 7-point gender needs framework will be critical as it is not only a gender lens but also somewhat sharp learning curve but one that will boost the scope for multiple and expanding public-private sector partnership.

Emerging from the gender assessment, some of the recommendations to be explored in this section will be based on a 7-point gender needs framework. The recommendations touch on 6 of the 7 points: access to resources, control over resources, decision-making power, participation, gender-based education and assets, access to and control over resources, and livelihoods.

Recommendations are divided into two categories: 1) general recommendations that speak to actions aimed at empowering programme actors and addressing gender mainstreaming gaps identified in the assessment; and 2) more specific recommendations that seek to guide programme actors’ interactions with vulnerable groups and overall programme design. Both sets of recommendations aim for a high level of ownership and participation at the local, community, group, household, and individual levels.

### General recommendations

These recommendations seek to empower programme actors and address gender mainstreaming gaps identified in the assessment. The general recommendations are as follows:

1. Partnership guidelines aimed to serve as a framework to be used by programme actors (government stakeholders) to guide how they interact and form working relationships with NGOs.
2. Partnership building exercises aimed at building strong working relationships between government agencies, NGOs as well as between government agencies to eliminate potential silos of activities.
3. Fostering collaboration between the three countries to enhance knowledge and experience sharing.
4. Execution of an in-depth gender capacity assessment of key government stakeholders and utilities involved in the programme. The development of gender sensitisation and gender-capacity-building training will be based on the results of the gender capacity assessment.
5. More in-depth analysis of the financial tools being offered by the banks, credit unions, etc., to ensure the financial tools being offered by the utilities as part of this programme are competitive.


---
**Page 218**
---

6. A review of the programme’s logic framework and the design of gender targets of the prog including sex disaggregated indicators.

7. Development of training programmes that could help persons to seek employment in the energy sector and support the distribution and installation of technologies.

8. The IUS programme should provide support designed to address gender inequalities such as - gender sensitive repayment terms (no ID required; longer periods; lower collateral requirements); flexible loan amounts; targeted outreach for women-led businesses, including SMEs; business; adjustable rates for female headed households with children; targeted capacity building outreach to women-led businesses and other vulnerable groups to support their ability to access the energy sector.

9. Consideration will need to be given to removing or mitigating inequity imbalances in terms of access to technologies including exploring more supportive pay back terms of loans, and ensuring that all groups are able to access them.

10. Assess lessons learned from existing post loan and grant programmes that target or engage vulnerable groups so that these can inform the GCF programme design and implementation and shape financing options and how these can be accessed differently by such groups.


### 6.1.2 Stakeholder engagement recommendations

Given the outward-facing nature of this programme and the many potential actors that could be involved throughout in this activities, there is a need to create a framework for gender-responsive engagement with the beneficiaries. The stakeholder engagement recommendations are as follows:

1. Development of a code of conduct for all programme actors to follow when engaging the public and particularly direct beneficiaries.

2. Development of GBV checklist to reduce the potential of GBV occurring within the project due to the interactions between programme actions and the public and between different publics as well at the household level.

3. Development a communication strategy that aims to provide the public with timely updates on the programme and ensure that these communications including updates and technology awareness is crafted to ensure the information is easily understood and accessible to those who are blind, deaf or speak different local languages such as the indigenous populations.

4. Development of a Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) for the programme that is readily accessible and easy to utilise by the public including multiple methods to access and use.

5. Development of a gender-sensitive stakeholder engagement plan to guide all programme activities, including training and awareness raising sessions.

6. The hosting of townhall meetings or alternative approaches to engage specific vulnerable groups to ensure their issues and concerns are considered within the programme.


Figure 6-1 aligns the recommended actions to gender needs and identified barriers mapping out in summary form the recommendations for inclusion into the Gender Action Plan (GAP). It responds to three main barriers: institutional, social and economic/financial.

# Gender Action Plan

## Key chapter takeaway

Ensuring the programme can fulfill its goal of supporting a just transition will require the execution of some gender-responsive actions in the short term and others that are more long-term. These actions address existing capabilities and gaps that were found in the gender assessment. Critical gender actions include ensuring that gender is mainstreamed into all aspects of the UUS programme, which are more long-term as well setting a good practice for other multi-country energy programmes. Utilizing the GCF’s Gender Policy (GP) will be critical to achieving this goal and overcoming what may seem somewhat sharp learning curve but one that will boost the scope for multiple and expanding public-private sector partnerships.

In the preparation of the proposal for the "Scaling up the Deployment of Integrated Utility Services (UIS) to Support Energy Sector Transformation in the Caribbean (Phase I)" Programme, it is essential to ensure that gender equality is mainstreamed into all aspects of the programme. The Caribbean Development Bank’s (CDB)'s requirements for gender equality. As a result, a gender assessment was done and from this assessment, emerged areas of concern and weakness that need to be addressed so that gender is adequately mainstreamed in this programme so it can support a Just Transition.

Gender mainstreaming is the process by which gender equality can be achieved through the integration of a gender perspective into all processes, policies and frameworks that are adopted. A gender-neutral stance, and by creating the necessary provisions and relevant supporting documents, ensures that gender equality is mainstreamed in all aspects of the UUS programme, this entails ensuring the selection and financing models employed by the programme are gender-responsive and provide benefits to marginalised groups as it pertains to the requirements of the GCF.

For the GCF, its updated 2020 Gender Policy (GP) seeks to ensure the institution and its network of partners adopt a comprehensive gender approach to support climate change interventions and innovations. The GP also aims at ensuring that gender equality is mainstreamed in all aspects taken through the promotion of climate investments and support the closing of the gender gap through mainstreaming gender equality in climate investments.

Applying the GCF’s GP to the UUS Programme would involve:

- Addressing and reducing gender inequality resulting in sustainable livelihoods opportunities, increased economic empowerment, and increased resilience to climate-induced shocks and risks for men and women.
- Creation of an enabling environment among programme stakeholders founded on existing policy commitments wherein social and environmental standards and grievance redress mechanisms are supported and sustained in order to achieve gender equality.


---
**Page 221**
---

- Support the comprehensive and systematic collection of sex-disaggregated data and execute qualitative and quantitative gender analysis to monitor, report and evaluate progress.

GCF’s 2018 Indigenous Peoples’ Policy seeks to guide the institution's engagement with IPs in climate change policies and action while acknowledging IPs as a distinct stakeholder group that needs to be involved. The policy is guided by the following principles:

- Recognize and respect the rights of IPs over their lands, territories and resources.
- Respect and enhance the rights of IPs on their lands, territories and resources.
- Recognize and respect the rights of IPs in laws and principles.
- Respect the right of IPs under voluntary association.
- Respect and recognize traditional knowledge and livelihood systems.
- Enhance the participation of IPs in GCF.
- Facilitate access to GCF resources for IPs.
- Respecting the system of self-governance.

In the context of the IUS Programme and its GAP, the IP policy would apply to the following areas:

- Programme actors properly seek free, prior and informed consent when engaging with IPs on their lands, territories or resources.
- Ensure that adverse impacts are avoided or minimized and compensate for residual impacts.

The CDB’s Gender Equality Policy and Operational Strategy (GEPOS: 2019) outlines CDB's commitment to advancing gender equality through recognizing the equal rights, responsibilities and opportunities of women, men, girls and boys which include the equal power to shape their own lives. The policy seeks to achieve gender equality in BMCS based on the following action pillars:

- Equitable access to infrastructure services for all,
- Economic empowerment of all,
- Education and training for all,
- Elimination of gender-based violence for all, and
- Resilience for all.

The guiding principles of the GEPOS that are applicable in the IUS programme include:

- Relevance, balance and partnership,
- Interdisciplinary, participatory and inclusive perspectives and approaches,
- Institutional strengthening for and increasing compliance with gender equality, and
- Results monitoring.


### 7.1 IUS Programme Approach to Gender Action Planning

Noting that the programme will be implemented in three countries with differing contexts to be considered, the action plan will outline actions that are generally applicable to all three countries and where relevant, actions that are tailored to a specific country will be indicated.


---
**Page 222**
---

7.1.1 Implementing Partners

The activities outlined in the GAP will be headed and supported by various partners. For clarity, the stakeholders who will be involved in the implementation of the GAP are outlined below. In the GAP, each partner is specifically discussed to each activity, also specifying whether they are the lead or support on the activity.

Stakeholders with responsibilities in the GAP:
- Government Agencies (Technical Assistance/TA Execution Team)
- National Executing Entities (the electric utilities in the BMICs).
- Implementing Partners (government agencies such as ministries of energy, utilities regulation bodies, etc.)
- The Accredited Entity (CDB Accredited Entity Team).
- Cross-cutting partners (such as UNDP and others) and
- Other partners (include NGOs that represent marginalised groups).

7.1.2 Policy Environments

The gender assessment (drafted and subsumed) which informs and accompanies this GAP highlights the policy environment in energy, relatively gender-neutral for most countries. Accordingly, a universal approach or broad-brush strategy based on model policy and/or legislation or even approaches to policy reform are not suitable and unlikely to be successful. Any such efforts will need to be tailored to each country’s unique context, which is viable, sustainable and scalable into other projects in the future. Each country has its own unique societal circumstances that determine what reforms are needed as well as how, where and when reforms are suitable, viable and likely to be impactful during the life of the project. Thus, this is an important pre-requisite in defining starting points for policy reform.

There is also fertile ground upon which to identify and achieve win-win opportunities via the policy placement of gender into existing frameworks, including NDCs which outline operational strategies and guidance for gender mainstreaming in order to achieve tangible gender impact and a gender just transition through mitigation investments in RE/EE.

7.2 Gender Action Plan

Given this context, the activities in this GAP are divided into (i) general activities that are foundational to the energy sector and are critical for achieving a gender-responsive approach; and (ii) activities critical for delivering specific gender-responsive actions per outcome/output based on the programme’s Theory of Change. The second set of activities are catalytic in nature, as they will help to scale up the first set of activities into an overall campaign shift in utility-led energy transition and investments that is more people-centred and deliver adaptation and mitigation benefits for all. These activities are also designed with a focus on scope and application that are tailored also to the common as well as differentiated needs of households, public sector service delivery actors as well as businesses.

Table 1 outlines the GAP in detail including indicators and targets, timelines, responsibilities and costs in relation to each activity within the framework of the GAP. It aligns with several key findings and recommendations outlined in the Gender Assessment.

Noting the GCF and CDB’s gender requirements stated earlier, it is critical that much of the gender mainstreaming work needed for this project (totaling USD 1,548,000) occurs as early as possible during the project lifespan (Table 1). This is needed to ensure that the necessary capacity building activities are in place and that the project can effectively integrate these gender requirements while also meeting its own objectives of facilitating access to RE/EE technologies for women. The GCF will cover all costs associated with this activity. The Inception Phase and Year 1 of the project will be when most activities occur. The Inception phase activities accrue a cost of USD 16,000 with activities centred on building capacity and developing systems to ensure that gender is integrated into the project design and implementation in developing later actions.

# Appendix A Stakeholder Engagement Plan

## Background/Introduction: Stakeholder Engagement Process

Stakeholder engagement is aimed at addressing gaps emerging from desk-based research. Noting the limited work done in the region on gender and energy (particularly gender and renewable energy) and the limited online availability of what has already been done, the main goal is to address these gaps by identifying barriers and constraints to accessing stable, reliable, affordable, and efficient energy services for women and other marginalized groups.

This plan builds upon a Baseline Assessment and addresses issues considered in the outlines for the Gender Assessment and the Gender Action Plan previously submitted to the Carbon Fund. The project will focus on technology types and supply, a gender and social inclusion analysis of the four technologies (roof-top solar, solar water heating, LED lighting and cooling), as well as barriers and constraints to accessing stable, reliable, affordable energy services.

While the project will focus on technology types and supply, a gender and social inclusion analysis of the four technologies (roof-top solar, solar water heating, LED lighting and cooling) is also in fashion and what will or may drive these including uptake of proposed interventions. Accordingly, areas to be explored include:

- **Barriers and Constraints to accessing stable, reliable, affordable energy services learned from the Covid19 pandemic**
- **Gender Differences in Knowledge, Exposure, Experience and Capacity particularly related to renewable energy projects (particularly for implementing actors)**
- **Gaps, Barriers and Constraints and Best practices for women's participation and economic empowerment in energy projects**
- **Requirements for an inclusive approach that considers the specific needs of persons with disabilities, the elderly, youth, LGBTQIA+ persons, indigenous people and other marginalized groups**

Given the focus of the project, the baseline findings on gender equality and initial gaps as well as the lessons learned from the Covid19 pandemic, it is important to ensure that the following marginalized groups need to be considered in our engagements in the context of the four technologies – roof-top solar, solar water heating, LED lighting and cooling:

- **Women**
- **Young people**
- **School-aged children for whom alternative and consistent access to energy was a significant issue during the COVID-19 pandemic.**
- **The elderly for whom cooling can be a critical asset given the NCDs prevalent in the region.**
- **Persons with Disability**


---
**Page 234**
---

• LGBTIQ+ persons or representative groups,
• Indigenous People.

Inputs from them directly where possible and from others in terms of how they currently engage with these needs are critical to the quality of analysis as well as the recommendations that may be made for the project. These inputs will also inform the gender mainstreaming focus of the project as well as the Gender Action Plan (GAP).

### Stakeholder Engagement Type

Stakeholder engagement will take three forms, key informant interviews (KII), group interviews (GI) and focused group discussions (FGD). The KIIs are intended to be one-on-one interviews or presentations and will primarily occur online except in cases where it would be more suitable to conduct engagements in the country.

KIs are aimed at primary government agencies and utilities. These interviews are intended to provide the representatives from these organisations with a safe space to speak about the topics from their organisation's perspective without any outside distractions.

Group interviews (GI) will occur for some of the key informant interviews, government and non-governmental organisations (NGO) that work in a closely related field and would benefit from being present when asked questions during the interview or may feel comfortable in a limited group setting (e.g. LGBTIQ+ organisations).

Focused Group Discussions are planned for NGO stakeholders with the idea that discussions with these organisations working in a similar/the same field would create an engaging, safe space to discuss topics of interest and provide feedback on the project when presented with a talking point that they may relate to but have a different experience with.

## Draft Email to Stakeholders

Dear Sir/Madam,

The Caribbean Development Bank (CDB) in collaboration with the governments and utilities of three Borrowing Member Countries (BMCs) is preparing a funding proposal to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) for the implementation of the project titled “Scaling Up Renewable Energy Sector Transformation in The Caribbean” programme. This programme arises out of the need to support countries in their transition from fossil fuels towards more sustainable, renewable energy sources. This first iteration of the programme focuses on Barbados, Belize, and Jamaica. The programme will support these countries to (1) reduce greenhouse gas emissions (GHG) by enhancing the share of distributed renewable energy (RE), energy efficiency (EE) and distributed energy resources (DER); (2) increase access to RE and EE; and (3) enhance gender equality through a wide spread investment in distributed RE, EE and other DERs while promoting equitable access to such technologies for all, including electric utilities to be able to continue applying the US model after the end of the programme.

CDB has contracted CPICS (in collaboration with FOKABS Inc and SAED Consulting Barbados Inc) to lead the implementation of this project. The role of CPICS is to manage the execution of the GCF. SAED Consulting’s role is to bring gender and social inclusion expertise to the process. This includes conducting a gender assessment and developing a gender mainstreaming action plan in compliance with GCF guidelines.

To accurately reflect the target countries’ gender equality and socio-economic context and provide an appropriate gender analysis, SAED Consulting will hold stakeholder consultations between April 10th to April 21st. Stakeholders targeted include key governmental and community stakeholders active in the energy transformation and social development sectors.


The following days are allocated to the three countries for stakeholder engagement (excludes weekends):

| Barbados – 3rd-4th |


---
**Page 236**
---

Belize – 11th to 13th

Jamaica – 19th to 21st

The stakeholder consultations will explore the following:

- Barriers and Constraints to accessing stable, reliable, affordable energy and lessons learnt from the COVID pandemic.
- Gender Mainstreaming Knowledge, Exposure, Experience and Capacity particularly related to women’s empowerment in the energy sector and supporting activity.
- Gaps, Barriers and Constraints and Best practices for women participation and economic empowerment in a just energy transition.
- Representation of Stakeholders that considers the specific needs of persons with disabilities, the elderly, youth, LGBTIQ+ persons, indigenous people and other marginalised groups as well as intersecting vulnerabilities.

Your views are important to us. Please provide your earliest possible response and any information the organisation or the identified representatives may be able to provide that is relevant to these issues!

We encourage confirmation by April 2nd as we begin planning for these engagements To facilitate this process, we encourage you to use our Calendly link to book a suitable time within your own schedule. If you have any questions about the engagement please contact Kwasi Pierre at juniorassociate@saediconsulting.com. Thank you in advance for your kind consideration.

Yours Sincerely,

SAEDI Consulting Barbados Inc

# Appendix C Unconsulted Stakeholders

Figure C-1 presents a list of stakeholders that the consultant attempted to engage as part of the stakeholder engagement process to support the gender assessment. Failure to obtain dates and times to meet or a lack of response from stakeholders were the reasons for the inability to meet with them. The list includes 20 civil society organisations and a few government organisations from Belize (and excludes the utilities, see Figure C-1).
